Analytic Fourier Integral Operators and a Problem from Seismic Inversion
Leonard Busch 1 🖂

Abstract
We establish a general result about the recovery of the analytic wavefront set of a distribution from the analytic wavefront set of its transform coming from a classical elliptic analytic Fourier integral operator (FIO) satisfying some conditions including the Bolker condition. Furthermore, we give a simple explicit analytic parametrix in the form of a classical elliptic analytic FIO for general analytic second order hyperbolic differential operators. Finally, we apply these results together with microlocal analytic continuation and a layer stripping argument to a problem from seismic inversion to prove the injectivity of a linearized operator in the analytic setting. It is the use of wave packet techniques that allows us to give the precise relation how the FIOs under consideration transform the analytic wavefront set, whereas the explicit analytic parametrix comes from a reformulation of the parametrix construction of Hadamard and Hörmander. † † 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification: Primary: 35S30, 35A18, 35A20. Secondary: 35L05, 42B10. † † Key words and phrases: Analytic Fourier integral operator, FBI transform, wave packet decomposition, analytic wavefront set, Bolker condition, inverse problem, acoustic wave equation



1
Introduction

In
[
MST23
]
it was observed that methods to tackle some geometric inverse problems in an analytic setting, in particular generalized X-ray transforms or bicharacteristic ray transforms – such as investigated in
[
BQ87
,
HZ17
,
Ste17
]
– fall under a common framework: that of an analytic double fibration transform. This method generally follows this recipe: given knowledge of the analytic singularities of
Tu
for some distribution
u
and transform
T
, recover the analytic singularities of
u
, use microlocal analytic continuation (Holmgren’s theorem) to control
u
locally and a layer stripping argument to determine
u
(semi-)globally.

The above method is bottlenecked, for example, by the set of transforms
T
for which one may recover the analytic singularities of
u
given those of
Tu
. If the transform
T
is an elliptic analytic pseudodifferential operator (
\Psi
DO) such a result is
[
Tre22
, Thm. 17.3.10]
, and we also refer
[
Hör71b
, Thm. 5.4]
for an analytic elliptic regularity theorem. The result
[
MST23
, Thm. 5.1]
provides a statement about the recoverability of singularities in a general setting where the transform in question,
T
, is an analytic Fourier integral operator (FIO) coming from a double fibration which satisfies the Bolker condition.

The Bolker condition was introduced in
[
Gui85
]
and, in the setting of
smooth
FIOs, guarantees that FIOs
T
coming from a double fibration can be composed with their adjoint
T^{\ast}
using the clean intersection calculus, see also the discussions in
[
GS77
,
GS79
]
. This will lead to the normal operator
T^{\ast}T
being a
\Psi
DO that is microlocally elliptic at certain points, guaranteeing the recoverability of some (smooth) singularities.

Due to a lack of a general compositional calculus for analytic FIOs (see the remarks at the beginning of
Section

2
), avoiding the use of the normal operator, the result established in
[
MST23
]
relies instead on a wave packet decomposition as developed in
[
CF78
]
. We will follow their approach of studying the analytic wavefront set using wave packets, which is essentially an FBI transform, see for example
[
Mar02
,
Zwo12
]
, owing its name to
[
BI75
,
Iag75
]
. We refer also to
[
CNR15
]
that study FIOs using ‘Gabor frames’, essentially wave packets, and
[
Fol89
]
.

Our result generalizes that of
[
MST23
]
in the following key points:

•

geometrically, the FIOs we consider are restricted only by a few conditions on their canonical relation, see
Definition

2.8
.

•

The amplitude of the FIO may depend on the frequency. In fact, we will be working with so-called formal amplitudes as introduced in
[
BK67
]
and widely employed, such as in
[
Sjö82
]
and
[
Tre22
]
.

For example, FIOs originating from the method of geometric optics, such as parametrices of strictly hyperbolic differential operators, are now within reach to be treated by our methods.

As a demonstration of the applicability of our result to analytic microlocal analysis, we consider the problem from
[
Rak88
]
in an analytic setting: recovering the wave speed of the subsurface of the earth from measurements of reflected waves at the surface. We show the injectivity of a linearized operator, which relies on the recipe we described above: exploiting microlocal unique continuation to propagate control of measurements locally and a layer stripping method to show injectivity in some compact domain.

1.1
Outline of Results

In
Section

2
we introduce the notions of phase and amplitude of the FIOs we work with and state elementary results about FIOs defined by these expressions.
Section

3
is dedicated to proving the main results
Theorem

3.1
and
Corollary

3.2
that state how an analytic FIO satisfying conditions stipulated therein transforms the analytic wavefront set of a distribution. Informally, we can summarize the main results of this section as

Theorem

(
Theorems

3.1
and
3.2
)
.

Let
T
be an FIO defined by an elliptic classical pseudoanalytic amplitude and a real-valued analytic non-degenerate phase
\varphi
with canonical relation
\Lambda_{\varphi}
so that
\varphi,\Lambda_{\varphi}
satisfy the Bolker condition and some other mild assumptions. For any
f\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
, we have

\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}(Tf)=\Lambda_{\varphi}\circ\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}(f)\,.

In fact, these results are microlocal in nature, so that they can be applied even when the assumptions, such as ellipticity etc., only hold microlocally.

In
Section

4
we find simple explicit pseudoanalytic FIO representations of the analytic parametrix of strictly hyperbolic differential operators of degree
2
. The main result of this section can be stated informally as

Theorem

(
Theorem

4.1
)
.

Let
\partial_{t}^{2}+P(x,D)
be a strictly hyperbolic second order differential operator with analytic coefficients. There are elliptic classical pseudoanalytic amplitudes
a,\tilde{a}
, so that for every
y
and every
x
in the injectivity radius of
y
, the fundamental solution at
(0,y)
for
\partial_{t}^{2}+P(x,D)
vanishing in negative time is given by

\int{R}e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\tilde{a}(x,y,\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta\mod C^{\omega}=\int{R}e^{i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}a(x,y,\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta\mod C^{\omega}\,,

for
t>0
and
d_{g}
the distance function arising from the metric defined by the principal symbol of
P
, and the formula on the RHS holds only for
x\neq y
.

This construction is employed in
Section

5
to study a problem from seismic inversion and adapting it to the analytic setting developed in the previous sections; the main result of
Section

5
is
Theorem

5.1
, which proves the injectivity of a linearized operator and can roughly be stated as

Theorem

(
Theorem

5.1
)
.

Let
DA[c]
be the linearization about
c\in C^{\omega}
of the operator mapping
c
to
u|_{x_{n}=0}
where
(c^{-2}\partial_{t}^{2}-\Delta_{x})u=\delta_{0}
and
u|_{t<0}=0
. Assume that the metric corresponding to
c
is simple, that there is no scattering over
\pi
and there are no grazing rays. Assume further that all rays entering some compact set of the subsurface
\{x_{n}<0\}
, when ‘reflected’ by flipping the vertical momentum return to the surface
\{x_{n}=0\}
. The linearized operator
DA[c](c_{\delta})
determines the perturbation
c_{\delta}
uniquely in this subset of the subsurface.

2
Analytic FIO

At the beginning of
[
HS18
, § 2]
three approaches to analytic microlocal analysis are described, two of which are relevant here.

The adaptation of the usual smooth microlocal analysis to the analytic setting using special cut-off functions, see
[
Hör71b
]
,
[
Hör03
, § 8.5]
and
[
Tre22
, Part V]
; refer also to
[
Trè80
,
Trè80a
]
. This approach originated from the study of Denjoy-Carleman classes (
[
Bom65
,
Bom64
]
) and most results in this approach can be stated in that generality, see also
[
Für20
]
. For a development of microlocal analysis in the Gevrey setting based on these methods, consult
[
Rod93
,
LR88
]
.

The second method employs so-called FBI transforms, which can be seen as a modification of Fourier transforms that localize near a microlocal point without the use of cut-off functions. FBI transforms come in two flavors: global and microlocal. The classic exposition for microlocal FBI transforms is
[
Sjö82
]
and their use (generally) necessitates working in spaces of germs, see also
[
Sjö80
,
Del92
]
and
[
Tre22
, Part VI]
. In this approach some results known in the smooth setting have analogues: for equivalence of phases see
[
Sjö82
, Thm. 11.17]
and
[
Tre22
, Thm. 22.3.13]
, for transversal compositional calculus
[
RSS20
]
, for composing with an FBI transform with opposite phase function (inversion) see
[
Sjö82
, § 4]
and
[
Tre22
, § 21]
; all of these results are microlocal in nature. The global FBI transform in the euclidean setting can be found in
[
Zwo12
, Chp. 13]
,
[
Mar02
, Chp. 3]
and
[
Hör91
]
, it is related to the Bargmann kernel, see
[
RSS20
]
and the references therein. On manifolds it was developed systematically in
[
Sjö80a
,
Sjö96
]
in the analytic setting, whereas
[
BJ20
]
and
[
WZ01
]
consider the Gevrey (including analytic) and smooth settings respectively.

In this document we will use a combination of methods from the first and second approach. In fact, due to the lack of advanced tools available in the first approach (or the lack of our knowledge thereof) our results are built up from fundamental tools, hopefully being accessible to readers not familiar with analytic microlocal analysis.
We use special cut-offs to reduce the study of an FIO to a microlocal neighborhood, and adopt the FBI transform from
[
Mar02
]
to perform that study. The only way in which we rely on
[
Sjö82
]
is by using its definition of the analytic wavefront set. In fact, however, throughout this article we use multiple definitions of the analytic wavefront set of a distribution, and
[
Bon77
]
has shown that these are all equivalent.

Finally, we mention in passing that some Lagrangians can be parametrized
globally
by a complex phase function, see
[
LSV94
,
CLV22
]
, which we cannot treat here as the phases of the FIOs we consider are real-valued. What keeps us from employing phases with non-negative imaginary part (such as in
[
Hör09
, Def. 25.4.3]
) is that we require real-ness of second order derivatives of a phase on its stationary set, whereas phases with non-negative imaginary part are only guaranteed to have real first order derivatives on their stationary set (see also the remarks near
[
Hör03
, Thm. 8.1.9]
).

In this section we introduce the terminology we use throughout this paper. Throughout this section, let
\Omega_{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N},\Omega_{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N^{\prime}}
denote open sets where
N,N^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
.

2.1
Amplitudes and Phases

We will gain inspiration from
[
Tre22
, Def. 18.7.1]
to define our notion of amplitudes and symbols:

Definition 2.1
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{R}
and
a(z,x,\eta)=a\in C^{\infty}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\})
so that there exist complex open neighborhoods
\Omega_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}\supset\Omega_{k},k=1,2
so that
a
can be extended to a function we also denote by
a
on
\Omega_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}\times\Omega_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
that is holomorphic with respect to
(z,x)
and to every compact
K\subset\Omega_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}\times\Omega_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
there exist
M_{a},C_{a},R_{a}>0
so that

\displaystyle\sup_{K\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}}\left(1+\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\right)^{-m+\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert}\left\lvert\partial^{\gamma}_{\eta}a(z,x,\eta)\right\rvert

\displaystyle<\infty\,,

\displaystyle\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}\,,\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq R_{a}\mathrm{max}\left\{1,\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert\right\}\implies\left\lvert\partial^{\gamma}_{\eta}a(z,x,\eta)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq M_{a}C_{a}^{\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert}\gamma!\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{m-\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert}\,.

(2.1)

We say that
a
is a
pseudoanalytic amplitude of order
m
, and denote the set of these by
\mathrm{S}^{m}_{\psi a}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2})
, and put
S_{\psi a}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2})\coloneqq\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{R}}S^{m}_{\psi a}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2})
.

Let us also introduce formal series of amplitudes of which the constants in
Eq.

2.1
share a common growth rate, as considered in
[
BK67
]
,
[
Sjö82
]
; we follow
[
Tre22
, Def. 17.2.3]
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{R}
and
a_{j},j=0,1,\dots
be a sequence of pseudoanalytic amplitudes
a_{j}\in S^{m-j}_{\psi a}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2})
so that there exist complex open neighborhoods
\Omega_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}\supset\Omega_{k},k=1,2
, independent of
j
, so that
a_{j},j\geq 0
can be extended to a function on
\Omega_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}\times\Omega_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
that is holomorphic with respect to
(z,x)
and to every compact
K\subset\Omega_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}\times\Omega_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
there exist
M_{a},C_{a},R_{a}>0
so that

\displaystyle\sup_{K\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}}\left(1+\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\right)^{-m+j+\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert}\left\lvert\partial^{\gamma}_{\eta}a_{j}(z,x,\eta)\right\rvert

\displaystyle<\infty\,,

\displaystyle\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}\,,\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq R_{a}\mathrm{max}\left\{1,\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert+j\right\}\implies\left\lvert\partial^{\gamma}_{\eta}a_{j}(z,x,\eta)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq M_{a}C_{a}^{\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert+j}\gamma!j!\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{m-j-\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert}\,.

(2.2)

We call the formal series
\underline{a}=\sum_{j\geq 0}a_{j}
a
formal pseudoanalytic amplitude of order
m\in\mathbb{R}
, and denote the set of these by
\mathrm{FS}^{m}_{\psi a}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2})
.

A particular type of formal pseudoanalytic amplitude will be of interest to us.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{R}
and
\underline{a}=\sum_{j\geq 0}a_{j}\in\mathrm{FS}^{m}_{\psi a}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2})
be a formal pseudoanalytic amplitude and
(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\in\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
fixed. We call
\underline{a}
a
classical pseudoanalytic amplitude at

(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})
if there is some
\lambda_{0}\geq 1
so that

a_{j}(z,x,\lambda\eta)=(\lambda/\lambda_{0})^{m-j}a_{j}(z,x,\lambda_{0}\eta)\quad\text{for all }j\in\mathbb{N}\ \ \text{and all }\lambda\geq\lambda_{0}

(2.3)

for all
(z,x,\eta)
in some open neighborhood of
(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})
.

We say that
\underline{a}
is
elliptic
at
(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})
, if there is
\lambda_{0}\geq 1
so that

a_{0}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\lambda\hat{\eta})\neq 0\,,\quad\text{for all }\lambda\geq\lambda_{0}\,.

(2.4)

We say
\underline{a}
is
classical in
(resp.
elliptic in
) some open set
U\subset\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
if there is
\lambda_{0}\geq 1
so that
Eq.

2.3
(resp.
Eq.

2.4
) holds on
U
. Finally, we call
\underline{a}

classical
(resp.
elliptic
) if there is
\varepsilon>0
so that
\underline{a}
is classical in (resp. elliptic in)
\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}\times\{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert>\varepsilon\}
.

Essentially,
Eq.

2.3
means that each
a_{j}
is homogeneous in
\eta
of degree
m-j
away from the origin.

We briefly explain how pseudoanalytic amplitudes that are homogeneous for large
\eta
are actually real-analytic for large
\eta
(see also
[
Tre22
, Lem. 17.2.25]
).

Remark 2.4
.

Let
a\in\mathrm{S}^{m}_{\psi a}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2})
with notation from
Eq.

2.1
. Assume that for
\lambda\geq 1
, some
R>0
and
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert>R>0
and all
(z,x)\in\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}
we have

a(z,x,\lambda\eta)=\lambda^{m}a(z,x,\eta)\,.

In this case we see
a(z,x,\eta)=\lambda^{-m}a(z,x,\lambda\eta),\lambda\geq 1
so that for any
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert>R
we have, choosing
\lambda\geq 1
so that
\lambda\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq R_{a}\max\{1,\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert\}
,

\left\lvert\partial^{\gamma}_{\eta}a(z,x,\eta)\right\rvert=\lambda^{-m}\left\lvert\partial^{\gamma}_{\eta}(a(z,x,\lambda\eta))\right\rvert\leq M_{a}C_{a}^{\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert}\gamma!\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{m-\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert}\,.

Thus, for any fixed
\eta
with
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert>R
, any compact
K\subset\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}\times\{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert>R\}
we have

\sup_{K}\left\lvert\partial^{\alpha}_{z}\partial^{\beta}_{x}\partial^{\gamma}_{\eta}a(z,x,\eta)\right\rvert\leq M_{a}C_{a}^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert+\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert}\alpha!\beta!\gamma!\sup_{\eta\in\pi_{\eta}K}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{m-\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert}\leq C_{K}M_{a}C_{a}^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert+\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert}\alpha!\beta!\gamma!

for some
C_{K}
depending on
K
.

From this estimate we see that
a
is real-analytic at every
(z,x,\eta)\in\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}\times\{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert>R\}
.

Finally, we introduce the notion of a finite realization of a formal pseudoanalytic amplitude. This is the analytic counterpart of the idea of asymptotic summability of smooth amplitudes as given in
[
Hör71
, Prop. 1.1.9]
. We draw inspiration also from
[
BJ20
]
.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
\mathrm{FS}^{m}_{\psi a}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2})\ni\underline{a}=\sum_{j\geq 0}a_{j}
. We say that
a\in\mathrm{S}^{m}_{\psi a}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2})
is a
finite realization
of
\underline{a}
if for every large enough
R>0
there exists
C>0
so that for all
(z,x,\eta)\in\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus\{0\}
,

\left\lvert a(z,x,\eta)-\sum_{j=0}^{R^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}a_{j}(z,x,\eta)\right\rvert\leq Ce^{-C^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\,.

Formal pseudoanalytic amplitudes always admit finite realizations. Using
[
Hör65
]
, this can be shown by inverting the
\bar{\partial}
operator, such as in
[
BJ20
]
and
[
Sjö82
]
, see also
[
Sjö96a
, Appx.]
. We use a constructive approach that is a consequence of the proof of
[
Tre22
, Prop. 17.2.5]
.

As they will be required in the following proof, we introduce specially crafted cut-off functions that become necessary when working in the analytic category, see
[
Hör71a
]
,
[
Hör03
, § 8.4]
or
[
Tre22
]
. These special cut-offs are sometimes called Ehrenpreis cut-offs after
[
Ehr60
]
, similar constructions being considered in
[
Man42
]
.

We introduce the notion of a sequence of Ehrenpreis cut-offs following
[
Tre22
, Def. 3.2.2]
: For any
N^{\ast}\in\mathbb{N}
and open
\mho,\mho^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N^{\ast}}
with
\mathrm{dist}(\mho^{\prime},\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mho)>0
(in particular
\mho^{\prime}\subset\mho
) we say a sequence
(\chi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a sequence of
Ehrenpreis cut-offs relative to
(\mho,\mho^{\prime})
if the following holds:

\chi_{j}|_{\mho^{\prime}}\equiv 1\,,\quad\mathrm{supp}\chi_{j}\Subset\mho\,,\quad 0\leq\chi_{j}\leq 1\,,\quad\exists C>0\colon\forall j\in\mathbb{N}\forall\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert\leq j\colon\left\lvert\partial^{\alpha}\chi_{j}\right\rvert\leq(Cj)^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}\,.

(2.5)

In addition, we assume that for every
\alpha
there is
C_{\alpha}>0
(independent of
j
) so that on
\mho
,
\left\lvert\partial^{\alpha}\chi_{j}\right\rvert\leq C_{\alpha}
. This will ensure that the sequence
(\chi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded in
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N^{\ast}})
(see the exercises in
[
Trè06
, Chpt. 14]
).

The existence of such cut-offs is shown, for example, in
[
Tre22
, Prop. 3.2.1]
(the assumption of ours that ensures boundedness of
(\chi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
in
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N^{\ast}})
is a consequence of the construction in that proof, see also
[
Hör71b
, Lem. 2.2]
).

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\mathrm{FS}^{m}_{\psi a}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2})\ni\underline{a}=\sum_{j\geq 0}a_{j}
. For any
\rho>2R_{a}
large enough (
R_{a}
from
Eq.

2.2
) there exists
a\in\mathrm{S}^{m}_{\psi a}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2})
that is a finite realization of
\underline{a}
and is equal to
a_{0}
for
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert<\rho
. Furthermore, every other finite realization
\tilde{a}
of
\underline{a}
differs from
a
by a term
\mathcal{O}(e^{-C\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert})
.

Proof.

Let
\rho>2R_{a}
for
R_{a}
from
Eq.

2.2
be large enough, and let
\varphi_{j}
be a sequence of Ehrenpreis cut-offs relative to the sets
\{\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\colon\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq 1\},\{\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\colon\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq 2\}
, see
Eq.

2.5
. In particular,
\varphi_{j}(\eta/(j\rho))=0
for
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\leq j\rho
and
\varphi_{j}(\eta/(j\rho))=1
for
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq 2j\rho
.

Define

a(z,x,\eta)\coloneqq a_{0}(z,x,\eta)+\sum_{j\geq 1}\varphi_{j}\left(\frac{\eta}{j\rho}\right)a_{j}(z,x,\eta)\,.

By the proof of part
I.
of
[
Tre22
, Prop. 17.2.5]
, we have that
a\in\mathrm{S}^{m}_{\psi a}
. The result
[
Tre22
, Prop. 17.2.5]
is stated where the dimensions satisfy
N=n
. In our case these may differ, so that this is not a direct consequence of the cited proposition, but the proof works exactly the same way.

Notice that by the support properties of
\varphi_{j}
, for
R>\rho
,

a-\sum_{j=0}^{R^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}a_{j}(z,x,\eta)=\sum_{R^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert<j<\rho^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\varphi_{j}\left(\frac{\eta}{j\rho}\right)a_{j}(z,x,\eta)+\sum_{\frac{1}{2}\rho^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert<j<R^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\left(\varphi_{j}\left(\frac{\eta}{j\rho}\right)-1\right)a_{j}(z,x,\eta)\,.

Since
\rho\geq 2R_{a}
, and
0\leq\varphi_{j}\leq 1
, the summation indices,
Eq.

2.2
, and Stirling’s estimate allow us to bound

\left\lvert a-\sum_{j=0}^{R^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}a_{j}(z,x,\eta)\right\rvert\leq M\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{m}\sum_{\frac{1}{2}\rho^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert<j<\rho^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\sqrt{2\pi j}\left(\frac{C_{a}j}{e\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\right)^{j}\,.

We may assume that
\rho
is large enough so that
C_{a}/\rho<1
and by the index bounds on
j
, we get

\sum_{\frac{1}{2}\rho^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert<j<\rho^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\sqrt{2\pi j}\left(\frac{C_{a}j}{e\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\right)^{j}\leq\sum_{\frac{1}{2}\rho^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert<j<\rho^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\sqrt{2\pi j}e^{-j}\leq\sqrt{2\pi}(\rho^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert)^{3/2}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\rho^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\leq Ce^{-C^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}

for some
C>0
.

To show the uniqueness up to exponential terms, notice how for a different finite realization
\tilde{a}
of
\underline{a}
, choosing
R>0
large enough for both finite realizations simultaneously,

\left\lvert a-\tilde{a}\right\rvert=\left\lvert a-\sum_{j=0}^{R^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}a_{j}-\left(\tilde{a}-\sum_{j=0}^{R^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}a_{j}\right)\right\rvert\leq 2Ce^{-C^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\,.

∎

Finally, we introduce our notion of (non-degenerate) analytic phase, which only differs from the smooth approach by assuming the phase is analytic. In particular, the notion of non-degeneracy is just that of
[
Hör71
]
.

Definition 2.7
.

We call a real-valued function
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\})
an
analytic phase
if
\varphi
can be extended holomorphically to
\Omega_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}\times\Omega_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}\times\Theta^{\mathbb{C}}_{\varphi}
where
\Omega_{j}^{\mathbb{C}}
are open complex neighborhoods of
\Omega_{j}
,
j\in\{1,2\}
and
\Theta^{\mathbb{C}}_{\varphi}
is a conic neighborhood of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
in
\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus i\mathbb{R}^{n}
, so that

\varphi(z,x,\sigma\eta)=\sigma\varphi(z,x,\eta)\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\nabla_{(z,x,\eta)}\varphi(z,x,\eta)\neq 0\quad\text{for all }(z,x,\eta)\in\Omega_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}\times\Omega_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}\times\Theta^{\mathbb{C}}_{\varphi},\sigma>0\,.

We call an analytic phase
non-degenerate
if
\varphi_{\eta}(z,x,\eta)=0
implies that the elements of

\{\nabla_{(z,x,\eta)}\varphi_{\eta_{j}}(z,x,\eta)\}_{j=1}^{n}

are linearly independent.

Finally, we say that the set

\Lambda_{\varphi}\coloneqq\left\{(z,\varphi_{z}(z,x,\eta);x,-\varphi_{x}(z,x,\eta))\colon\varphi_{\eta}(z,x,\eta)=0,(z,x,\eta)\in\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}\right\}

is the
canonical relation corresponding to
\varphi
.

For (geometric) properties of the canonical relation refer to
[
Dui11
,
Hör71
]
. For example, according to
[
Dui11
, Lem. 2.3.2]
, when
\varphi
is non-degenerate its canonical relation is a conic manifold.

2.2
Salutary FIO

Definition 2.8
.

Let
\Lambda
be a canonical relation in
T^{\ast}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2})\setminus\{0\}
.

We say that
\Lambda
satisfies the
Bolker condition
at
(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta},\hat{x},\hat{\xi})\in\Lambda
if

\pi_{L}\colon\Lambda\to T^{\ast}\Omega_{1}\,,\quad(z,\zeta,x,\xi)\mapsto(z,\zeta)

satisfies

d\pi_{L}|_{(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta},\hat{x},\hat{\xi})}\quad\text{is injective}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\pi_{L}^{-1}(\{(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta})\})=\{(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta},\hat{x},\hat{\xi})\}\,.

We say that a canonical relation
\Lambda
in
T^{\ast}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2})\setminus\{0\}
is
salutary at

(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta},\hat{x},\hat{\xi})\in\Lambda
if

\mathrm{ph}
1.

\Lambda
satisfies the Bolker condition at
(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta},\hat{x},\hat{\xi})
.

\mathrm{ph}
2.

In an open neighborhood of
(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta},\hat{x},\hat{\xi})
, the map

\Lambda\ni(z,\zeta;x,\xi)\mapsto(z,x,\xi)

is an immersion.

\mathrm{ph}
3.

In an open neighborhood of
(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta},\hat{x},\hat{\xi})
, we have

\Lambda\ni(z,\zeta;x,\xi)\implies\zeta\neq 0\,.

We will have to introduce one more condition on a phase that gives rise to a canonical relation.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
\varphi
be a non-degenerate analytic phase according to
Definition

2.7
and let
\Lambda_{\varphi}
be the canonical relation corresponding to
\varphi
.

Letting
\Sigma_{\varphi}\coloneqq\{(z,x,\eta)\colon\varphi_{\eta}(z,x,\eta)=0\}
be the
stationary set
and

G_{\varphi}\colon\Sigma_{\varphi}\to\Lambda_{\varphi}\,,\quad(z,x,\eta)\mapsto(z,\varphi_{z}(z,x,\eta),x,-\varphi_{x}(z,x,\eta))\,,

we say that
\varphi
satisfies the
unique frequency condition
at
(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta},\hat{x},\hat{\xi})\in\Lambda_{\varphi}
for
\hat{\eta}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
if

G_{\varphi}^{-1}\left(\{(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta},\hat{x},\hat{\xi})\}\right)=\{(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\}\,.

Finally, we say that
\varphi
is
salutary at some

(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta},\hat{x},\hat{\xi})\in\Lambda_{\varphi}
if the canonical relation
\Lambda_{\varphi}
is salutary according to
Definition

2.8
and

\mathrm{ph}
4.

\varphi
satisfies the unique frequency condition at
(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta},\hat{x},\hat{\xi})
.

Remark 2.10
.

Let us consider the special case where
\Lambda
is a canonical relation coming from a double fibration, which is to say that
\Lambda=(N^{\ast}W\setminus\{0\})^{\prime}
where
W\subset\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}
is a double fibration, see for example
[
MST23
, Def. 1.5]
. The condition

ph

2.
is an immediate consequence of the fact that
\Lambda
comes from a double fibration and

ph

3.
follows from
[
MST23
, Lem. 5.3]
(which in fact also guarantees
Eq.

3.2
below). Furthermore, choosing to represent an FIO associated to this canonical relation locally with the phase chosen in
[
MST23
, § 5.1]
, the condition

ph

4.
is fulfilled too. In conclusion, this set-up of salutary canonical relations and phases generalizes that of double fibration transforms as considered in
[
MST23
]
.

We state a general consequence of a few of these assumptions.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
\Lambda
be a canonical relation associated to some non-degenerate analytic phase
\varphi
near some
(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta},\hat{x},\hat{\xi})\in\Lambda
, and let
(\hat{\zeta},\hat{\xi})=(\varphi_{z},-\varphi_{x})(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})
for some
(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\in\Sigma_{\varphi}
.

Let the Bolker condition

ph

1.
be satisfied at
(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta};\hat{x},\hat{\xi})
. We have
\mathrm{d}\pi_{L}|_{(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta},\hat{x},\hat{\xi})}
is injective if and only if for all curves
(z,x,\eta)(t)
on
\Sigma_{\varphi}
with
(z,x,\eta)(0)=(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})
we have

(\dot{z}(0),\partial_{x}\varphi_{z}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\dot{x}(0)+\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{z}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\dot{\eta}(0))=0\implies(\dot{x}(0),\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{x}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\dot{\eta}(0))=0\,.

(2.6)

Furthermore, if
\Lambda
satisfies assumption

ph

2.
,
the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{x}\varphi_{z}&\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{z}\\
\partial_{x}\varphi_{\eta}&\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{\eta}\end{pmatrix}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\colon\mathbb{R}^{N^{\prime}+n}\to\mathbb{R}^{N+n}

(2.7)

is injective.

Proof.

Letting
(z(t),x(t),\eta(t))
be a curve on
\Sigma_{\varphi}
with initial condition
(z(0),x(0),\eta(0))=(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})
,
d\pi_{L}
being injective means that

\displaystyle{}(\dot{z}(0),\partial_{z}\varphi_{z}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\dot{z}(0)+\partial_{x}\varphi_{z}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\dot{x}(0)+\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{z}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\dot{\eta}(0))=0

\displaystyle\qquad\implies(\dot{x}(0),\partial_{z}\varphi_{x}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\dot{z}(0)+\partial_{x}\varphi_{x}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\dot{x}(0)+\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{x}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\dot{\eta}(0))=0\,,

which is equivalent to

(\dot{z}(0),\partial_{x}\varphi_{z}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\dot{x}(0)+\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{z}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\dot{\eta}(0))=0\implies(\dot{x}(0),\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{x}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\dot{\eta}(0))=0\,,

which concludes the proof of
Eq.

2.6
.

We turn to proving the second part. Notice first that by the non-degeneracy of
\varphi
,

\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{z}\\
\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{x}\\
\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{\eta}\end{pmatrix}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\cdot\dot{\eta}(0)=0\implies\dot{\eta}(0)=0\,.

Second, since
(z,x,\eta)(t)
is a curve on
\Sigma_{\varphi}
, we have

0=\partial_{t}(\varphi_{\eta}((z,x,\eta)(t)))=\partial_{z}\varphi_{\eta}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\dot{z}(0)+\partial_{x}\varphi_{\eta}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\dot{x}(0)+\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{\eta}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\dot{\eta}(0)\,,

so that

\dot{z}(0)=0\,,\dot{x}(0)=0\implies\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{\eta}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\dot{\eta}(0)=0\,.

Therefore,
\varphi
being non-degenerate implies

\dot{z}(0)=0\,,\dot{x}(0)=0\,,\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{z}\\
\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{x}\end{pmatrix}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\dot{\eta}(0)=0\implies\dot{\eta}(0)=0\,.

(2.8)

Now by assumption

ph

2.
we have

\dot{z}(0)=0,\dot{x}(0)=0,\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{x}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\dot{\eta}(0)=0\implies\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{z}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\dot{\eta}(0)=0\,,

which combined with
Eq.

2.8
gives

\dot{z}(0)=0,\dot{x}(0)=0,\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{x}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\dot{\eta}(0)=0\implies\dot{\eta}(0)=0\,.

Thus, using
Eq.

2.6
, we get

(\dot{z}(0),\partial_{x}\varphi_{z}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\dot{x}(0)+\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{z}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\dot{\eta}(0))=0\implies(\dot{x}(0),\dot{\eta}(0))=0\,,

so that the matrix
(\partial_{x}\varphi_{z},\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{z})(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})
is injective on the vector space

W\coloneqq\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\dot{x}(0)\\
\dot{\eta}(0)\end{pmatrix}\colon(z,x,\eta)(t)\text{ curve on }\Sigma_{\varphi}\text{ with }\dot{z}(0)=0,(z,x,\eta)(0)=(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\right\}\,.

Rewriting
W
, we know that

W=T_{(\hat{x},\hat{\eta})}\left(\left\{(x,\eta)\colon(\hat{z},x,\eta)\in\Sigma_{\varphi}\right\}\right)=\mathrm{ker}((\partial_{x}\varphi_{\eta},\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{\eta})(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta}))\,.

Locally, of course,
\mathbb{R}^{N^{\prime}+n}=W\oplus W^{\perp}
, and we note that if
w_{1},w_{2}\in W^{\perp}
with
(\partial_{x}\varphi_{\eta},\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{\eta})(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})w_{1}=(\partial_{x}\varphi_{\eta},\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{\eta})(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})w_{2}
, we have
w_{1}-w_{2}\in W^{\perp}\cap W
so that
w_{1}=w_{2}
. This is to say that the matrix

(\partial_{x}\varphi_{\eta},\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{\eta})(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\colon W^{\perp}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}

(2.9)

is injective.

Now write any element in
\mathbb{R}^{N^{\prime}+n}
as
w+w^{\perp}
with
w\in W,w^{\perp}\in W^{\perp}
. Then

\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{x}\varphi_{z}&\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{z}\\
\partial_{x}\varphi_{\eta}&\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{\eta}\end{pmatrix}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})(w+w^{\perp})=\begin{pmatrix}(\partial_{x}\varphi_{z},\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{z})(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})(w+w^{\perp})\\
(\partial_{x}\varphi_{\eta},\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{\eta})(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})w^{\perp}\end{pmatrix}

where the RHS can only be zero if
w^{\perp}=0
by
Eq.

2.9
, and then
w=0
because
(\partial_{x}\varphi_{z},\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{z})(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})
is injective on
W
. This concludes the proof of
Eq.

2.7
.
∎

2.3
A Partial Integration Operator

One of the methods by which we estimate the analytic wavefront set of an FIO is by using repeated partial integrations, sometimes called the method of non-stationary phase. This differs from its smooth analogue only in that we must keep careful control of the constants bounding the amplitude. This is done already in
[
Hör03
, Thm. 7.7.1]
, which, however, cannot be adapted to our needs in some situations. We have also become aware of
[
LR88
, § 1.5]
, but let us nevertheless repeat the setup in this section briefly.

Throughout this section let
\Omega_{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N},\Omega_{3}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be bounded and
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open and away from the origin and
\kappa=\kappa(z,\eta)\in C^{\omega}(\Omega_{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\};\mathbb{C})
be a function.

Defining
\rho_{\kappa}\coloneqq\left\lvert\kappa_{z}\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\kappa_{\eta}\right\rvert^{2}
, for
\kappa
we consider two possible additional properties.

\kappa
1.

There are some
c,C>0
so that for
\eta\in\Gamma

c\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2}\leq\rho_{\kappa}\leq C\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\left\lvert\partial^{\alpha}_{z}\partial^{\beta}_{\eta}\kappa\right\rvert\leq C^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert+1}\alpha!\beta!\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{1-\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}\,.

(2.10)

\kappa
2.

We have
\inf_{(z,\eta)\in\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{3}}\left\lvert\nabla_{z}\kappa\right\rvert>0
.

Notice that if assumptions

κ

1.
and

κ

2.
are true on some sets
\Gamma
and
\Omega_{3}
respectively, then there we have (respectively)

-i\lambda^{-1}\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{z}\cdot\nabla_{z}+\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2}\bar{\kappa}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\eta}}{\rho_{\kappa}}e^{i\lambda\kappa}=e^{i\lambda\kappa}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad-i\lambda^{-1}\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{z}\cdot\nabla_{z}}{\left\lvert\kappa_{z}\right\rvert^{2}}e^{i\lambda\kappa}=e^{i\lambda\kappa}\,.

We will want to use the adjoints of the above for partial integration. Thus, let us define

\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}\colon p\mapsto\left(\frac{\Delta_{z}\bar{\kappa}+\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2}\Delta_{\eta}\bar{\kappa}+2\eta\cdot\nabla_{\eta}\bar{\kappa}}{\rho_{\kappa}}-\frac{\nabla_{z}\rho_{\kappa}\cdot\nabla_{z}\bar{\kappa}+\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2}\nabla_{\eta}\rho_{\kappa}\cdot\nabla_{\eta}\bar{\kappa}}{\rho_{\kappa}^{2}}\right)p+\frac{\nabla_{z}\bar{\kappa}\cdot\nabla_{z}p+\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2}\nabla_{\eta}\bar{\kappa}\cdot\nabla_{\eta}p}{\rho_{\kappa}}\,,

(2.11)

and

\mathcal{L}_{\kappa,2}\colon p\mapsto\left(\frac{\Delta_{z}\bar{\kappa}}{\left\lvert\nabla_{z}\kappa\right\rvert^{2}}-\frac{\nabla_{z}\left\lvert\nabla_{z}\kappa\right\rvert^{2}\cdot\nabla_{z}\bar{\kappa}}{\left\lvert\nabla_{z}\kappa\right\rvert^{4}}\right)p+\frac{\nabla_{z}\bar{\kappa}\cdot\nabla_{z}p}{\left\lvert\nabla_{z}\kappa\right\rvert^{2}}\,,

(2.12)

where
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa},\mathcal{L}_{\kappa,2}
act on any suitable function space, for example
C^{\infty}(\Omega_{1}\times\Gamma)
or
C^{\infty}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{3})
.

We now state that under the assumption

κ

1.
, the coefficients of the differential operator
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}
behave well. The proof of the this statement can be found in
Section

B
.

Lemma 2.12
.

Assume
\kappa
satisfies assumption

κ

1.
. For some
C>0
, depending on the distance from
\Gamma
to the origin, for all
\alpha,\beta
, uniformly in
(z,\eta)\in\Omega_{1}\times\Gamma
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\partial^{\alpha}_{z}\partial^{\beta}_{\eta}\left(\frac{\Delta_{z}\bar{\kappa}+\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2}\Delta_{\eta}\bar{\kappa}+2\eta\cdot\nabla_{\eta}\bar{\kappa}}{\rho_{\kappa}}-\frac{\nabla_{z}\rho_{\kappa}\cdot\nabla_{z}\bar{\kappa}+\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2}\nabla_{\eta}\rho_{\kappa}\cdot\nabla_{\eta}\bar{\kappa}}{\rho_{\kappa}^{2}}\right)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert+1}\alpha!\beta!\,.

(2.13)

Having concluded the setup of the estimates about the coefficients of the differential operators
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa,2}
, we state

Proposition 2.13
.

Let
a\in\mathrm{S}^{m}_{\psi a}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2})
be a pseudoanalytic amplitude and let
\kappa
satisfy condition

κ

1.
for some
\Gamma
, and let
\inf_{\eta\in\Gamma}\left\{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\right\}\eqqcolon r_{\Gamma}>0
,
r_{\Gamma}^{\prime}=\min\{1,r_{\Gamma}\}
, and let
\Omega_{3}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be bounded. Use the notation
R_{a},C_{a},M_{a}
from
Eq.

2.1
for
a
. There is a constant
C_{\mathcal{L}}
independent of
a
so that the following holds.

Let
(\psi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset C^{\infty}(\Omega_{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
be a sequence of Ehrenpreis cut-offs, see
Eq.

2.5
. For any
L\in\mathbb{N}
, and any
\lambda\geq 1
, we have that

\displaystyle\eta\in\Gamma,\lambda\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq LR_{a}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}^{L}(\psi_{L}(z,\eta)a(z,x,\lambda\eta))\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{m}M_{a}(C_{a}r_{\Gamma}^{\prime}C_{\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}})^{L}L^{L}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{m}\,,

(2.14)

and if
\kappa
satisfies assumption

κ

2.
on
\Omega_{3}

\displaystyle\eta\in\Omega_{3}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{L}_{\kappa,2}^{L}(\psi_{L}(z,\eta)a(z,x,\lambda\eta))\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{m}M_{a}(C_{a}C_{\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}})^{L}L^{L}\,.

(2.15)

Furthermore, for every real-analytic complex valued
b\in C^{\omega}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{3};\mathbb{C})
there are
M_{b},C_{b}>0
independent of
L
so that if
\kappa
satisfies
\inf_{(z,\eta)\in\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{3}}\left\lvert\rho_{\kappa}\right\rvert>0
then

\displaystyle\eta\in\Omega_{3}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}^{L}(\psi_{L}(z,\eta)b(z,\eta))\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq M_{b}(C_{b}C_{\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}})^{L}L^{L}\,.

(2.16)

Proof.

We first prove
Eq.

2.14
.
By definition of pseudodifferential amplitudes, see
Eq.

2.1
,

\eta\in\Gamma,\lambda\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq R_{a}\max\{\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert,1\}\implies\left\lvert\partial^{\alpha}_{z}\partial^{\beta}_{\eta}a(z,x,\lambda\eta)\right\rvert\leq\lambda^{m}M_{a}C_{a}^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}\alpha!\beta!\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{m-\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}\,.

Notice that if
\psi_{L}
are Ehrenpreis cut-offs satisfying
\left\lvert\partial^{\alpha}_{z}\partial^{\beta}_{\eta}\psi_{L}\right\rvert\leq(C_{\psi}L)^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}
for
\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert\leq L
, then

\displaystyle{}\eta\in\Gamma,\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert\leq L\,,\lambda\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq R_{a}\max\{\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert,1\}\implies

\displaystyle{}\left\lvert\partial^{\alpha}_{z}\partial^{\beta}_{\eta}(\psi_{L}(z,\eta)a(z,x,\lambda\eta))\right\rvert\leq M_{a}\lambda^{m}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{m}(r_{\Gamma}^{\prime})^{-\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}\sum_{(\gamma,\mu)\leq(\alpha,\beta)}\frac{\alpha!\beta!}{\gamma!\mu!}(C_{\psi}L)^{\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert+\left\lvert\mu\right\rvert}C_{a}^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert-\left\lvert\mu\right\rvert+\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert-\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert}\,,

where we bounded
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{-\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert+\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert}\leq r_{\Gamma}^{-\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert+\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert}\leq(r_{\Gamma}^{\prime})^{-\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}
. Now an application of
Lemma

A.2
(assuming without loss that
C_{a}\geq 4C_{\psi}
) shows that (bounding
1\leq(r_{\Gamma}^{\prime})^{-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}
),

\eta\in\Gamma,\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert\leq L\,,\lambda\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq R_{a}\max\{\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert,1\}\implies\left\lvert\partial^{\alpha}_{z}\partial^{\beta}_{\eta}(\psi_{L}(z,\eta)a(z,x,\lambda\eta))\right\rvert\leq M_{a}\lambda^{m}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{m}2^{N+n}\left(\frac{C_{a}L}{r_{\Gamma}^{\prime}}\right)^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}\,.

An application of
Corollary

A.4
using
Lemma

2.12
concludes the proof of
Eq.

2.14
.

The same argument works on
\Omega_{3}
for
\kappa
satisfying assumption

κ

2.
to find
Eq.

2.15
. This relies on the fact that
\Omega_{3}
is a bounded set and
\kappa
is analytic, we omit the repetition.

The result
Eq.

2.16
can be seen as a consequence of
Eq.

2.15
. If we let
\tilde{z}=(z,\eta)
be a new variable, then the analytic function
b=b(\tilde{z})
can be seen as a pseudoanalytic amplitude with no frequency variable. Now the assumptions on
\kappa
correspond to assumption

κ

2.
in the variable
\tilde{z}
, and thus we can conclude
Eq.

2.16
.
∎

We remark that the above result can also be deduced as a consequence of
[
Hör03
, Lem. 8.6.3]
or
[
LR88
, Prop. 1.5.4]
, see also
[
Gra87
, Lem. 3.1]
.

2.4
Analytic Wavefront Set of Oscillatory Integral Distributions

We begin this section with a result concerning the analytic wavefront set, based on its characterization from
[
Hör71b
]
, see also
[
Tre22
, Thm. 3.5.13]
.

Proposition 2.14
.

For a distribution
u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
we have that
(x_{0},\xi_{0})\not\in\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}{u}
if there exists a sequence of Ehrenpreis cutoffs
(\psi_{L})_{L\in\mathbb{N}}
identically
1
near
x_{0}
, constants
C,r>0
and a number
k\in\mathbb{N}
so that for all
L\in\mathbb{N}
and all
t\geq rL
,

\left\lvert\mathcal{F}(\psi_{L}u)(t\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^{-1}\xi)\right\rvert\leq t^{k}C(CL/t)^{L}\,,

(2.17)

for all
\xi
in a conic neighborhood of
\xi_{0}
.

Proof.

By replacing
(\psi_{L})_{L\in\mathbb{N}}
with
(\psi_{L+k})_{L\in\mathbb{N}}
and using
Lemma

A.1
, we may assume that
k=0
, see also the remark after
[
Hör71b
, Prop. 2.4]
.

According to
[
Hör03
, Def. 8.4.3]
the proof will be complete if we show

\left\lvert\mathcal{F}(\psi_{L}u)(t\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^{-1}\xi)\right\rvert\leq C(CL/t)^{L}

for
t\leq rL
, since the above already holds for
t>rL
by
Eq.

2.17
.

Note that
\psi_{L}u
is a bounded sequence in
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
, so that by the Payley-Wiener-Schwarz theorem and the uniform boundedness principle, for some
M,C>0
independent of
L
we have for all
t>0

\left\lvert\mathcal{F}(\psi_{L}u)(t\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^{-1}\xi)\right\rvert\leq C(1+t)^{M}\,,

see also the proof of
[
Hör03
, Prop. 8.4.2]
. For
t\leq rL
we then find

\left\lvert\mathcal{F}(\psi_{L}u)(t\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^{-1}\xi)\right\rvert\leq C(1+t)^{M}(rL/t)^{L}\,,

and replacing
(\psi_{L})_{L\in\mathbb{N}}
with
(\psi_{L+M^{\prime}})_{L\in\mathbb{N}}
for some
M^{\prime}\geq M
,
M^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
completes the proof.
∎

Following the proof of
[
Hör03
, Lem 8.4.4]
one can turn
Proposition

2.14
into an if and only if statement. We omit these details as here we will only use the sufficiency.

Let
\Omega_{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
be an open set and
m\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
S^{m}_{\psi a}(\Omega_{1})
be the set of pseudoanalytic amplitudes of order
m
that only depend on one spacial variable (the frequency
\eta
still varies in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
). For any
a\in S^{m}_{\psi a}(\Omega_{1})
we shall first introduce, defined via its complement, the
essential support
, denoted by
\mathrm{ess}\ \mathrm{supp}a
. Denoting still by
a
the smooth extension of
a
to
\Omega_{1}{C}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
that is holomorphic in
z
, where
\Omega_{1}{C}
is some complex neighborhood of
\Omega_{1}
, for any
(z_{0},\eta_{0})\in\Omega_{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
, we say
(z_{0},\eta_{0})\not\in\mathrm{ess}\ \mathrm{supp}a
if for all
\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert\leq\max\{\lceil m\rceil+n+1,0\}
there are
C,c>0
so that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\partial^{\alpha}_{\eta}a(z,\eta)\right\rvert\leq Ce^{-c\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\,,\quad\text{for all}\quad(z,\eta)\in\Omega_{1}{C}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}\colon\left(z,\frac{\eta}{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\right)\ \text{near}\ \left(z_{0},\frac{\eta_{0}}{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\right)\,.

By definition
\mathrm{ess}\ \mathrm{supp}a
is a closed set.

A version of the following may be found in
[
KRS21
, Prop. D.3]
, but we shall prove it again for the convenience of the reader.

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
a\in\mathrm{S}^{m}_{\psi a}(\Omega_{1})
with
m\in\mathbb{R}
and
\varphi
an analytic phase function. The distribution
A
defined by

C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega_{1})\ni u\mapsto\iint e^{i\varphi(z,\eta)}a(z,\eta)u(z)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z

satisfies

\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}{A}\subset\left\{(z,\varphi_{z}(z,\eta))\in T^{\ast}(\Omega_{1})\setminus\{0\}\colon(z,\eta)\in\mathrm{ess}\ \mathrm{supp}a\,,\ \text{and}\ \ \varphi_{\eta}(z,\eta)=0\right\}\,.

Proof.

Let
(z_{0},\zeta_{0})\not\in\{(z,\varphi_{z}(z,\eta))\colon\varphi_{\eta}(z,\eta)=0,(z,\eta)\in\mathrm{ess}\ \mathrm{supp}a\}
.

According to
Proposition

2.14
the result is proved if we can find a sequence
\psi_{L}
of Ehrenpreis cutoffs, identically equal to
1
near
z_{0}
, and a conic neighborhood
\Gamma
of
\zeta_{0}
, and a
k\in\mathbb{N}
so that for all
\sigma\in\Gamma
,
\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert=1
and
t\geq 1
large enough,

\left\lvert\mathcal{F}(\psi_{L}A)(t\sigma)\right\rvert\leq t^{k}C(CL/t)^{L}\,.

Let
\psi_{L}
be a sequence of Ehrenpreis cut-offs, identically equal to
1
near
z_{0}
, the support of which we denote by
Z=\mathrm{supp}\psi_{L}
, which will be chosen small enough throughout this proof.

Additionally, let
\sigma\in\Gamma\cap\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
, where
\Gamma
is an open conic neighborhood of
\zeta_{0}
we will choose slim enough throughout this proof.

By
[
Fri98
, Thm. 8.4.1]
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\psi_{L}A)(t\sigma)

\displaystyle=\langle\psi_{L}A,e^{-itz\cdot\sigma}\rangle_{z}=\langle A(z),\psi_{L}(z)e^{-itz\cdot\sigma}\rangle_{z}

\displaystyle=\iint e^{i\varphi(z,\eta)-itz\cdot\sigma}a(z,\eta)\psi_{L}(z)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=t^{n}\iint e^{it(\varphi(z,\eta)-z\cdot\sigma)}a(z,t\eta)\psi_{L}(z)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z

(2.18)

where we used substitution in the last line.

Let us remark at this point that we may assume without loss of generality that
m<-n-1
, where
m
is the order of
a
. If this were not so, the distribution
A
were defined via an oscillatory integral which would have acted on
\psi_{L}(z)e^{-itz\cdot\sigma}
via an analytic differential operator of order
\leq\lceil m\rceil+n+1
, introducing some finite power of
t
and acting on
\psi_{L}
. As seen in the proof of
Proposition

2.14
, one can shift the indices of the Ehrenpreis cut-offs, so that this is no obstacle. Furthermore, the amplitude
a
would have been differentiated and multiplied by symbols at most
\lceil m\rceil+n+1
many times, so that the resulting symbol is still exponentially decaying on
\mathrm{ess}\ \mathrm{supp}a
.

Notice that since
\varphi
is homogeneous with respect to
\eta
of degree
1
, if
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\leq 2\delta
is small enough and
\sigma
varies in a small enough conic set
\Gamma
, then
\left\lvert\nabla_{z}(\varphi(z,\eta)-z\cdot\sigma)\right\rvert\geq c>0
for some
c>0
because
\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert=1
. We will use this fact to introduce cut-offs with respect to
\eta
: Let
(\gamma_{j}(\eta))_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
a sequence of Ehrenpreis cut-offs so that
\gamma_{j}\equiv 1
on
\{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\leq\delta\}
and
\mathrm{supp}\gamma_{j}\subset\{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\leq 2\delta\}
.

(\mathrm{ess}\ \mathrm{supp}a)^{\complement}
\delta
2\delta
\mathrm{supp}(1-\gamma_{L})(1-\chi_{L})
\chi_{L}\equiv 1

Figure 1:
The space represents
\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The two circles marked
\delta
and
2\delta
are the spheres of radius
\delta,2\delta
respectively around
\eta=0
. The conic region in between the two dashed lines is
(\mathrm{ess}\ \mathrm{supp}a)^{\complement}
, the region of space marked by dashed diagonal lines is where
\chi_{L}\equiv 1
, and
(1-\gamma_{L})(1-\chi_{L})
is supported in the region marked by dots.

Notice also that
(z_{0},\zeta_{0})\not\in\{(z,\varphi_{z}(z,\eta))\colon\varphi_{\eta}(z,\eta)=0,(z,\eta)\in\mathrm{ess}\ \mathrm{supp}a\}
implies that there exists an open neighborhood
Z^{\prime}\times\Gamma^{\prime}
of
\mathrm{ess}\ \mathrm{supp}a
so that

(z_{0},\zeta_{0})\not\in\{(z,\varphi_{z}(z,\eta))\colon\varphi_{\eta}(z,\eta)=0,(z,\eta)\in Z^{\prime}\times\Gamma^{\prime}\}\,.

(2.19)

Now, let
(\chi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be a sequence of Ehrenpreis cut-offs that are identically
1
in an open neighborhood of the set
\left(\{\eta\colon(z_{0},\eta)\in\mathrm{ess}\ \mathrm{supp}a\}\cap\{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq\delta\}\right)
and supported in
\Gamma^{\prime}\cap\{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq\frac{1}{2}\delta\}
. Refer to
Fig.

1
for a sketch of the support properties of
\gamma_{L},\chi_{L}
.

According to
Eq.

2.18
we can write

\mathcal{F}(\psi_{L}A)(t\sigma)=t^{n}(I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3})\,,

(2.20)

where

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=\iint e^{it(\varphi(z,\eta)-z\cdot\sigma)}a(z,t\eta)\gamma_{L}(\eta)\psi_{L}(z)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=\iint e^{it(\varphi(z,\eta)-z\cdot\sigma)}a(z,t\eta)(1-\gamma_{L}(\eta))(1-\chi_{L}(\eta))\psi_{L}(z)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=\iint e^{it(\varphi(z,\eta)-z\cdot\sigma)}a(z,t\eta)(1-\gamma_{L}(\eta))\chi_{L}(\eta)\psi_{L}(z)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z\,.

Estimating
I_{1}
:

For
\kappa(z,\eta)=\varphi(z,\eta)-z\cdot\sigma
we have argued above that
\nabla_{z}\kappa\neq 0
on the support of
\gamma_{L}
, so that
\kappa
satisfies assumption

κ

2.
there. Thus, after
L
applications of partial integration with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa,2}
defined in
Eq.

2.12
, we apply
Eq.

2.15
to see that for
t\geq R_{a}L
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert I_{1}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\iint e^{it(\varphi(z,\eta)-z\cdot\sigma)}a(z,t\eta)\gamma_{L}(\eta)\psi_{L}(z)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z\right\rvert\leq t^{m}M_{a}(C_{a}C_{\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}}L/t)^{L}\int_{Z}\int_{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert<2\delta}1\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\leq t^{m}M^{\prime}(C^{\prime}L/t)^{L}

for some
M^{\prime},C^{\prime}>0
.

Estimating
I_{2}
:

Because
1-\chi_{L}
is supported in a subset of
(\complement\{\eta\colon(z_{0},\eta)\in\mathrm{ess}\ \mathrm{supp}a\})\cup\{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert<\delta\}
, and since
1-\gamma_{L}
is supported in
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq\delta
, if we let
Z=\mathrm{supp}\psi_{L}
be a small enough neighborhood around
z_{0}
we conclude that on the support of
(1-\gamma_{L}(\eta))(1-\chi_{L}(\eta))\psi_{L}(z)
we know that
a
is exponentially decaying. Thus,
there exist
c,C>0
so that

\left\lvert I_{2}\right\rvert=\left\lvert\iint e^{it(\varphi(z,\eta)-z\cdot\sigma)}a(z,t\eta)(1-\gamma_{L}(\eta))(1-\chi_{L}(\eta))\psi_{L}(z)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z\right\rvert\leq C\int_{Z}\int_{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq\delta}e^{-ct\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z

Because for
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq\delta
,

e^{-\frac{c}{2}t\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\leq e^{-\frac{c\delta}{2}t}=\left(e^{\frac{c\delta t}{2L}}\right)^{-L}\leq\left(\frac{c\delta t}{2L}+1\right)^{-L}\leq\left(\frac{c\delta t}{2L}\right)^{-L}\,,

we conclude

\left\lvert I_{2}\right\rvert\leq C\int_{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq\delta}e^{-ct\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\mathrm{d}\eta=C\int_{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq\delta}e^{-\frac{c}{2}t\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}e^{-\frac{c}{2}t\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\mathrm{d}\eta\leq C\left(\frac{2L}{c\delta t}\right)^{L}\int_{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq\delta}e^{-\frac{c}{2}t\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\mathrm{d}\eta\leq C\left(\frac{2L}{c\delta t}\right)^{L}\,,

where the constant
C>0
may have changed from one inequality to the next.

Estimating
I_{3}
:

Recall again the choice
\kappa(z,\eta;\sigma)=\varphi(z,\eta)-z\cdot\sigma
. We have established in
Eq.

2.19
and in the definition of
\chi_{L}
that

(z_{0},\eta)\in\mathrm{supp}(\psi_{L}(z)\chi_{L}(\eta))\wedge\varphi_{\eta}(z_{0},\eta)=0\implies\varphi_{z}(z_{0},\eta)\neq\zeta_{0},

which implies that choosing
Z=\mathrm{supp}\psi_{L}\subset Z^{\prime}
small enough (containing
z_{0}
), and
\Gamma
slim enough (around
\zeta_{0}
),

\nabla_{z,\eta}\kappa(z,\eta;\sigma)\neq 0\quad\forall(z,\eta,\sigma)\in\mathrm{supp}\psi_{L}\chi_{L}\times\Gamma\,.

Due to the fact that
\kappa(z,\eta;\sigma)
is the sum of real-analytic homogeneous functions of degree
1
and
0
in
\eta
, we see that
\kappa
satisfies assumption

κ

1.
on
\mathrm{supp}\psi_{L}\chi_{L}
.

Now we may apply partial integration
L
times using
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}
from
Eq.

2.11
to get

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=\iint e^{it(\varphi(z,\eta)-z\cdot\sigma)}a(z,t\eta)(1-\gamma_{L}(\eta))\chi_{L}(\eta)\psi_{L}(z)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=(it)^{-L}\iint e^{it(\varphi(z,\eta)-z\cdot\sigma)}\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}^{L}\left(a(z,t\eta)(1-\gamma_{L}(\eta))\chi_{L}(\eta)\psi_{L}(z)\right)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z\,.

Finally, according to
Eq.

2.14
we get for
t\geq R_{a}L/\delta
that

\left\lvert I_{3}\right\rvert\leq M_{a}C(C_{a}\max\{1,\delta\}^{-1}C_{\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}}L/t)^{L}\int_{Z}\int_{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq\delta}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{m}\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z\leq C^{\prime}(C^{\prime}L/t)^{L}\,,

for some
C^{\prime}>0
, as
m<-n-1
.

The estimates for
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}
together with
Eq.

2.20
and application of
Proposition

2.14
complete the proof.
∎

We can thus state a version of
[
Tre22
, Thm. 18.7.2]
as an immediate corollary of
[
Hör03
, Thm. 8.5.5]
and
Proposition

2.15
:

Corollary 2.16
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{R}
and
a\in S^{m}_{\psi a}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2})
and
\varphi
an analytic phase with
\varphi_{x}\neq 0,\varphi_{z}\neq 0
when
\varphi_{\eta}=0,\eta\neq 0
. The operator
S\colon C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega_{2})\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega_{1})
defined for all
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega_{2})
by

Su(z)=\iint e^{i\varphi(z,x,\eta)}a(z,x,\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta u(x)\mathrm{d}x

satisfies, when extended to distributions
u\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\Omega_{2})
,

\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}(Su)\subset\Lambda_{\varphi}\circ\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}(u)\,,

where
\Lambda_{\varphi}
is the canonical relation associated to
\varphi
.

Remark 2.17
.

If
a
in the statement of
Proposition

2.15
is exponentially decaying for all large
\eta
, the assumption that
a
is a pseudoanalytic amplitude can be weakened, see
[
Tre22
, Prop. 17.1.19]
. One will find then that the analytic wavefront set of
A
is empty.

3
Analytic Wavefront Transformed by Elliptic Analytic FIO

We will fix some notation that will remain throught throughout the entire section.

Let
\mathbb{N}\ni N\geq N^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\hat{\Lambda}\subset(T^{\ast}\mathbb{R}^{N}\times T^{\ast}\mathbb{R}^{N^{\prime}})\setminus\{0\}
be a conic Lagrangian manifold. Let
(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta},\hat{x},\hat{\xi})\in\hat{\Lambda}
be fixed and let there be open neighborhoods
\hat{V}\ni(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta})
and
\hat{U}\ni(\hat{x},\hat{\xi})
so that
\hat{\Lambda}
is an analytic manifold in
\hat{\Lambda}\cap(\hat{V}\times\hat{U})
.

We introduce the projections

\pi_{L}\colon\hat{\Lambda}\cap(\hat{V}\times\hat{U})\ni(z,\zeta;x,\xi)\mapsto(z,\zeta)\in\hat{V}\,,\quad\pi_{R}\colon\hat{\Lambda}\cap(\hat{V}\times\hat{U})\ni(z,\zeta;x,\xi)\mapsto(x,\xi)\in\hat{U}\,,

and put
\hat{Z}=\pi_{z}(\hat{V})
and
\hat{X}=\pi_{x}(\hat{U})
.

Finally, we will denote by
X\subset\hat{X}
and
V\subset\hat{V}
open neighborhoods of
\hat{x}
and
(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta})
respectively that will be chosen small enough throughout this section.

Note here that the assumption
N\geq N^{\prime}
corresponds to the notion that measurements vary in a space of dimension at least equal to that of the space the input varies in. This formally guarantees (over-)determinedness of the problem.

Theorem 3.1
.

On
\hat{\Lambda}\cap(\hat{V}\times\hat{U})
let
\hat{\Lambda}
be associated to a non-degenerate analytic phase
\varphi
, and let
\varphi
be salutary. Let
(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta},\hat{x},\hat{\xi})\in\hat{\Lambda}
be fixed as above, and
\hat{\eta}
so that
G_{\varphi}^{-1}(\{\hat{z},\hat{\zeta},\hat{x},\hat{\xi}\})=\{(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})\}
(defined in
Definition

2.9
).
Let
m\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathrm{FS}^{m}_{\psi a}(\hat{Z}\times\hat{X})\ni\underline{a}=\sum_{j\geq 0}a_{j}
be a formal pseudoanalytic amplitude that is elliptic and classical at
(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})
, and let
a
be any finite realization of
\underline{a}
.

Let
T\colon C_{c}^{\infty}(\hat{X})\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\hat{Z})
be an FIO with kernel

T(z,x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}}e^{i\varphi(z,x,\eta)}a(z,x,\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\,.

(3.1)

In this situation, for any
f\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(X)
, where
X\subset\hat{X}
is sufficiently small, we have

(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta})\not\in\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}(Tf)\implies(\hat{x},\hat{\xi})\not\in\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}(f)\,.

In particular we note that the analytic wavefront set does not depend on the choice of finite realization of the formal amplitude
\underline{a}
. The proof of
Theorem

3.1
will be performed in
Section

3.1
.

Corollary 3.2
.

In the same situation as
Theorem

3.1
under the additional assumption that for any
x\in\hat{X}
,

\{(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta},x,\xi)\}\in\hat{\Lambda}\implies\xi\neq 0

(3.2)

we have that for any
f\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\hat{X})
,

(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta})\not\in\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}(Tf)\iff(\hat{x},\hat{\xi})\not\in\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}(f)\,.

(3.3)

Proof.

Notice that
Corollary

2.16
together with the global part of the Bolker condition

ph

1.
gives the “
\impliedby
” direction of
Eq.

3.3
, so we must only show the other direction.

Let
X\subset\hat{X}
be sufficiently small for the result of
Theorem

3.1
to hold. Let
\gamma\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\hat{X})
with
\mathrm{supp}\gamma\subset X
and
\gamma\equiv 1
near
\hat{x}\in X
. Let
(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta})\not\in\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}(Tf)
.

According to
Proposition

2.15
and the fact that the phase
\varphi
is associated to
\hat{\Lambda}
, we know that
\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}(T)\subset\hat{\Lambda}
. Notice that assumption

ph

3.
means we may apply
[
Hör03
, Thm. 8.5.5]
, which, using the notation therein, implies

\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}(T(1-\gamma)f)\subset\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}(T)_{Z}\cup(\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}(T)\circ\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}((1-\gamma)f))\,.

(3.4)

Assumption
Eq.

3.2
implies
(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta})\not\in\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}(T)_{Z}
. Now because
(1-\gamma)f
vanishes identically near
\hat{x}
, we have
(\hat{x},\hat{\xi})\not\in\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}((1-\gamma)f)
. Furthermore, the Bolker condition

ph

1.
says
\pi_{L}^{-1}((\hat{z},\hat{\zeta}))=\{(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta},\hat{x},\hat{\xi})\}
from which we conclude
(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta})\not\in\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}(T)\circ\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}((1-\gamma)f)
, and thus
Eq.

3.4
gives
(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta})\not\in\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}(T(1-\gamma)f)
. This in turn implies
(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta})\not\in\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}(T\gamma f)
because
(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta})\not\in\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}(Tf)
.

Now
\mathrm{supp}\gamma f\subset X
so that we may conclude from
Theorem

3.1
that
(\hat{x},\hat{\xi})\not\in\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}(\gamma f)
, and since
\gamma
is identically
1
near
\hat{x}
, we thus see that
(\hat{x},\hat{\xi})\not\in\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}(f)
.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

Using the arguments in the proof of
Corollary

3.2
, following also the proof of
[
MST23
, Thm. 5.1]
allows the reformulation of
Theorem

3.1
into a geometric context as long as the canonical relation
\hat{\Lambda}
is represented with salutary phases in each of its coordinate charts.

Recalling that
(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta},\hat{x},\hat{\xi})\in\hat{\Lambda}
is a fixed point, using
[
Hör03
, Thm. 8.5.6’]
(and
[
Hör03
, Prop. 8.5.8]
to allow
C^{1}
regularity),
Corollary

3.2
allows us to conclude

Theorem 3.4
.

Let
\hat{H}
be a
C^{1}
hypersurface in
\hat{X}
with normal
\hat{\xi}
at
\hat{x}
, and let
f\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\hat{X})
vanish on one side of
\hat{H}
near
\hat{x}
. If
T
is an FIO as in
Theorem

3.1
being in the same situation as in
Corollary

3.2
, then

Tf(z)\in C^{\omega}\ \ \text{for}\ z\ \text{near}\ \hat{z}\implies f(x)=0\ \ \text{for}\ x\ \text{near}\ \hat{x}\,.

3.1
FBI Transform and Proof of
Theorem

3.1

From this point onward, in all of
Section

3
,
T
and
\varphi
will refer to the FIO and phase respectively defined in
Eq.

3.1
enjoying the properties stated in
Theorem

3.1
. In order to study the analytic wavefront set of the analytic FIO
T
given in
Eq.

3.1
, we use Gaussian wave packets and the FBI transform.

Let
M\colon\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R},m\colon\mathbb{R}^{N^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{R}
be
M(z)=c_{N}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\lvert z\right\rvert^{2}}
and
m(x)=c_{n}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{2}}
, with
c_{k}=2^{-{k}/2}\pi^{-\frac{3k}{4}},k\in\mathbb{N}
and for
\lambda>0
and
u=(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2N^{\prime}}
and
v=(v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2N}
set

M_{v}^{\lambda}(z)\coloneqq\lambda^{\frac{3N}{4}}e^{i\lambda z\cdot v_{2}}M(\sqrt{\lambda}(z-v_{1}))\,,\quad\text{and}\quad m_{u}^{\lambda}(x)\coloneqq\lambda^{\frac{3N^{\prime}}{4}}e^{i\lambda x\cdot u_{2}}m(\sqrt{\lambda}(x-u_{1}))\,.

Following
[
Mar02
, § 3.1]
, for
f\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\hat{X})
, the FBI transform
L_{m}^{\lambda}f
of
f
is given by

(L_{m}^{\lambda}f)(u)\coloneqq\int f(x)\overline{m_{u}^{\lambda}}(x)\mathrm{d}x\,,

which, by
[
Mar02
, Prop. 3.1.6]
, admits the inversion

f(x)=\int(L_{m}^{\lambda}f)(u)m_{u}^{\lambda}(x)\mathrm{d}u\,.

In order to deal with the technicality of applying
T
to non-compactly supported functions, in
Section

3.2
we show

Lemma 3.5
.

If
\hat{Z}
and
\hat{X}
are small enough open neighborhoods of
\hat{z},\hat{x}
respectively and
\overline{\hat{X}},\overline{\hat{Z}}
denote the closure of
\hat{X},\hat{Z}
respectfully, the operator
T\colon C_{c}^{\infty}(\hat{X})\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\hat{Z})
can be extended to
T\colon C^{\infty}(\overline{\hat{X}})\to\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\overline{\hat{Z}})
, where
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\overline{\hat{Z}})
is the continuous dual space of
C^{\infty}(\overline{\hat{Z}})
.

In the proof of
Theorem

3.1
we will show that we may always reduce to this case of
\hat{Z},\hat{X}
being as small as we desire, but for now let us assume that it is the case.

Letting
L_{M}
denote the FBI transform over
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\hat{Z})
, using the inversion of the FBI transform, for all
v\in\mathbb{R}^{2N}
we see

\displaystyle(L_{M}^{\lambda}Tf)(v)

\displaystyle=\int f(x)K^{\lambda}(x,v)\mathrm{d}x\,,

where, recalling
u
ranges over
\mathbb{R}^{2N^{\prime}}
,

\displaystyle K^{\lambda}(x,v)

\displaystyle=\int\langle Tm_{u}^{\lambda},M_{v}^{\lambda}\rangle\overline{m_{u}^{\lambda}(x)}\mathrm{d}u\,.

(3.5)

In
Section

3.3
we will give a precise description of the kernel
K^{\lambda}
, and in
Sections

3.7
,
3.6
and
3.9
the kernel will be estimated, culminating in

Proposition 3.6
.

Let the open neighborhoods
X\times V\ni(\hat{x},\hat{v})
be small enough (where
\hat{v}=(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta})
). We can write
K^{\lambda}=K_{1}^{\lambda}+K_{2}^{\lambda}
so that the following holds for all
(x,v)\in X\times V
.

First,

K_{1}^{\lambda}(x,v)=\lambda^{m-(N+n)/2}e^{i\lambda\psi(x,v)}b(x,v;\lambda)+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})\,,

(3.6)

where
\psi\in C^{\omega}(X\times V;\mathbb{C})
is real-analytic and
b
is an elliptic formal classical analytic amplitude according to
[
Sjö82
, § 1]
, which is independent of the choice of finite realization
a
of
\underline{a}
modulo
\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})
. Recall,
m\in\mathbb{R}
is the order of
\underline{a}
from
Theorem

3.1
.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle K_{2}^{\lambda}(x,v)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})\,.

(3.7)

Using the Bolker condition, see
Definition

2.9
, the proof being modified only by citing
[
SH02
, Lem. 4.3]
, we can state without proof

Lemma 3.7

(
[
MST23
, Lem. 5.5]
)
.

There exists a neighborhood
\Lambda
of
(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta},\hat{x},\hat{\xi})
in
\hat{\Lambda}
so that
\pi_{L}(\Lambda)\ni(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta})
is an analytic
(N+N^{\prime})
-dimensional submanifold of
T^{\ast}\mathbb{R}^{N}
and the map

\chi\coloneqq\pi_{R}\circ\pi_{L}^{-1}\colon\pi_{L}(\Lambda)\to\pi_{R}(\Lambda)

is an analytic surjective submersion.

Thus,
Lemma

3.7
allows us to uniquely determine the canonical relation near
(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta},\hat{x},\hat{\xi})
knowing only the measurements (the interpretation of
(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta})
). This will also be used in
Section

3.8
where we show that the phase
\psi
appearing in
Eq.

3.6
satisfies

Proposition 3.8
.

If the open neighborhoods
X\times V\ni(\hat{x},\hat{v})
are small enough, then for
\Lambda
from
Lemma

3.7
and all
v
in a complex neighborhood of
\pi_{L}(\Lambda)\cap V
,

1.

\psi(\pi_{z}(\chi(v)),v)=-v_{1}\cdot v_{2}
, where
v=(v_{1},v_{2})
,

2.

\psi_{x}(\pi_{z}(\chi(v)),v)=-u_{2}
, where
\chi(v)=(u_{1},u_{2})
,

3.

For real
x,v
, we have
\mathrm{Im}\psi(x,v)\geq C\left\lvert x-\pi_{z}(\chi(v))\right\rvert^{2}
.

Combining the above results we can then prove
Theorem

3.1
, the proof following that of
[
MST23
, Thm. 5.2]
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.1
.

Notice that
(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta})\not\in\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}(Tf)
if and only if this is true for the restriction of
Tf
to any open set containing
\hat{z}
, so that we may assume without loss that
\hat{Z}
is as small as we like. Similarly, by taking
\mathrm{supp}f
small enough (and thus
\hat{X}
too) we may assume that
Lemma

3.5
may be applied.

By assumption
\hat{v}=(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta})\not\in\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}(Tf)
, so that by
[
Sjö82
, Def. 6.1]
, there exists
\varepsilon>0
so that for all
v=(v_{1},v_{2})
in a neighborhood of
\hat{v}
,

\overline{(L_{M}^{\lambda}Tf)(v)}e^{-i\lambda v_{1}\cdot v_{2}}=c_{N}\lambda^{\frac{3N}{4}}\int\overline{Tf(z)}e^{i\lambda(z-v_{1})\cdot v_{2}}e^{-\frac{\lambda}{2}(z-v_{1})^{2}}\mathrm{d}z=\mathcal{O}(e^{-\lambda\varepsilon})\,.

Taking the complex conjugate, using
Eq.

3.5
together with
Proposition

3.6
, sucking the polynomial term in
\lambda
coming from
K_{1}^{\lambda}
into the right-hand side, we conclude

\int e^{i\lambda(\psi(x,v)+v_{1}\cdot v_{2})}f(x)b(x,v;\lambda)\mathrm{d}x=\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})\,.

(3.8)

for some elliptic formal classical analytic amplitude according to
[
Sjö82
, § 1]
.

Choosing
\Lambda
in
Lemma

3.7
small enough, using the constant rank theorem (see the proof of
[
Lee13
, Thm. 4.26]
), we find an analytic
\chi^{+}\colon\pi_{R}(\Lambda)\to\pi_{L}(\Lambda)
so that
\chi\circ\chi^{+}=\mathrm{id}
. Taking
X\supset\mathrm{supp}f
with
X\subset\pi(\pi_{R}(\Lambda))
small enough for
Propositions

3.6
and
3.8
to hold, we define

\tilde{\psi}(x,u)\coloneqq\psi(x,\chi^{+}(u))+\chi^{+}(u)_{1}\cdot\chi^{+}(u)_{2}\,,\quad\tilde{\psi}\colon X\times\pi_{R}(\Lambda)\to\mathbb{C}\,.

Since
Eq.

3.8
is true for all
v
near
\hat{v}
and thus for all
v\in\pi_{L}(\Lambda)
, it is true for all
v\in\chi^{+}(\pi_{R}(\Lambda))
. Thus, putting
\tilde{b}(x,u;\lambda)\coloneqq b(x,\chi^{+}(u);\lambda)
, as a consequence of
Eq.

3.8
, for all
u
near
\hat{u}=(\hat{x},\hat{\xi})
we have

\int f(x)e^{i\lambda\tilde{\psi}(x,u)}\tilde{b}(x,u;\lambda)\mathrm{d}x=\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})\,.

Proposition

3.8
together with
\chi\circ\chi^{+}=\mathrm{id}
ensure that
\tilde{\psi}
admits the necessary properties to apply
[
Sjö82
, Def. 6.1]
, from which we may conclude that
(\hat{x},-\hat{\xi})\not\in\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}(\overline{f})
, which implies that
(\hat{x},\hat{\xi})\not\in\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}(f)
.

Since the analytic symbol
b
is independent of the choice of finite realization
a
of
\underline{a}
modulo an exponentially decaying term (see
Proposition

3.6
), this completes the proof.
∎

Loosely, one can interpret the proof above as follows: the FIO
T
when composed from the left by an FBI transform becomes an FBI transform, which allows one to read off the analytic wavefront set of
T
.

3.2
A Good Representation of the FIO

The FIO
T
defined in
Eq.

3.1
is defined as an oscillatory integral as usual, see
[
Dui11
, Thm. 2.2.1]
or
[
Hör03
, § 7.8]
. However, we will want to maintain properties (especially the ellipticity
Eq.

2.4
) of the amplitude
a
on a bounded set of frequencies for which we will choose to define
T
by a specific choice of oscillatory integral (which aligns with usual one).

Lemma 3.9
.

If
\hat{Z},\hat{X}
are small enough open neighborhoods of
\hat{z},\hat{x}
respectively the following holds. For any
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, identically
1
near the origin, the action of
T\colon C_{c}^{\infty}(\hat{X})\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\hat{Z})
is given by

\langle Tf,g\rangle=\langle T_{1,\chi}f,g\rangle+\langle T_{2,\chi}f,g\rangle\,,

where
T_{1,\chi}
is the distribution kernel

\displaystyle T_{1,\chi}(z,x)

\displaystyle=\int e^{i\varphi(z,x,\eta)}\chi(\eta)a(z,x,\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\,,

(3.9)

and there exist
a_{\alpha,\chi}\in S^{-n-1}_{\psi a}(\hat{Z}\times\hat{X})
so that

\displaystyle\langle T_{2,\chi}f,g\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert\leq\lceil m\rceil+n+1}\int_{\hat{Z}}\int_{\hat{X}}\int e^{i\varphi(z,x,\eta)}a_{\alpha,\chi}(z,x,\eta)f(x)\partial^{\alpha}_{z}g(z)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}z\,,

(3.10)

and each
a_{\alpha,\chi}(z,x,\eta)=\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}((1-\chi(\eta))a(z,x,\eta))
where
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
is an analytic differential operator in
(z,\eta)
of order
\lceil m\rceil+n+1
.

Finally, this extension
T
is independent of the choice of
\chi
.

Proof.

We begin by remarking that for
T_{1,\chi}
from
Eq.

3.9
we have
T_{1,\chi}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\hat{Z}\times\hat{X})
since the support of
\chi
is bounded, so that we must only make sense of
T_{2,\chi}
in an oscillatory integral fashion.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, consider the amplitude
a_{\varepsilon}(z,x,\eta)=e^{-\varepsilon\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2}}a(z,x,\eta)\in S^{-\infty}_{\psi a}(\hat{Z}\times\hat{X})
. Following arguments in
[
Tre22
, § 18.1.4]
and
[
Hör71
, p. 88]
, we have
a_{\varepsilon}\to a
in
S_{\psi a}^{m^{\prime}}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
for every
m^{\prime}>m
.

For this choice of amplitude, the kernel

T_{2,\chi}^{\varepsilon}=\int e^{i\varphi(z,x,\eta)}(1-\chi(\eta))a_{\varepsilon}(z,x,\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\hat{Z}\times\hat{X})

is well-defined.

Since
\varphi
is a salutary analytic phase at
(\hat{z},\hat{x})
, it satisfies
\left\lvert\nabla_{z,\eta}\varphi\right\rvert\neq 0
for all
(z,x)
near
(\hat{z},\hat{x})
and
\eta
with
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert=1
. Let
\rho=\left\lvert\varphi_{z}\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\varphi_{\eta}\right\rvert^{2}
. We have
\rho\neq 0
and (for
\hat{Z},\hat{X}
small enough around
\hat{z},\hat{x}
) the minimum

\min\{\rho(z,x,\eta)\colon z\in\overline{\hat{Z}},x\in\overline{\hat{X}},\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert=1\}=\delta>0\,,

is attained, and since
\rho
is homogeneous of degree
2
, we thus have that for
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert
away from the origin (in particular
\eta
in the support of
1-\chi
),
\rho(z,x,\eta)\geq\delta\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2}
.

Let

\mathcal{M}^{\ast}\coloneqq-i\frac{\varphi_{z}\cdot\nabla_{z}+\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2}\varphi_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\eta}}{\rho}\,,

and note that
\mathcal{M}^{\ast}e^{i\varphi}=e^{i\varphi}
.

By partial integration, we calculate that for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\langle T_{2,\chi}^{\varepsilon}f,g\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\hat{Z}}\int_{\hat{X}}\int e^{i\varphi(z,x,\eta)}f(x)\mathcal{M}^{\ell}\left(g(z)(1-\chi(\eta))a_{\varepsilon}(z,x,\eta)\right)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}z

(3.11)

where
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M}^{\ast})^{\ast}
is the adjoint of the operator
\mathcal{M}^{\ast}
.

Notice that since
\chi
has compact support,
(1-\chi(\eta))a(z,x,\eta)\in S^{m}_{\psi a}(\hat{Z}\times\hat{X})
. Taking
\ell=\lceil m\rceil+n+1
and letting
\varepsilon\to 0
in
Eq.

3.11
concludes the proof that
T
is represented by the sum of the RHS of
Eqs.

3.9
and
3.10
.

We turn to the independence of this extension with respect to
\chi
. Introduce

T_{1,\chi}^{\varepsilon}(z,x)=\int e^{i\varphi(z,x,\eta)}\chi(\eta)a_{\varepsilon}(z,x,\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\hat{Z}\times\hat{X})

and let
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be a cut-off identically
1
near the origin. We see that

T^{\varepsilon}_{1,\chi}+T^{\varepsilon}_{2,\chi}=\int e^{i\varphi}a_{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}\eta=\int e^{i\varphi}\psi(\eta)a_{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}\eta+\int e^{i\varphi}(1-\psi(\eta))a_{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}\eta\eqqcolon T_{1}^{\varepsilon}+T^{\varepsilon}_{2}\,.

Now we can make use of repeated partial integration as in
Eq.

3.11
to find an expression for
T^{\varepsilon}_{2}
that has absolutely integrable integrand and thus has a limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Thus, we see that

\langle T_{1,\chi}f,g\rangle+\langle T_{2,\chi}f,g\rangle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\langle T_{1,\chi}^{\varepsilon}f,g\rangle+\langle T_{2,\chi}^{\varepsilon}f,g\rangle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\langle T_{1}^{\varepsilon}f,g\rangle+\langle T_{2}^{\varepsilon}f,g\rangle

where the RHS is independent of
\chi
. Thus, the extension
T
we have chosen is independent of
\chi
which completes the proof.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

3.5
.

Notice that the right-hand side of
Eq.

3.10
is the finite sum of well-defined, absolutely convergent integrals if
f\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\hat{X}}),g\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\hat{Z}})
. Consequently, for
f\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\hat{X}}),g\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\hat{Z}})
, we will define
\langle T_{2,\chi}f,g\rangle
by the right-hand side of
Eq.

3.10
.

Furthermore, the right-hand side of the definition of
T_{1,\chi}
in
Eq.

3.9
,
is a well-defined, absolutely convergent integral, which we may define to be
\langle T_{1,\chi}f,g\rangle
for
f\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\hat{X}}),g\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\hat{Z}})
.
∎

3.3
An Expression for the Kernel

In this section we find an explicit expression for the kernel
K^{\lambda}
in
Eq.

3.5
. Note first that by the extension of
T
given in
Lemma

3.5
, we may take
T_{1}=T_{1,\chi}
and
T_{2}=T_{2,\chi}
so that defining

K_{1,\chi}^{\lambda}(x,v)\coloneqq\int\langle T_{1,\chi}m_{u}^{\lambda},M_{v}^{\lambda}\rangle\overline{m_{u}^{\lambda}(x)}\mathrm{d}u\,,\quad\text{and}\quad K_{2,\chi}^{\lambda}(x,v)\coloneqq\int\langle T_{2,\chi}m_{u}^{\lambda},M_{v}^{\lambda}\rangle\overline{m_{u}^{\lambda}(x)}\mathrm{d}u\,,

(3.12)

we have

(L_{M}^{\lambda}Tf)(v)=\int f(x)K_{1,\chi}^{\lambda}(x,v)\mathrm{d}x+\int f(x)K_{2,\chi}^{\lambda}(x,v)\mathrm{d}x\,,

Notice that while
K^{\lambda}
is independent of
\chi
, the kernels
K^{\lambda}_{1,\chi}
and
K^{\lambda}_{2,\chi}
depend on a choice of
\chi
(see
Lemma

3.9
), which we will make in
Section

3.5
.

We now compute
K^{\lambda}_{1,\chi}
and
K^{\lambda}_{2,\chi}
precisely.

Lemma 3.10
.

For
K_{1,\chi}^{\lambda},K_{2,\chi}^{\lambda}
from
Eq.

3.12
and
(x,v)\in\hat{X}\times\hat{V}
we have

\displaystyle K_{1,\chi}^{\lambda}(x,v)

\displaystyle=c_{N}\lambda^{\frac{1}{4}(N^{\prime}+3N)}\int_{\hat{Z}}\int e^{i\lambda\Phi(z,\eta;x,v)}\chi(\lambda\eta)a(z,x,\lambda\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z

(3.13)

\displaystyle K_{2,\chi}^{\lambda}(x,v)

\displaystyle=c_{N}\lambda^{\frac{1}{4}(N^{\prime}+3N)}\sum_{\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert,j=0}^{\ell}\lambda^{j}\int_{\hat{Z}}\int e^{i\lambda\Phi(z,\eta;x,v)}\partial^{\gamma}(1-\chi(\lambda\eta))a_{j,\gamma,v}(z,x,\lambda\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z

(3.14)

where
\Phi(z,\eta;x,v)=\varphi(z,x,\eta)-z\cdot v_{2}+\frac{i}{2}\left\lvert z-v_{1}\right\rvert^{2}
and
c_{N}
is the constant defined at the beginning of
Section

3.1
.

Here,
\ell=\lceil m\rceil+n+1
and each
a_{j,\gamma,v}\in S^{-n-1+\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert}_{\psi a}(\hat{Z}\times\hat{X})
.

Proof.

First, using
Eq.

3.12
, we calculate an expression for
K_{1,\chi}^{\lambda}
:

\displaystyle K_{1,\chi}^{\lambda}(x,v)

\displaystyle=c_{N^{\prime}}^{2}c_{N}\lambda^{\frac{3}{4}(2N^{\prime}+N)}\int\int_{\hat{X}}\int_{\hat{Z}}\int e^{i\varphi(z,y,\eta)}\chi(\eta)a(z,y,\eta)e^{-\frac{\lambda}{2}(\left\lvert y-u_{1}\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert z-v_{1}\right\rvert^{2})}e^{i\lambda(y\cdot u_{2}-z\cdot v_{2})}\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot e^{-i\lambda u_{2}\cdot x}e^{-\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lvert x-u_{1}\right\rvert^{2}}\mathrm{d}u

where
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N^{\prime}}}e^{i\lambda u_{2}\cdot(y-x)}\mathrm{d}u_{2}=(2\pi)^{N^{\prime}}\lambda^{-N^{\prime}}\delta_{0}(y-x)
so that because
x\in\hat{X}
:

\displaystyle=c_{N^{\prime}}^{2}c_{N}(2\pi)^{N^{\prime}}\lambda^{\frac{3}{4}(N^{\prime}+N)}\int\int_{\hat{Z}}\int e^{i\varphi(z,x,\eta)}\chi(\eta)a(z,x,\eta)e^{-\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lvert z-v_{1}\right\rvert^{2}}e^{-i\lambda z\cdot v_{2}}\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}ze^{-\lambda\left\lvert x-u_{1}\right\rvert^{2}}\mathrm{d}u_{1}

and integrating out
u_{1}
:
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N^{\prime}}}e^{-\lambda\left\lvert x-u_{1}\right\rvert^{2}}\mathrm{d}u_{1}=\pi^{N^{\prime}/2}\lambda^{-N^{\prime}/2}
(and recalling
c_{N^{\prime}}
):

\displaystyle=\lambda^{\frac{1}{4}(N^{\prime}+3N)}c_{N}\int_{\hat{Z}}\int e^{i\varphi(z,x,\eta)}\chi(\eta)a(z,x,\eta)e^{-\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lvert z-v_{1}\right\rvert^{2}}e^{-i\lambda z\cdot v_{2}}\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z

and changing variables to
\lambda\eta

\displaystyle=\lambda^{\frac{1}{4}(N^{\prime}+3N)}c_{N}\int_{\hat{Z}}\int e^{i\lambda\varphi(z,x,\eta)}e^{-\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lvert z-v_{1}\right\rvert^{2}}e^{-i\lambda z\cdot v_{2}}\chi(\lambda\eta)a(z,x,\lambda\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z\,.

In calculating
K_{2,\chi}^{\lambda}
, the same recipe with a few modifications will lead to the statement. We will make these necessary modifications.

Note that for any multi-index
\alpha
,
\partial^{\alpha}_{z}M_{v}^{\lambda}(z)=q_{v,\alpha}(z,\lambda)M_{v}^{\lambda}(z)
where
q_{v,\alpha}
is a polynomial in
(z,\lambda)
, which is of degree
\leq 2\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert
, and with respect to
\lambda
it is of degree
