\leq\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert
.

Note that by the oscillatory integral definition of
T_{2,\chi}
in
Eq.

3.10
, we have that for
\ell=\lceil m\rceil+n+1
,

\displaystyle\langle T_{2,\chi}m_{u}^{\lambda},M_{v}^{\lambda}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert\leq\ell}\int_{\hat{Z}}\int_{\hat{X}}\int e^{i\varphi(z,x,\eta)}m_{u}^{\lambda}(x)M_{v}^{\lambda}(z)q_{v,\alpha}(z,\lambda)a_{\alpha,\chi}(z,x,\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}z\,,

where each
a_{\alpha,\chi}\in S^{-n-1}_{\psi a}(\hat{Z}\times\hat{X})
and
a_{\alpha,\chi}=\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}((1-\chi(\eta))a(z,x,\eta))
.

Isolating the powers of
\lambda
and absorbing the remaining polynomial coming from
p_{v,\alpha}
into the symbol
a_{\alpha,\chi}
, we may rewrite this as

\displaystyle\langle T_{2,\chi}m_{u}^{\lambda},M_{v}^{\lambda}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}\lambda^{j}\int_{\hat{Z}}\int_{\hat{X}}\int e^{i\varphi(z,x,\eta)}m_{u}^{\lambda}(x)M_{v}^{\lambda}(z)\tilde{a}_{\chi,j,v}(z,x,\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}z

(3.15)

where each
\tilde{a}_{\chi,j,v}\in S^{-n-1}_{\psi a}(\hat{Z}\times\hat{X})
and
\tilde{a}_{\chi,j,v}=\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{j,v}((1-\chi(\eta)a(z,x,\eta)))
for some analytic differential operator
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{j,v}
with respect to
(z,\eta)
of order
\leq\ell
.
Using
Eq.

3.15
we can calculate

K_{2,\chi}^{\lambda}(x,v)=\int\langle T_{2,\chi}m_{u}^{\lambda},M_{v}^{\lambda}\rangle\overline{m_{u}^{\lambda}(x)}\mathrm{d}u

from
Eq.

3.12
the same way we did
K_{1,\chi}^{\lambda}
where we exchange
a
with
a_{\chi,j,v}
to get

K_{2,\chi}^{\lambda}(x,v)=c_{N}\lambda^{\frac{1}{4}(N^{\prime}+3N)}\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}\lambda^{j}\int_{\hat{Z}}\int e^{i\lambda\Phi(z,\eta;x,v)}\tilde{a}_{\chi,j,v}(z,x,\lambda\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z\,.

Now, by writing out the action of
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
on
(1-\chi)a
, one in fact finds
Eq.

3.14
.
∎

3.4
The Phase in
K_{1,\chi},K_{2,\chi}

In this section we investigate the exponent

\Phi(z,\eta;x,v)=\varphi(z,x,\eta)-z\cdot v_{2}+\frac{i}{2}\left\lvert z-v_{1}\right\rvert^{2}

as it appeared in
Lemma

3.10
. Because we assumed that
\varphi
satisfies the unique frequency condition

ph

4.
, let
\hat{\eta}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
be the unique solution of the equation

(\hat{\zeta},\hat{\xi})=(\varphi_{z}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta}),-\varphi_{x}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta}))

and we fix this definition of
\hat{\eta}
throughout. Recall also that
\hat{v}=(\hat{z},\hat{\zeta})
is fixed.

Lemma 3.11
.

Recall the set
\Theta^{\mathbb{C}}_{\varphi}
from
Definition

2.7
. There exist
R_{1},C_{0},C_{1}>0
, complex open neighborhoods
V^{\mathbb{C}}\ni\hat{v}
,
Z^{\mathbb{C}}\ni\hat{z}
,
X^{\mathbb{C}}\ni\hat{x}
and
\Xi^{\mathbb{C}}\ni\hat{\eta}
with
\Xi^{\mathbb{C}}\subset\Theta_{\varphi}^{\mathbb{C}}
, so that setting

\displaystyle\rho(z,\eta,x,v)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\Phi_{\eta}(z,\eta;x,v)\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\Phi_{z}(z,\eta;x,v)\right\rvert^{2}\,,

for all open
\Xi^{\prime}\subset\Xi=\Xi{C}\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\hat{\eta}\in\Xi^{\prime}
and all
(z,x,v)\in Z^{\mathbb{C}}\times X^{\mathbb{C}}\times V^{\mathbb{C}}
, we have that

\displaystyle\eta\in\Theta_{\varphi}^{\mathbb{C}}\,,\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\leq R_{1}

\displaystyle\implies\left\lvert\Phi_{z}(z,\eta;x,v)\right\rvert\geq C_{0}

(3.16)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq R_{1}/2\,\,\text{and}\,\,\eta\not\in\Xi^{\prime}

\displaystyle\implies C_{0}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2}\leq\rho(z,\eta,x,v)\leq C_{1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2}\,.

(3.17)

Furthermore,
there are holomorphic functions
\mathbf{z}\colon X^{\mathbb{C}}\times V^{\mathbb{C}}\to Z^{\mathbb{C}}
,
\bm{\eta}\colon X^{\mathbb{C}}\times V^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}^{n}
, so that

(z,\eta,x,v)\in Z^{\mathbb{C}}\times\Theta^{\mathbb{C}}_{\varphi}\times X^{\mathbb{C}}\times V^{\mathbb{C}}\colon\nabla_{z\eta}\Phi(z,\eta;x,v)=0\iff(z,\eta)=(\mathbf{z}(x,v),\bm{\eta}(x,v))\,,

(3.18)

and
\mathbf{z}(\hat{x},\hat{v})=\hat{z},\bm{\eta}(\hat{x},\hat{v})=\hat{\eta}
, and the Hessian of
\Phi
is non-vanishing at
(\hat{z},\hat{\eta};\hat{x},\hat{v})
and has positive semi-definite imaginary part there.

Proof.

Notice first that by direct computation,
\nabla_{z\eta}\Phi(\hat{z},\hat{\eta};\hat{x},\hat{v})=0
.

Write
\Phi
and
\varphi
for the holomorphic extension of
\Phi
and
\varphi
respectfully in some complex neighborhood around
(\hat{z},\hat{\eta};\hat{x},\hat{v})
, and let
\Sigma_{\varphi}
denote the kernel of
\varphi_{\eta}
.

We follow the proof of
[
MST23
, Lem. 5.11]
to show
Eq.

3.18
. We calculate that

((\nabla_{z,\eta})^{2}\Phi)(\hat{z},\hat{\eta};\hat{x},\hat{v})=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{z}\varphi_{z}&\partial_{z}\varphi_{\eta}\\
\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{z}&\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{\eta}\end{pmatrix}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})+i\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{id}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\eqcolon A+iB\,,

with
A,B
real matrices. We will show that for any real
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{N+n}
if
(A+iB)(a+ib)=0
then
a=b=0
from which we will conclude the non-degeneracy of
(\nabla_{z,\eta}^{2}\Phi)
near
(\hat{z},\hat{\eta};\hat{x},\hat{v})
:
If
(A+iB)(a+ib)=0
, then
Aa=Bb
and
Ab=-Ba
so that

Bb\cdot b=Aa\cdot b=a\cdot Ab=-Ba\cdot a\,,

and then
B^{T}Bb\cdot b=(Bb)^{2}=-B^{T}Ba\cdot a=-(Ba)^{2}
, from which we may conclude that
Ba=Bb=0
which means that
a=(0,a^{\prime})
and
b=(0,b^{\prime})
with
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Now we have that

0=A(a+ib)=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{z}\\
\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{\eta}\end{pmatrix}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})a^{\prime}+i\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{z}\\
\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{\eta}\end{pmatrix}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})b^{\prime}\,,

and as a consequence of
Eq.

2.7
the matrix
\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{z}\\
\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{\eta}\end{pmatrix}(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})
is injective, from which we conclude that
a^{\prime}=b^{\prime}=0
.
Thus, the Hessian of
\Phi
is non-degenerate at
(\hat{z},\hat{\eta};\hat{x},\hat{v})
and has positive semi-definite imaginary part there.

Applying the implicit function theorem then gives
Eq.

3.18
for all
\eta
in some complex neighborhood of
\hat{\eta}
, this will be upgraded to all
\eta\in\Theta{C}_{\varphi}
at the end of this proof.

We begin with the proof of
Eq.

3.16
. Note that

\left\lvert\Phi_{z}(z,\eta,x,v)\right\rvert=\left\lvert\varphi_{z}(z,x,\eta)-v_{2}+i(z-v_{1})\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert v_{2}\right\rvert-\left\lvert\varphi_{z}(z,x,\eta)\right\rvert-\left\lvert z-v_{1}\right\rvert\,.

Now choose open
Z{C}
small enough so that
\left\lvert z-\hat{z}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert\hat{\zeta}\right\rvert/4
for all
z\in Z{C}
and open
V{C}
so small that
\left\lvert v-\hat{v}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert\hat{\zeta}\right\rvert/4
. Choose
R_{1}>0
small enough, so that using that
\varphi_{z}
is homogeneous of degree
1
in
\eta
, for
(z,x)\in Z{C}\times X{C}
and
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert<R_{1}
we have
\left\lvert\varphi_{z}(z,x,\eta)\right\rvert<\left\lvert\hat{\zeta}\right\rvert/4
, so that
Eq.

3.16
is satisfied for some
C_{0}=\left\lvert\hat{\zeta}\right\rvert/4>0
.

We get to proving
Eq.

3.17
.
As a consequence of assumption

ph

3.
, the minimum

\min_{\overline{Z{C}}\times\overline{X{C}}\times\{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert=1\}}\left\lvert\varphi_{\eta}(z,x,\eta)\right\rvert+\left\lvert\varphi_{z}(z,x,\eta)\right\rvert=c_{0}>0

is attained by compactness and continuity.
The homogeneity of
\varphi
in
\eta
then lets us conclude that there exists some
C>0
so that

\left\lvert\varphi_{\eta}(z,x,\eta)\right\rvert<c_{0}/2\implies\left\lvert\varphi_{z}(z,x,\eta)\right\rvert\geq C\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\,.

Finally, we use the fact that
\Phi_{\eta}(z,\eta,x,v)=\varphi_{\eta}(z,x,\eta)
and
\Phi_{z}(z,\eta,x,v)=\varphi_{z}(z,x,\eta)-v_{2}+i(z-v_{1})
so that for
z
near
\hat{z}
and for some
R_{2}>0
we have

\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq R_{2}\implies\left(\left\lvert\Phi_{\eta}(z,\eta,x,v)\right\rvert<c_{0}/2\implies\left\lvert\Phi_{z}(z,\eta,x,v)\right\rvert>C\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\right)\,,

for some
C>0
.

Thus we have shown that for some
C_{0}>0
,

\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq R_{2}\implies\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\Phi_{\eta}(z,\eta;x,v)\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\Phi_{z}(z,\eta;x,v)\right\rvert^{2}\geq C_{0}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2}\,,

(3.19)

which together with the fact that
\Phi
is the sum of functions homogeneous of degree
1
and
0
implies
Eq.

3.17
as long as
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq R_{2}
.

Let open
\Xi^{\prime}\subset\Xi=\Xi{C}\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}
so that
\hat{\eta}\in\Xi^{\prime}
. In order to prove
Eq.

3.17
with
\eta\in\Theta_{\varphi}^{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\Xi^{\prime}
with
R_{1}/2\leq\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\leq R_{2}
, notice that by the unique frequency condition

ph

4.
,

\inf_{R_{1}/2\leq\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\leq R_{2},\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\Xi^{\prime}}\left\lvert\nabla_{z\eta}\Phi(\hat{z},\eta;\hat{x},\hat{v})\right\rvert=\varepsilon^{\prime}>0\,,

so that in a small complex open neighborhood
Z^{\mathbb{C}}\times X^{\mathbb{C}}\times V^{\mathbb{C}}
around
(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{v})
and for the open set
\Xi{C}
small enough containing
\Xi^{\prime}
, we have

\inf\left\{\left\lvert\nabla_{z\eta}\Phi(\hat{z},\eta;\hat{x},\hat{v})\right\rvert\colon(z,\eta;x,v)\in Z^{\mathbb{C}}\times\left(\left\{\eta\in\Theta^{\mathbb{C}}\colon R_{1}/2\leq\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\leq R_{2}\right\}\setminus\Xi^{\mathbb{C}}\right)\times X^{\mathbb{C}}\times V^{\mathbb{C}}\right\}=\varepsilon^{\prime}/2>0\,.

Thus, together with
Eq.

3.19
, we conclude
Eq.

3.17
, which implies that the local version of
Eq.

3.18
is upgraded to a global one (on all of
\Theta^{\mathbb{C}}_{\varphi}
) with respect to
\eta
.
∎

We will now move on to use
Lemma

3.11
to make a good choice of
\chi
as it appears in
K_{1,\chi}^{\lambda},K_{2,\chi}^{\lambda}
.

3.5
Choosing the Cut-Off for the Kernels

Recall that in
Eq.

3.12
we defined the kernels
K_{1,\chi}^{\lambda},K_{2,\chi}^{\lambda}
based on
T_{1,\chi},T_{2,\chi}
, which themselves arose from
Lemma

3.9
under a suitable choice of
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. As one might expect, we will want
\chi
to be an element of a sequence of Ehrenpreis cut-offs, see
Eq.

2.5
.

For
\Xi
from
Lemma

3.11
, we introduce the set
\Xi^{\prime}\subset\Xi
to be an open neighborhood of
\hat{\eta}
so that
\mathrm{dist}(\Xi^{\prime},\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\Xi)>0
.
For
R_{1}>0
from
Eq.

3.16
,
let
\chi_{j}
and
\chi^{\prime}_{j}
be the sequences of Ehrenpreis cut-offs relative to
(\Xi,\Xi^{\prime})
and
\left(\left\{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\leq R_{1}\right\},\left\{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\leq R_{1}/2\right\}\right)
, respectively.

Notice at this point that we have constructed sequences of cut-offs and that the choice
\chi
, present in
Lemma

3.9
, however, is fixed. We remedy this by taking
\chi
to be an element of this sequence for some large enough index. The size of this index will depend on
\lambda
, so that we will define this index as the output of some function depending on
\lambda
.

Let
\tau\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}\to\mathbb{N}
be a function to be determined later. Given this
\tau
, we will make a choice for
\chi
appearing in
Lemma

3.9
(depending on
\lambda
):

\chi_{\lambda}(\eta)\coloneqq\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(\lambda^{-1}\eta)+\chi^{\prime}_{\tau(\lambda)}(\lambda^{-1}\eta)\,,\quad\text{so that}\quad\chi_{\lambda}(\lambda\eta)=\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)+\chi^{\prime}_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)\,.

(3.20)

We will accrue upper bounds on the function
\tau
throughout
Sections

3.6
and
3.7
and make a final choice in
Section

3.9
. Importantly we remark that
\chi_{\lambda}(\lambda\eta)
is the
\tau(\lambda)
-th element of a sequence of Ehrenpreis cut-offs independent of
\lambda
.

Based on this choice of cut-off
\chi_{\lambda}
, we now define

K_{1}^{\lambda}\coloneqq K_{1,\chi_{\lambda}}^{\lambda}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad K_{2}^{\lambda}\coloneqq K_{2,\chi_{\lambda}}^{\lambda}\,.

(3.21)

In total, we will estimate the expressions for
K_{1}^{\lambda}
and
K_{2}^{\lambda}
using two different methods. We will use stationary phase for
K_{1}^{\lambda}
on the support of
\chi_{\lambda}
and repeated partial integration for
K_{2}^{\lambda}
and
K_{1}^{\lambda}
on the support of
\chi^{\prime}_{\lambda}
.

3.6
The Kernel
K_{2}^{\lambda}

The main result of this section is

Proposition 3.12
.

There are small open
X\ni\hat{x},V\ni\hat{v}
so that there exists a
\vartheta_{0}>0
so that for any
\vartheta\in(0,\vartheta_{0}]
, if

\max\{\lceil m\rceil+n+1,\vartheta\lambda/2\}\leq\tau(\lambda)\leq\lambda\vartheta\,,

(3.22)

there are constants
C,\varepsilon>0
so that for all
\lambda\geq 1
, uniformly in
x\in X,v\in V
,

\left\lvert K_{2}^{\lambda}(x,v)\right\rvert\leq Ce^{-\varepsilon\lambda}\,.

and will be proven at the end of this section.

To make the proof of
Proposition

3.12
more palatable, we show the intermediary result

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
l\in\mathbb{N}
. There are small open
X\ni\hat{x},V\ni\hat{v}
so that there exists a
\vartheta_{0}>0
so that for any
\vartheta\in(0,\vartheta_{0}]
, if

\max\{l,\vartheta\lambda/2\}\leq\tau(\lambda)\leq\lambda\vartheta\,,

(3.23)

then the following is true:

For all
\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert\leq l
and all
a_{\gamma}\in S_{\psi a}^{-n-1+\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert}(\hat{Z}\times\hat{X})
, if

b(z,x,\lambda\eta)=\partial^{\gamma}(1-\chi_{\lambda}(\lambda\eta))a_{\gamma}(z,x,\lambda\eta)\,,

and
\chi_{\lambda}
is from
Eq.

3.20
, there are constants
C,\varepsilon>0
so that for all
\lambda\geq 1
, uniformly in
x\in X,v\in V
,

\left\lvert\int_{\hat{Z}}\int e^{i\lambda\Phi(z,\eta;x,v)}b(z,x,\lambda\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z\right\rvert\leq Ce^{-\varepsilon\lambda}\,.

(3.24)

Proof.

Notice that the outer integral in
Eq.

3.24
runs over
z\in\hat{Z}
, which we want to reduce to any small open
Z\ni\hat{z}
: the integral over
\eta
on the LHS of
Eq.

3.24
is absolutely convergent because either
\gamma=0
, making
a_{\gamma}
of order
<-n
, or
\partial^{\gamma}(1-\chi_{\lambda}(\lambda\eta))
is compactly supported, independent of
\lambda
. Now for any open
Z\ni\hat{z}
with
Z\subset\hat{Z}
we have, allowing
v_{1}
to vary over
V_{1}
small enough,

\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\},z\in\hat{Z}\setminus Z\implies\Phi^{i\mathbb{R}}(z,\eta;x,v)\geq\frac{1}{2}\left\lvert z-v_{1}\right\rvert^{2}>\varepsilon

for some
\varepsilon>0
. Thus, introducing a sequence of Ehrenpreis cut-offs
(\psi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
with respect to
(\hat{Z},Z)
, modulo an exponentially decaying term, the LHS of
Eq.

3.24
is equal to

\displaystyle\int_{\hat{Z}}\int e^{i\lambda\Phi(z,\eta;x,v)}\psi_{j}(z)b(z,x,\lambda\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z

(3.25)

for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
.

Because
\Phi
is the sum of real-analytic functions that are homogeneous of degree
1
and
0
in
\eta
respectively, we see that for
\eta
away from the origin we have

\left\lvert\partial^{\alpha}_{z}\partial^{\beta}_{\eta}\Phi(z,\eta;x,v)\right\rvert\leq C^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert+1}\alpha!\beta!\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{1-\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}\,.

(3.26)

Due to
Eq.

3.26
and
Eq.

3.17
, we see that on the set
\left\{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq R_{1}/2\right\}\cap\complement(\Xi^{\prime})\supset\mathrm{supp}b
the function
\kappa=\Phi
satisfies assumption

κ

1.
.

Thus, using partial integration with the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\Phi}
(defined in
Eq.

2.11
) on the integral in
Eq.

3.25
, choosing
j=\tau(\lambda)
,

\int_{\hat{Z}}\int e^{i\lambda\Phi}\psi_{\tau(\lambda)}(z)b(z,x,\lambda\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z=(-i\lambda)^{-\tau(\lambda)+l}\int_{\hat{Z}}\int e^{i\lambda\Phi}\mathcal{L}_{\Phi}^{\tau(\lambda)-l}\left(\psi_{\tau(\lambda)}(z)b(z,x,\lambda\eta)\right)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z\,.

Let us first consider the case
\gamma\neq 0
, so that
b(z,x,\lambda\eta)
can be written as
b_{1}(\eta)b_{2}(z,x,\lambda\eta)
where for some
m\geq-n-1
,
b_{2}\in S^{m}_{\psi a}(\hat{Z}\times\hat{X})
is independent of
\lambda
and
b_{1}
is the
(\leq l)
-th derivative of the
\tau(\lambda)
-th element of a sequence of Ehrenpreis cut-offs with compact support
\subset\{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq R_{1}/2\}
where
R_{1}>0
is from
Eq.

3.16
. This follows from the definition of
\chi_{\lambda}
from
Eq.

3.20
, and since
\chi_{\lambda}
is the
\tau(\lambda)
-th element of a sequence of Ehrenpreis cut-offs.

One may then repeat the proof of
Proposition

2.13
to find that for some
R_{b},M_{b},C_{b}>0
,

\lambda\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq R_{b}\tau(\lambda)\implies\left\lvert\mathcal{L}_{\Phi}^{\tau(\lambda)-l}\left(\psi_{\tau(\lambda)}(z)b(z,x,\lambda\eta)\right)\right\rvert\leq\lambda^{m}M_{b}(C_{b}C_{\mathcal{L}_{\Phi}})^{-l}(C_{b}C_{\mathcal{L}_{\Phi}}\tau(\lambda))^{\tau(\lambda)}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{m}\,,

and because
\mathrm{supp}b\subset\{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\leq C\}
for some
C>0
independent of
\lambda
, we can in fact estimate

\lambda\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq R_{b}\tau(\lambda)\implies\left\lvert\mathcal{L}_{\Phi}^{\tau(\lambda)-l}\left(\psi_{\tau(\lambda)}(z)b(z,x,\lambda\eta)\right)\right\rvert\leq\lambda^{m}M_{b}C^{m+n+1}(C_{b}C_{\mathcal{L}_{\Phi}})^{-l}(C_{b}C_{\mathcal{L}_{\Phi}}\tau(\lambda))^{\tau(\lambda)}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{-n-1}\,.

(3.27)

In the case
\gamma=0
,
b
is not compactly supported with respect to
\eta
, but the amplitude
a_{\gamma}
has order
-n-1
, so that we find
Eq.

3.27
by an application of
Proposition

2.13
instead. Thus let
\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert\leq l
be arbitrary again.

Now because
\mathrm{supp}b\subset\{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq R_{1}/2\}
we use
Eq.

3.27
to see that for
\lambda\geq 2R_{1}^{-1}\tau(\lambda)R_{b}
(which is to say that
\tau(\lambda)\leq\lambda R_{1}R_{b}^{-1}/2
) and
\tau(\lambda)\geq l
we have for some
C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}>0
,

\displaystyle{}\left\lvert(-i\lambda)^{-\tau(\lambda)+l}\int_{\hat{Z}}\int e^{i\lambda\Phi(z,\eta;x,v)}\mathcal{L}_{\Phi}^{\tau(\lambda)-l}\left(\psi_{\tau(\lambda)}(z)b(z,x,\lambda\eta)\right)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{m-\tau(\lambda)+l}C^{\prime}(C_{b}C_{\mathcal{L}_{\Phi}})^{-l}(C_{b}C_{\mathcal{L}_{\Phi}}\tau(\lambda))^{\tau(\lambda)-l}\int_{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq R_{1}/2}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{-n-1}\mathrm{d}\eta\leq C^{\prime\prime}\lambda^{m+l}\left(\frac{C_{b}C_{\mathcal{L}_{\Phi}}\tau(\lambda)}{\lambda}\right)^{\tau(\lambda)}\,.

To get the exponential decay promised in the statement of this lemma, we enforce
\tau(\lambda)\leq\frac{\lambda}{C_{b}C_{\mathcal{L}_{\Phi}}e}
, so that

\left(\frac{C_{b}C_{\mathcal{L}_{\Phi}}\tau(\lambda)}{\lambda}\right)^{\tau(\lambda)}\leq e^{-\tau(\lambda)}\,.

and we may take any
\vartheta_{0}\leq\min\left\{R_{1}R_{b}^{-1}/2,(eC_{b}C_{\mathcal{L}_{\Phi}})^{-1}\right\}
to make the statement of this lemma true. ∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.12
.

First recall that
K_{2}^{\lambda}=K_{2,\chi_{\lambda}}^{\lambda}
from
Eq.

3.21
, where
\chi_{\lambda}
is the cut-off defined in
Eq.

3.20
. Now according to
Eq.

3.14
, we have

\displaystyle K_{2}^{\lambda}(x,v)=c_{N}\lambda^{\frac{1}{4}(N^{\prime}+3N)}\sum_{\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert,j=0}^{\ell}\lambda^{j}\int_{\hat{Z}}\int e^{i\lambda\Phi(z,\eta;x,v)}\partial^{\gamma}(1-\chi_{\lambda}(\lambda\eta))a_{j,\gamma,v}(z,x,\lambda\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z

where
a_{j,\gamma,v}\in S^{-n-1+\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert}_{\psi a}(\hat{Z}\times\hat{X})
for
\ell=\lceil m\rceil+n+1
.

This is to say that
K_{2}^{\lambda}
is a linear combination of terms of the form on the LHS of
Eq.

3.24
, so that applying
Lemma

3.13
concludes the proof and
Eq.

3.22
is a consequence of
Eq.

3.23
.
∎

3.7
The Kernel
K_{1}^{\lambda}

Recall the following notation: for any complex-valued function
f
let
f^{\mathbb{R}}
and
f^{i\mathbb{R}}
denote its real and imaginary parts respectively, and similarly for every
w\in\mathbb{C}^{k}
let
w^{\mathbb{R}}
and
w^{i\mathbb{R}}
be the real and imaginary parts of
w
.

The kernel
K_{1}^{\lambda}
is defined in
Eq.

3.21
as
K_{1}^{\lambda}=K_{1,\chi}^{\lambda}
, where we recall the expression
Eq.

3.13
here:

\displaystyle K_{1}^{\lambda}(x,v)

\displaystyle=c_{N}\lambda^{\frac{1}{4}(N^{\prime}+3N)}\int_{\hat{Z}}\int e^{i\lambda\Phi(z,\eta;x,v)}\chi_{\lambda}(\lambda\eta)a(z,x,\lambda\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=c_{N}\lambda^{\frac{1}{4}(N^{\prime}+3N)}\int_{\hat{Z}}\int e^{i\lambda\Phi(z,\eta;x,v)}(\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)+\chi^{\prime}_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta))a(z,x,\lambda\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z\,,

(3.28)

where individually
\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta),\chi^{\prime}_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)
are elements of a sequence of Ehrenpreis cut-offs relative to
(\Xi,\Xi^{\prime})
and
\left(\left\{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\leq R_{1}\right\},\left\{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\leq R_{1}/2\right\}\right)
respectively, of which each element in the sequence is independent of
\lambda
.

In order to deal with the part of
K_{1}^{\lambda}
arising from
\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}
(essentially by the method of stationary phase), we show

Proposition 3.14
.

For any small enough open
Z\ni\hat{z}
there exist small open
V\ni\hat{v}
,
X\ni\hat{x}
and
\Xi\ni\hat{\eta}
, and there is a
\vartheta_{0}>0
so that for all
0<\vartheta\leq\vartheta_{0}
, if the function
\tau
satisfies

\vartheta\lambda/2\leq\tau(\lambda)\leq\vartheta\lambda,

(3.29)

the following holds.

For every
\lambda\geq 1
large enough, and for
\sum_{k\geq 0}a_{k}=\underline{a}\in\mathrm{FS}_{\psi a}^{m}(\hat{Z}\times\hat{X})
elliptic and classical in
Z\times X\times\Xi
there is an elliptic classical analytic amplitude
\underline{a^{\sharp}_{v}}(x)
in the sense of
[
Sjö82
, § 1]
(or see
Definition

C.1
) so that if
a^{\sharp}_{v}(x;\lambda)
is any finite realization of
\underline{a^{\sharp}_{v}}(x)
in the sense of
[
Sjö82
, § 1]
(or
Definition

C.1
) and
a\in\mathrm{S}^{m}_{\psi a}(\hat{Z}\times\hat{X})
is any finite realization of
\underline{a}
(in the sense of
Definition

2.5
), then for some
\varepsilon>0
,

\lambda^{m-\frac{N+n}{2}}e^{i\lambda\Phi(\mathbf{z}(x,v),\bm{\eta}(x,v);x,v)}a^{\sharp}_{v}(x;\lambda)=\int_{Z}\int e^{i\lambda\Phi(z,\eta;x,v)}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)a(z,x,\lambda\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})\,.

(3.30)

Remark 3.15
.

The above proposition states that the action of taking finite realizations and applying stationary phase commute. Formally this means that

\lambda^{m-\frac{N+n}{2}}e^{i\lambda\Phi(\mathbf{z}(x,v),\bm{\eta}(x,v);x,v)}\underline{a_{v}^{\sharp}}(x)=\int_{Z}\int e^{i\lambda\Phi(z,\eta;x,v)}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)\sum_{k\geq 0}a_{k}(z,x,\lambda\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z\,.

Proof of
Proposition

3.14
.

Notice first that by assumption of classicality,
Eq.

2.3
, we know that there exists
\lambda_{0}\geq 1
so that for all
\lambda\geq\lambda_{0}
and all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
the amplitude
a_{k}(z,x,\lambda\eta)
is homogeneous of degree
m-k
in
\eta
near
(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})
. On the other hand, for all
\lambda\geq 1
we can write

a_{k}(z,x,\lambda\eta)=a_{k}(z,x,\lambda\lambda_{0}^{-1}\lambda_{0}\eta)=\lambda^{m-k}\lambda_{0}^{k-m}a_{k}(z,x,\lambda_{0}\eta)\,,

where the RHS is homogeneous of degree
m-k
in
\eta
near
(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})
. We may thus assume (by replacing
a_{k}(z,x,\eta)
by
\lambda_{0}^{k-m}a_{k}(z,x,\lambda_{0}\eta)
) that
a_{k}(z,x,\eta)
is homogeneous of degree
m-k
in
\eta
near
(\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta})
for
\lambda\geq 1
. By a similar argument, we may assume that
\lambda_{0}=1
in
Eq.

2.4
: that is,
a_{0}
is non-vanishing in
Z\times X\times\Xi
.

By
Remark

2.4
, if necessary contracting
Z,X,\Xi
about
\hat{z},\hat{x},\hat{\eta}
respectively, we conclude that each
a_{k}(z,x,\lambda\eta)
is real-analytic in
Z\times X\times\Xi
for
\lambda\geq 1
and has a holomorphic extension to a complex neighborhood thereof (independent of
k
) which we also denote by
a_{k}
.

In order to set up an application of classical analytic stationary phase (
Theorem

C.4
), we introduce
\tilde{a}_{k}((z,\eta),(x,v))\coloneqq a_{k}(z,x,\eta)
(which does not depend on
v
) and
\tilde{\Phi}((z,\eta),(x,v))\coloneqq\Phi(z,\eta;x,v)
. It is routine to check that
\underline{\tilde{a}}\coloneqq(\tilde{a}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a classical formal analytic amplitude according to
Definition

C.1
.

Since the statement of
Theorem

C.4
requires an Ehrenpreis cut-off in
(z,\eta)
and so far we only have the cut-off
\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)
, we proceed as follows. Taking
Z^{\prime}\subset Z
to be an open set with
\hat{z}\in Z^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{dist}(Z^{\prime},\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus Z)>0
, we introduce a sequence of Ehrenpreis cut-offs
\chi_{j,z}(z)
relative to
(Z,Z^{\prime})
and put

\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(z,\eta)\coloneqq\chi_{\tau(\lambda),z}(z)\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)\,,

which is an Ehrenpreis cut-off with respect to
(Z\times\Xi,Z^{\prime}\times\Xi^{\prime})
.

Let
\tilde{a}
be any finite realization of
\underline{\tilde{a}}
on a compact neighborhood of the support of
\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(z,\eta)
and let
a(z,x,\lambda\eta)
be a finite realization of
\sum_{k\geq 0}a_{k}(z,x,\lambda\eta)
in the sense of
Definition

2.5
. This means that for some
\varepsilon>0
,

\tilde{a}(z,x,\eta)=\sum_{k\leq\lambda R^{-1}}\lambda^{-k}\tilde{a}_{k}(z,x,\eta)+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})\,,\quad\text{and}\quad a(z,x,\lambda\eta)=\sum_{k\leq\lambda(R^{\prime})^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}a_{k}(z,x,\lambda\eta)+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert})\,.

Letting
r=\min\left\{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\colon\eta\in\mathrm{supp}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}\right\}>0
, and taking
R^{\prime},R>0
large enough and choosing
(R^{\prime})^{-1}r=R^{-1}
, we have

\sum_{k\leq\lambda(R^{\prime})^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}a_{k}(z,x,\lambda\eta)=\sum_{k\leq\lambda R^{-1}}a_{k}(z,x,\lambda\eta)+\sum_{\lambda R^{-1}<k\leq\lambda(R^{\prime})^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}a_{k}(z,x,\lambda\eta)\,,

and due to
\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}
being supported compactly and away from
0
and the indices
k
runs over, assuming
C_{a}(R^{\prime})^{-1}\leq e^{-1}
by taking
R^{\prime}
large,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)\sum_{\lambda R^{-1}<k\leq\lambda(R^{\prime})^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}a_{k}(z,x,\lambda\eta)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq M_{a}\left\lvert\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)\right\rvert\sum_{\lambda R^{-1}<k\leq\lambda(R^{\prime})^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}C_{a}^{k}k!(\lambda\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert)^{m-k}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lvert\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)\right\rvert\lambda^{m}\sum_{\lambda R^{-1}<k\leq\lambda(R^{\prime})^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\left(\frac{C_{a}k}{\lambda\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\right)^{k}\leq C\lambda^{m+1}(R^{\prime})^{-1}e^{-\lambda R^{-1}}\,,

so that using the homogeneity of
a_{k}
for
\lambda\geq 1
guaranteed above,

\displaystyle\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)a(z,x,\lambda\eta)

\displaystyle=\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)\left(\sum_{k\leq\lambda R^{-1}}a_{k}(z,x,\lambda\eta)+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})\right)=\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)\left(\sum_{k\leq\lambda R^{-1}}\lambda^{m-k}a_{k}(z,x,\eta)+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})\right)

\displaystyle=\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)\lambda^{m}\tilde{a}((z,\eta),(x,v))+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})\,.

Of course, the above statement merely says that on the support of
\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}
, the two different notions of finite realization agree modulo an exponentially decaying term.

In particular,

\displaystyle\lambda^{m}\int

\displaystyle e^{i\lambda\tilde{\Phi}((z,\eta),(x,v))}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}((z,\eta))\tilde{a}((z,\eta),(x,v))\mathrm{d}(z,\eta)

(3.31)

\displaystyle=\int_{Z}\int e^{i\lambda\Phi(z,\eta;x,v)}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)a(z,x,\lambda\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z+\tilde{R}(x,v;\lambda)+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})\,,

where

\tilde{R}(x,v;\lambda)=\int_{Z}\int e^{i\lambda\Phi(z,\eta;x,v)}(1-\chi_{\tau(\lambda),z}(z))\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)a(z,x,\lambda\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z\,.

Since
1-\chi_{\tau(\lambda),z}(z)
is identically
0
in a neighborhood of
z=\hat{z}
, we have that for
(z,\eta)\in\mathrm{supp}(1-\chi_{\tau(\lambda),z}(z))\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)
, for some
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
,

\Phi^{i\mathbb{R}}(z,\eta;x,v)\geq\frac{1}{2}\left\lvert z-v_{1}\right\rvert^{2}\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}>0

if
V
is chosen small enough (depending on
Z
), since
\hat{v}_{2}=\hat{z}
. Thus, because
\left\lvert a(z,x,\lambda\eta)\right\rvert\leq C\lambda^{m}
on
Z\times X\times\Xi
,

\tilde{R}(x,v;\lambda)=\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon^{\prime}\lambda})\,.

Applying the classical analytic method of stationary phase,
Theorem

C.4
, to the LHS of
Eq.

3.31
completes the proof.
∎

In order to finalize the estimation of
K_{1}^{\lambda}
, ie. the part corresponding to
\chi^{\prime}_{\tau(\lambda)}
, we prove

Proposition 3.16
.

For any small enough open
V\ni\hat{v},X\ni\hat{x}
there are
\varepsilon,\vartheta_{0}>0
so that for all
0<\vartheta\leq\vartheta_{0}
if the function
\tau
satisfies

\vartheta\lambda/2\leq\tau(\lambda)\leq\vartheta\lambda\,,

(3.32)

then, uniformly in
X\times V
,

\int_{\hat{Z}}\int e^{i\lambda\Phi(z,\eta;x,v)}\chi^{\prime}_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)a(z,x,\lambda\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z=\mathcal{O}(e^{-\frac{1}{2}\vartheta\lambda}+e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})\,.

Proof.

Let open
Z\subset\hat{Z}
with
\hat{z}\in Z
which will be chosen small throughout this proof, and let
(\psi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of Ehrenpreis cut-offs relative to
(\hat{Z},Z)
.

Note now that for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
, on the support of
1-\psi_{j}
,
\Phi
satisfies
\Phi^{i\mathbb{R}}\geq\varepsilon>0
for some
\varepsilon>0
, so that

\int_{\hat{Z}}\int e^{i\lambda\Phi(z,\eta;x,v)}\chi^{\prime}_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)a(z,x,\lambda\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z=\int_{\hat{Z}}\int e^{i\lambda\Phi(z,\eta;x,v)}\chi^{\prime}_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)\psi_{\tau(\lambda)}(z)a(z,x,\lambda\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})\,.

Furthermore, on the support of
\chi^{\prime}_{\tau(\lambda)}
, we have
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\leq R_{1}
so that we may apply the result of
Eq.

3.16
. While
\varphi
is not defined at
\eta=0
, notice that
\lim_{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\to 0}\Phi_{z}(z,\eta;x,v)=-v_{2}+i(z-v_{1})
, which is non-zero for small enough
Z\ni\hat{z},V\ni\hat{v}
. Thus,
\kappa=\Phi
satisfies assumption

κ

2.
on
\hat{Z}\times\{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\leq R_{1}\}
and we may apply partial integration
\tau(\lambda)
times with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{\Phi,2}
from
Eq.

2.12
to the first term on the RHS above,
so
Eq.

2.15
allows us to conclude

\left\lvert(-i\lambda)^{-\tau(\lambda)}\int_{\hat{Z}}\int e^{i\lambda\Phi(z,\eta;x,v)}\mathcal{L}_{\Phi,2}^{\tau(\lambda)}\left(\chi^{\prime}_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)\psi_{\tau(\lambda)}(z)a(z,x,\lambda\eta)\right)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z\right\rvert\leq\lambda^{m}M_{a}(C_{a}\mathcal{L}_{\Phi}\tau(\lambda)/\lambda)^{\tau(\lambda)}\,,

so that if we take
\vartheta_{0}=(eC_{a}\mathcal{L}_{\Phi})^{-1}
and
\tau(\lambda)
according to the statement, the proof is complete.
∎

It is evident from
Proposition

3.14
that the function

\Phi(\mathbf{z}(x,v),\bm{\eta}(x,v);x,v)

with
\mathbf{z},\bm{\eta}
from
Lemma

3.11
, plays a significant role, so that we devote a section to its study.

3.8
The Phase
\psi

We adopt the notation of the previous two sections, in particular from
Lemma

3.11
. Define the holomorphic function
\psi\colon X^{\mathbb{C}}\times V^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
via

\psi(x,v)\coloneqq\Phi(\mathbf{z}(x,v),\bm{\eta}(x,v),x,v)=-\mathbf{z}(x,v)\cdot v_{2}+\frac{i}{2}(\mathbf{z}(x,v)-v_{1})^{2}\,,

(3.33)

where we used the fact that
\varphi(\mathbf{z}(x,v),x,\bm{\eta}(x,v))=\varphi_{\eta}(\mathbf{z}(x,v),x,\bm{\eta}(x,v))\cdot\bm{\eta}(x,v)=0
since
\varphi
is homogeneous of degree
1
.

In this section let
\Lambda\subset\hat{\Lambda}
be the set defined in
Lemma

3.7
. We freely use the notation
\chi
from
Lemma

3.7
and
\pi
(=
\pi_{z}
) for the canonical projection. We reiterate an important consequence of the definition of
\chi
and
Lemma

3.11
: let open
V\ni\hat{v}
be small enough, for any
v\in\pi_{L}(\Lambda)\cap V
,

(u_{1},u_{2})=\chi(v)\iff(v_{1},v_{2};u_{1},u_{2})\in\Lambda\iff(v_{2},u_{2};0)=(\varphi_{z},-\varphi_{x};\varphi_{\eta})(v_{1},u_{1},\bm{\eta}(u_{1},v))\,.

(3.34)

Lemma 3.17
.

For all
v\in\pi_{L}(\Lambda)\cap V
, provided open
V\ni\hat{v}
small enough, we have
\mathbf{z}(\pi(\chi(v)),v)=v_{1}
and
\bm{\eta}(\pi(\chi(v)),v)
is real.

Proof.

For all
v\in V\cap\pi_{L}(\Lambda)
, we have
(v,\chi(v))\in\Lambda
, and so, denoting
x^{\ast}\coloneqq\pi(\chi(v))
, there exists
\eta^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
so that

(v,\chi(v))=(v_{1},\varphi_{z}(v_{1},x^{\ast},\eta^{\ast});x^{\ast},-\varphi_{x}(v_{1},x^{\ast},\eta^{\ast}))\,,

which implies that
\nabla_{z,\eta}\Phi(v_{1},\eta^{\ast};x^{\ast},v)=0
. Furthermore, as a consequence of
Lemma

3.11
we then know that
\nabla_{z,\eta}\Phi(\mathbf{z}(x^{\ast},v),\bm{\eta}(x^{\ast},v);x^{\ast},v)=0
and this critical point is unique for
v
ranging in
V\ni\hat{v}
small enough, and thus

\mathbf{z}(\pi(\chi(v)),v)=v_{1}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{\eta}(\pi(\chi(v)),v)=\eta^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}\,,

which concludes the proof.
∎

The proofs in this section are based on those given in
[
MST23
, § 5.6]
.

Lemma 3.18

(
[
MST23
, Lem. 5.13]
)
.

For open
X\ni\hat{x},V\ni\hat{v}
small enough, there is
C_{0}>0
so that

\left\lvert x-\pi(\chi(v))\right\rvert+\left\lvert\bm{\eta}(x,v)-\bm{\eta}(\pi(\chi(v)),v)\right\rvert\leq C_{0}\left\lvert\mathbf{z}(x,v)-v_{1}\right\rvert

for all
x\in X
and
v\in V\cap\pi_{L}(\Lambda)
.

Proof.

Introduce the shorthand
\eta^{\ast}\coloneqq\bm{\eta}(\pi(\chi(v)),v),x^{\ast}\coloneqq\pi(\chi(v))
.

Notice that by the defining properties of
\mathbf{z},\bm{\eta}
(see
Eq.

3.18
), we have

\begin{pmatrix}\Phi_{z}\\
\Phi_{\eta}\end{pmatrix}(\mathbf{z}(x,v),\bm{\eta}(x,v),x,v)=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad\text{so that}\quad\begin{pmatrix}\varphi_{z}\\
\varphi_{\eta}\end{pmatrix}(\mathbf{z}(x,v),x,\bm{\eta}(x,v))-\begin{pmatrix}v_{2}\\
0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-i(\mathbf{z}(x,v)-v_{1})\\
0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(3.35)

By the properties of
\chi
(see
Eq.

3.34
) we have
\begin{pmatrix}\varphi_{z}\\
\varphi_{\eta}\end{pmatrix}(v_{1},x^{\ast},\eta^{\ast})=\begin{pmatrix}v_{2}\\
0\end{pmatrix}
and inserting this into the equation on the right side of
Eq.

3.35
, we see

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\varphi_{z}\\
\varphi_{\eta}\end{pmatrix}(\mathbf{z}(x,v),x,\bm{\eta}(x,v))-\begin{pmatrix}\varphi_{z}\\
\varphi_{\eta}\end{pmatrix}(v_{1},x^{\ast},\eta^{\ast})=\begin{pmatrix}-i(\mathbf{z}(x,v)-v_{1})\\
0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(3.36)

By Taylor’s expansion we may also write for some matrix
R
,

\begin{pmatrix}\varphi_{z}\\
\varphi_{\eta}\end{pmatrix}(\mathbf{z}(x,v),x,\bm{\eta}(x,v))=\begin{pmatrix}\varphi_{z}\\
\varphi_{\eta}\end{pmatrix}(v_{1},x,\bm{\eta}(x,v))+R(\mathbf{z}(x,v)-v_{1})

so that we can conclude from
Eq.

3.36
that

\begin{pmatrix}\varphi_{z}\\
\varphi_{\eta}\end{pmatrix}(v_{1},x,\bm{\eta}(x,v))-\begin{pmatrix}\varphi_{z}\\
\varphi_{\eta}\end{pmatrix}(v_{1},x^{\ast},\eta^{\ast})=R^{\prime}(\mathbf{z}(x,v)-v_{1})\,,

for some matrix
R^{\prime}
.

Focusing on the LHS of the equation abvove, by Taylor’s expansion,

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\varphi_{z}\\
\varphi_{\eta}\end{pmatrix}(v_{1},x,\bm{\eta}(x,v))-\begin{pmatrix}\varphi_{z}\\
\varphi_{\eta}\end{pmatrix}(v_{1},x^{\ast},\eta^{\ast})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{x}\varphi_{z}&\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{z}\\
\partial_{x}\varphi_{\eta}&\partial_{\eta}\varphi_{\eta}\end{pmatrix}(v_{1},x^{\ast},\eta^{\ast})\begin{pmatrix}x-x^{\ast}\\
\bm{\eta}(x,v)-\eta^{\ast}\end{pmatrix}

(3.37)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}(\left\lvert x-x^{\ast}\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\bm{\eta}(x,v)-\eta^{\ast}\right\rvert^{2})\,.

According to
Eq.

2.7
, we know that the matrix on the RHS of
Eq.

3.37
has a left-inverse we will call
B
. We may therefore conclude that

\begin{pmatrix}x-x^{\ast}\\
\bm{\eta}(x,v)-\eta^{\ast}\end{pmatrix}=BR^{\prime}(\mathbf{z}(x,v)-v_{1})+\mathcal{O}(\left\lvert x-x^{\ast}\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\bm{\eta}(x,v)-\eta^{\ast}\right\rvert^{2})\,.

Choosing
(X,V)\ni(\hat{x},\hat{v})
small enough the proof is complete.
∎

For any complex-valued function
f
let
f^{\mathbb{R}}
and
f^{i\mathbb{R}}
denote its real and imaginary parts respectively, and similarly for every
w\in\mathbb{C}^{k}
let
w^{\mathbb{R}}
and
w^{i\mathbb{R}}
be the real and imaginary parts of
w
.

Lemma 3.19

(
[
MST23
, Lem. 5.14]
)
.

There is
C_{1}>0
so that

\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\bm{\eta}^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)\right\rvert^{2}\leq C_{1}\psi^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)

(3.38)

for all
(x,v)\in X\times V
for
X\times V
sufficiently small about
(\hat{x},\hat{v})
.

Proof.

Looking closely at the proof of
[
MST23
, Lem. 5.14]
, one sees that that
Eq.

3.38
is proven there already. For completeness, however, we reiterate some steps of this proof. It is shown in the proof of
[
MST23
, Lem. 5.14]
(via a Taylor expansion of
\Phi
, which has non-negative imaginary part on its real domain) that

\displaystyle{}\psi^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)\geq\left\lvert\mathrm{Im}(\mathbf{z},\bm{\eta})(x,v)\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\cdot\sup_{\left\lvert s\right\rvert\leq 1}-\mathrm{Im}\left(\sum_{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert=2}\frac{\left(\partial_{(z,\eta)}^{\alpha}\Phi\right)((\mathbf{z},\bm{\eta})(x,v);x,v)}{\alpha!}\left(s+i\frac{\mathrm{Im}(\mathbf{z},\bm{\eta})(x,v)}{\left\lvert\mathrm{Im}(\mathbf{z},\bm{\eta})(x,v)\right\rvert}\right)^{\alpha}+\mathcal{O}(\left\lvert\mathrm{Im}(\mathbf{z},\bm{\eta})(x,v)\right\rvert)\right)\,.

Applying
[
Hör03
, Lem. 7.7.9]
with the fact that
\partial_{(z,\eta)}^{2}\Phi(\hat{z},\hat{\eta};\hat{x},\hat{v})
is a symmetric non-degenerate matrix with positive semi-definite imaginary part due to
Lemma

3.11
,

\sup_{\left\lvert s\right\rvert\leq 1}-\mathrm{Im}\left(\left(s+i\omega\right)\cdot\left(\partial_{(z,\eta)}^{2}\Phi\right)(\hat{z},\hat{\eta};\hat{x},\hat{v})\left(s+i\omega\right)\right)\geq C>0

uniformly for all
\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{N+n}
,
\left\lvert\omega\right\rvert=1
.

Using continuity and compactness we may then conclude that

\psi^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)\geq\left\lvert\mathrm{Im}(\mathbf{z},\bm{\eta})(x,v)\right\rvert^{2}(c+\mathcal{O}(\left\lvert\mathrm{Im}(\mathbf{z},\bm{\eta})(x,v)\right\rvert))

for some
c>0
.

Now
\mathrm{Im}(\mathbf{z},\bm{\eta})(\hat{x},\hat{v})=\mathrm{Im}(\hat{z},\hat{\eta})=0
, so that choosing
X\times V\ni(\hat{x},\hat{v})
small enough, the proof is complete.
∎

Lemma 3.20

(
[
MST23
, Lem. 5.16]
)
.

Let open
(X,V)\ni(\hat{x},\hat{v})
be small enough. There exists
C_{2}>0
so that for all
\varepsilon>0
, all
x\in X
and all
v\in\pi_{L}(\Lambda)\cap V
,

-\mathrm{Im}(\mathbf{z}(x,v)\cdot v_{2})\geq-C_{2}\varepsilon^{-1}\mathrm{Im}(\psi(x,v))-\varepsilon\left\lvert x-\pi(\chi(v))\right\rvert^{2}-C_{2}\varepsilon^{-1}\left\lvert x-\pi(\chi(v))\right\rvert^{3}\,.

(3.39)

Proof.

Notice that
\pi(\chi(v)),\mathbf{z}(\pi(\chi(v)),v)
and
\bm{\eta}(\pi(\chi(v)),v)
are real since they originate from the canonical relation.

Now, by the defining property of
\mathbf{z},\bm{\eta}
(see
Eq.

3.18
), we have

\varphi(\mathbf{z}(x,v),x,\bm{\eta}(x,v))=\varphi_{\eta}(\mathbf{z}(x,v),x,\bm{\eta}(x,v))\cdot\bm{\eta}(x,v)=0\,,

and in particular

\varphi^{i\mathbb{R}}(\mathbf{z}(x,v),x,\bm{\eta}(x,v))=0\,.

(3.40)

Let us remark here that at real-valued inputs
\varphi
is real-valued and so are all of its real derivates.

We introduce the shorthand

w(x,v)\coloneqq(\mathbf{z}(x,v),x,\bm{\eta}(x,v))\,,

so that

w(\pi(\chi(v)),v)=(\mathbf{z}^{\mathbb{R}}(\pi(\chi(v)),v),\pi(\chi(v)),\bm{\eta}^{\mathbb{R}}(\pi(\chi(v)),v))\,.

Let us differentiate
Eq.

3.40
with respect to
x
to get

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\varphi^{i\mathbb{R}}_{x}(w(x,v))+(\mathbf{z}^{\mathbb{R}}(x,v))_{x}^{T}\varphi^{i\mathbb{R}}_{z^{\mathbb{R}}}(w(x,v))

(3.41)

\displaystyle+(\mathbf{z}^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v))_{x}^{T}\varphi^{i\mathbb{R}}_{z^{i\mathbb{R}}}(w(x,v))+(\bm{\eta}^{\mathbb{R}}(x,v))_{x}^{T}\varphi^{i\mathbb{R}}_{\eta^{\mathbb{R}}}(w(x,v))

\displaystyle+(\bm{\eta}^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v))^{T}_{x}\varphi^{i\mathbb{R}}_{\eta^{i\mathbb{R}}}(w(x,v))\,.

Note that if we evaluate
Eq.

3.41
at
x=\pi(\chi(v))
then all terms where
\varphi^{i\mathbb{R}}
is not differentiated with respect to an imaginary variable evaluate to zero. That is to say that

0=(\mathbf{z}^{i\mathbb{R}}_{x}(\pi(\chi(v)),v))^{T}\varphi^{i\mathbb{R}}_{z^{i\mathbb{R}}}(w(\pi(\chi(v)),v))+(\bm{\eta}^{i\mathbb{R}}_{x}(\pi(\chi(v)),v))^{T}\varphi^{i\mathbb{R}}_{\eta^{i\mathbb{R}}}(w(\pi(\chi(v)),v))\,.

Due to the Cauchy-Riemann equations,
\varphi^{i\mathbb{R}}_{\eta^{i\mathbb{R}}}=\varphi^{\mathbb{R}}_{\eta^{\mathbb{R}}}
, which is equal to
0
at
(z,x,\eta)=(\mathbf{z}(x,v),x,\bm{\eta}(x,v))=w(x,v)
for
x=\pi(\chi(v))
by the defining property of
\mathbf{z},\bm{\eta}
.

Additionally,
\varphi^{i\mathbb{R}}_{z^{i\mathbb{R}}}(w(\pi(\chi(v)),v))=\varphi^{\mathbb{R}}_{z^{\mathbb{R}}}(w(\pi(\chi(v)),v))=v_{2}
, so that we may conclude that

0=(\mathbf{z}^{i\mathbb{R}}_{x}(\pi(\chi(v)),v))^{T}\cdot v_{2}=\partial_{x}\left(\mathbf{z}^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)^{T}\cdot v_{2}\right)|_{x=\pi(\chi(v))}\,.

(3.42)

Furthermore, if we differentiate
Eq.

3.41
with respect to
x
and remember that the only terms that do not vanish at
x=\pi(\chi(v))
are those with at least one imaginary derivative in
\varphi^{i\mathbb{R}}
, we see that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{z}^{i\mathbb{R}}_{x}(\pi(\chi(v)),v))^{T}A_{1}+B_{1}(\mathbf{z}^{i\mathbb{R}}_{x}(\pi(\chi(v)),v))+(\bm{\eta}_{x}^{i\mathbb{R}}(\pi(\chi(v)),v))^{T}A_{2}+B_{2}(\bm{\eta}_{x}^{i\mathbb{R}}(\pi(\chi(v)),v))

\displaystyle+(\mathbf{z}^{i\mathbb{R}}_{xx}(\pi(\chi(v)),v))\varphi^{i\mathbb{R}}_{z^{i\mathbb{R}}}(w(\pi(\chi(v)),v))+(\bm{\eta}_{xx}^{i\mathbb{R}})\varphi^{i\mathbb{R}}_{\eta^{i\mathbb{R}}}(w(\pi(\chi(v)),v))\,.

For some matrices
A_{1},A_{2},B_{1},B_{2}
depending on
v
. Again due to the Cauchy-Riemann equations, we have
\varphi^{i\mathbb{R}}_{\eta^{i\mathbb{R}}}(w(\pi(\chi(v)),v))=\varphi^{\mathbb{R}}_{\eta^{\mathbb{R}}}(w(\pi(\chi(v)),v))=0
and
v_{2}=\varphi^{i\mathbb{R}}_{z^{i\mathbb{R}}}(w(\pi(\chi(v)),v))
, so that we may conclude

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle-\partial_{xx}^{2}(\mathbf{z}^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)\cdot v_{2})|_{x=\pi(\chi(v))}

(3.43)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{z}_{x}^{i\mathbb{R}}(\pi(\chi(v)),v))^{T}A_{1}+B_{1}(\mathbf{z}_{x}^{i\mathbb{R}}(\pi(\chi(v)),v))+(\bm{\eta}_{x}^{i\mathbb{R}}(\pi(\chi(v)),v))^{T}A_{2}+B_{2}(\bm{\eta}_{x}^{i\mathbb{R}}(\pi(\chi(v)),v))\,.

Now we Taylor expand
\mathbf{z}^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)\cdot v_{2}
in
x
around
\pi(\chi(v))
(of which the zero-th and first order terms at
x=\pi(\chi(v))
vanish due to
Eq.

3.42
) using
Eq.

3.43
to get for any
\varepsilon>0

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)\cdot v_{2}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\langle(x-\pi(\chi(v))),\left((\mathbf{z}_{x}^{i\mathbb{R}}(\pi(\chi(v)),v))^{T}A_{1}+B_{1}(\mathbf{z}_{x}^{i\mathbb{R}}(\pi(\chi(v)),v))\right)(x-\pi(\chi(v)))\rangle\right\rvert

\displaystyle+\left\lvert\langle(x-\pi(\chi(v))),\left((\bm{\eta}_{x}^{i\mathbb{R}}(\pi(\chi(v)),v))^{T}A_{2}+B_{2}(\bm{\eta}_{x}^{i\mathbb{R}}(\pi(\chi(v)),v))\right)(x-\pi(\chi(v)))\rangle\right\rvert

\displaystyle+C\left\lvert x-\pi(\chi(v))\right\rvert^{3}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{-1}\left\lvert(\mathbf{z}_{x}^{i\mathbb{R}}(\pi(\chi(v)),v))(x-\pi(\chi(v)))\right\rvert^{2}+C\varepsilon^{-1}\left\lvert(\bm{\eta}_{x}^{i\mathbb{R}}(\pi(\chi(v)),v))(x-\pi(\chi(v)))\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon\left\lvert x-\pi(\chi(v))\right\rvert^{2}+C\left\lvert x-\pi(\chi(v))\right\rvert^{3}\,.

(3.44)

Note again that
\mathbf{z}^{i\mathbb{R}}(\pi(\chi(v)),v)=\bm{\eta}^{i{\mathbb{R}}}(\pi(\chi(v)),v)=0
so that by Taylor’s expansion

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{z}_{x}^{i\mathbb{R}})^{T}(\pi(\chi(v)),v)(x-\pi(\chi(v)))+\mathcal{O}((x-\pi(\chi(v)))^{2})

\displaystyle\bm{\eta}^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)

\displaystyle=(\bm{\eta}_{x}^{i\mathbb{R}})^{T}(\pi(\chi(v)),v)(x-\pi(\chi(v)))+\mathcal{O}((x-\pi(\chi(v)))^{2})\,,

so that we may conclude from
Eq.

3.44
that

\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)\cdot v_{2}\right\rvert\leq C\varepsilon^{-1}\left(\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\bm{\eta}^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)\right\rvert^{2}\right)+\varepsilon\left\lvert x-\pi(\chi(v))\right\rvert^{2}+C\varepsilon^{-1}\left\lvert x-\pi(\chi(v))\right\rvert^{3}\,.

(3.45)

Now, using
Lemma

3.19
, we have

\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\bm{\eta}^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)\right\rvert^{2}\leq C\mathrm{Im}(\psi(x,v))

so that from
Eq.

3.45
we may conclude that

\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)\cdot v_{2}\right\rvert\leq C\varepsilon^{-1}\mathrm{Im}(\psi(x,v))+\varepsilon\left\lvert x-\pi(\chi(v))\right\rvert^{2}+C\varepsilon^{-1}\left\lvert x-\pi(\chi(v))\right\rvert^{3}\,.

∎

Corollary 3.21

(
[
MST23
, Cor. 5.17]
)
.

Let open
(X,V)\ni(\hat{x},\hat{v})
be small enough. For some
C>0
, and all
x\in X
and all
v\in\pi_{L}(\Lambda)\cap V
,

\psi^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)\geq C\left\lvert x-\pi(\chi(v))\right\rvert^{2}

Proof.

Recall that
\pi(\chi(v))
and
\bm{\eta}(\pi(\chi(v)),v)
are real from
Lemma

3.17
.

By
Lemma

3.18
we have

C_{0}^{-1}\left\lvert x-\pi(\chi(v))\right\rvert^{2}\leq\left\lvert\mathbf{z}(x,v)-v_{1}\right\rvert^{2}=\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{\mathbb{R}}(x,v)-v_{1}\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)\right\rvert^{2}=\mathrm{Re}((\mathbf{z}(x,v)-v_{1})^{2})+2\left\lvert\mathbf{z}^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)\right\rvert^{2}

where we used that
v_{1}
is real. Applying
Lemma

3.19
we get

\mathrm{Re}((\mathbf{z}(x,v)-v_{1})^{2})\geq C_{0}^{-1}\left\lvert x-\pi(\chi(v))\right\rvert^{2}-2C_{1}\psi^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)\,.

Together with the definition of
\psi
, and using
Eq.

3.39
, we thus conclude

\displaystyle\psi^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{z}^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)\cdot v_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Re}((\mathbf{z}(x,v)-v_{1})^{2})

\displaystyle\geq(C_{0}^{-1}/2-C_{2}\varepsilon)\left\lvert x-\pi(\chi(v))\right\rvert^{2}-(C_{2}\varepsilon^{-1}+C_{1})\psi^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)-C_{2}\varepsilon^{-1}\left\lvert x-\pi(\chi(v))\right\rvert^{3}\,.

For some
\delta>0
, take first
\varepsilon>0
so that
C_{0}^{-1}/2-C_{2}\varepsilon>2\delta
and
X,V
small so that
C_{2}\varepsilon^{-1}\left\lvert x-\pi(\chi(v))\right\rvert\leq\delta
, so that we can conclude

(1+C_{2}\varepsilon^{-1}+C_{1})\psi^{i\mathbb{R}}(x,v)\geq\delta\left\lvert x-\pi(\chi(v))\right\rvert^{2}\,,

which completes the proof.
∎

3.9
Proofs of Propositions
3.6
and
3.8

We now have all necessary results for the

Proof of
Proposition

3.6
.

Let open
Z\subset\hat{Z}
with
\hat{z}\in Z
be small enough and choose
X\ni\hat{x},V\ni\hat{v}
open, small enough depending on
Z
for the results of
Proposition

3.14
to hold.

Writing out
K_{1}^{\lambda}
again from
Eq.

3.28
,

K_{1}^{\lambda}(x,v)=c_{N}\lambda^{\frac{1}{4}(N^{\prime}+3N)}\int_{\hat{Z}}\int e^{i\lambda\Phi(z,\eta;x,v)}(\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)+\chi^{\prime}_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta))a(z,x,\lambda\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z\,,

(3.46)

and we know that

\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\},z\in\hat{Z}\setminus Z\implies\Phi^{i\mathbb{R}}(z,\eta;x,v)\geq\varepsilon

for some
\varepsilon>0
, so that modulo an exponentially decaying term we can replace the domain of integration
\hat{Z}
in
Eq.

3.46
by any open
Z\ni\hat{z}
.

Notice that
Propositions

3.14
,
3.16
and
3.12
depend on a choice of definition of
\tau(\lambda)
. However, each of these results only enforces an upper bound on
\tau(\lambda)
: see
Eqs.

3.22
,
3.29
and
3.32
(the constant lower bound on
\tau(\lambda)
from
Proposition

3.12
can be ignored by taking
\lambda
large enough), so that we can choose to define
\tau
satisfying these three conditions simultaneously, which we do.

Replacing the domain of integration
\hat{Z}
with
Z
in the RHS of
Eq.

3.46
,
Proposition

3.16
shows exponential decay of the term corresponding to
\chi^{\prime}_{\tau(\lambda)}
in the RHS of
Eq.

3.46
, so that
Proposition

3.14
then applies to the term on the RHS of
Eq.

3.46
corresponding to
\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}
to give
Eq.

3.6
where we take
b(x,v;\lambda)=\underline{a^{\sharp}_{v}}(x)
, defined prior to
Eq.

3.30
and
\psi(x,v)=\Phi(\mathbf{z}(x,v),\bm{\eta}(x,v);x,v)
.

Finally,
Eq.

3.7
is just
Proposition

3.12
.

Notice that the proofs of
Propositions

3.12
and
3.16
do not rely on a specific choice of finite realization
a
of
\underline{a}
, and the statement of
Proposition

3.14
is inherently finite-realization-agnostic. This completes the proof.
∎

As we have already studied the function
\psi(x,v)=\Phi(\mathbf{z}(x,v),\bm{\eta}(x,v);x,v)
in
Section

3.8
, we give the

Proof of
Proposition

3.8
.

According to the proof of
Proposition

3.6
, we know that the function
\psi
in the statement of
Proposition

3.8
is exactly the function
\psi
defined in
Eq.

3.33
, so that we may use the results of
Section

3.8
to prove this statement.

For
v\in\pi_{L}(\Lambda)\cap V
real, by
Lemma

3.17
and the definition of
\psi
from
Eq.

3.33
we see property
1
. By analyticity, it must be true for nearby complex
v
too.

Extending to a complex neighborhood by analyticity, it suffices to show property 2. for real
v
: take
(x,v)\in X\times V
and recall that by homogeneity,
\varphi(\mathbf{z}(x,v),x,\bm{\eta}(x,v))=0
, and differentiating this expression with respect to
x
, we see that

\varphi_{z}(\mathbf{z}(x,v),x,\bm{\eta}(x,v))\mathbf{z}_{x}(x,v)+\varphi_{x}(\mathbf{z}(x,v),x,\bm{\eta}(x,v))+\varphi_{\eta}(\mathbf{z}(x,v),x,\bm{\eta}(x,v))\bm{\eta}_{x}(x,v)=0\,,

(3.47)

of which the third term on the LHS vanishes.

By the definition of
\chi
and the fact that
\varphi
is associated to the canonical relation (see
Eq.

3.34
),

(v_{1},v_{2};u_{1},u_{2})=(v,\chi(v))=\left(v_{1},\varphi_{z}\left(v_{1},\pi(\chi(v)),\bm{\eta}(\pi(\chi(v)),v)\right);\pi(\chi(v)),-\varphi_{x}\left(v_{1},\pi(\chi(v)),\bm{\eta}(\pi(\chi(v)),v)\right)\right),

so that
Eq.

3.47
evaluated at
x=\pi(\chi(v))
becomes

\mathbf{z}_{x}(x,v)\cdot v_{2}|_{x=\pi(\chi(v))}=u_{2}\,,

which, together with the definition of
\psi
, see
Eq.

3.33
, and the fact
\mathbf{z}(\pi(\chi(v)),v)=v_{1}
implies

\psi_{x}(\pi(\chi(v)),v)=-u_{2}\,.

Finally, property
3
is a consequence of
Corollary

3.21
.
∎

4
The Analytic Hadamard-Hörmander Parametrix

Throughout this section we fix an open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
, a real positive definite matrix of analytic functions
(g^{jk})_{j,k=1}^{N}
in
\Omega
, and analytic functions
b^{j}\in C^{\omega}(\Omega),j\in\{1,\dots,N\},c\in C^{\omega}(\Omega)
. We fix the differential operator

P(x,D)=-\sum\partial_{j}(g^{jk}\partial_{k})+\sum b^{j}\partial_{j}+c\,,

and study the equation

(\partial_{t}^{2}+P(x,D))u(t,x)=f(t,x)\,,\qquad\mathrm{supp}u\subset\{t\geq 0\}\,,

(4.1)

where we remark that
\partial_{t}^{2}+P(x,D)
is a strictly hyperbolic differential operator. We would like to describe the solution operator of
Eq.

4.1
, ie. the operator mapping
f
to
u
in
Eq.

4.1
.

The first construction of this solution operator goes back to
[
Had32
]
and later
[
Rie49
]
. For the special case we investigate in
Section

5
, the linear wave equation with non-constant coefficients, one may find a construction of the solution operator in modern language in
[
Bér77
,
Fri75
,
Gar64
,
BGP07
]
.

The solution operator to this (or similar) differential equations have already been constructed in the analytic setting. In fact,
[
Had32
]
states a result in the analytic setting, and we refer also to
[
Zel12
,
Leb18
,
GLS96
,
Ste14
,
Zel17
]
in more contemporary language. Nevertheless, we shall need our solution operator to be of a specific form so that we must revisit this construction.

In the smooth setting, one can use energy methods to show existence of solutions without an explicit representation of the solution operator, see for example
[
Hör07
, § 23]
.

In order to obtain an explicit solution operator, one can also use the method of geometric optics, called the WKB method in the semiclassical setting, see
[
GS94
,
Dui11
,
Tay81
,
DH94
,
Sjö82
,
Rau12
,
Tre22
,
GS77
,
Lax57
]
, and in particular
[
Tre22
, Prop. 23.2.7]
for an adapatation to the analytic setting. This method constructs local solutions in the form of FIOs which can, in the smooth setting, be patched together into global FIOs using the composition of smooth FIO (see
[
Tay81
, § VIII.5]
or the proof of
[
Hör07
, Thm. 23.2.4]
for arguments as to this ‘patching together’).

As described at the beginning of
Section

2
, we are restricted by looking for an explicit global solution which would, in general, disqualify the constructions mentioned above. In the application in
Section

5
we will assume that the manifold in question is simple, thus guaranteeing the existence of global normal coordinates.
We will rely on a citation that guarantees the existence of solutions and subsequently make use of the Hadamard parametrix solution to obtain an as-explicit-as-possible representation of the solution operator in the case of analytic coefficients.

We remark that we may consider
g
as giving rise to a metric on
\Omega
, so that we may consider
(\Omega,g)
a Riemannian manifold. For any
y\in\Omega
we may then define the injectivity radius
\mathrm{inj}_{g}(y)
of normal geodesic coordinates centered at
y
in that geometry. See
[
Lee18
]
for the definition and properties of normal coordinates and the injectivity radius.

Let us introduce the concept of the domain of dependence. Let
T>0
and
X\subset\Omega
open. We shall consider bicharacteristic strips of the Hamiltonian vector field associated to the principal symbol of
\partial_{t}^{2}+P(x,D)
, see for example
[
Hör03
, § 6.4]
for a defintion (or
[
Hör71b
, § 7]
). We call the projection onto the base of a bicharacteristic strip a
bicharacteristic curve
. We let
D(T,X)\subset(-T,T)\times X
be the set of points so that all bicharacteristic curves (remaining inside
\mathbb{R}\times X
) with initial point in
D(T,X)
intersect
(-T,0)\times X
(flowing backwards or forwards in time). See also the comments before
[
Dui11
, Thm. 5.1.6]
or
[
Fri75
, Def. 4.2.1]
. The domain
D_{+}(T,X)
can be thought of as the set of points that uniqueness of the solution
u
to
(\partial_{t}^{2}+P(x,D))u=f
can be propagated to if
u
vanishes for
t<0
.

Presume for the moment that the solution operator
S
(ie. the operator mapping
f
to
u
in
Eq.

4.1
) is well-defined (which will be shown in
Lemma

4.2
). Writing
\mathbb{R}_{+}=(0,\infty)
, the main result of this section, in essence a reformulation of
[
Hör07
, Prop. 17.4.3]
, is

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
y\in\Omega
and let
X\Subset\Omega
be an open normal neighborhood of
y
. Let
T>0
with
T<\mathrm{inj}_{g}(y)
and write
X\times X\setminus\mathrm{d}\coloneqq\{(x,y)\in X\times X\colon x\neq y\}
.

1.

There is
\mathrm{FS}_{\psi a}^{\frac{n-3}{2}}(X\times X)\ni\underline{\tilde{a}}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\tilde{a}_{j}
that is classical and elliptic with the following property. For every finite realization
\tilde{a}\in S_{\psi a}^{\frac{n-3}{2}}(X\times X)
of
\underline{\tilde{a}}
there is
\tilde{u}\in C^{\omega}(D(T,X)\times X)
so that

(t,x,y)\in D_{+}(T,X)\times X\implies\int e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}^{2}(x,y))}\tilde{a}(x,y,\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta-S(\delta_{(0,y)})(t,x)=\tilde{u}(t,x,y)\,.

2.

There is
\mathrm{FS}_{\psi a}^{\frac{n-3}{2}}(X\times X\setminus\mathrm{d})\ni\underline{a}=\sum_{j\geq 0}a_{j}
that is classical and elliptic with the following property. For every finite realization
a\in S_{\psi a}^{\frac{n-3}{2}}(X\times X\setminus\mathrm{d})
of
\underline{a}
, and any
\tilde{a}
finite realization of
\underline{\tilde{a}}
from 1., there is
u\in C^{\omega}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times X\times X\setminus\mathrm{d})
so that

(t,x,y)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times X\times X\setminus\mathrm{d}\implies\int e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}^{2}(x,y))}\tilde{a}(x,y,\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta-\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}a(x,y,\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta=u(t,x,y)\,.

Perhaps the first statement is not new, see for instance
[
Zel17
, § 6.1]
, but we shall perform its proof nonetheless in order to facilitate the proof of the second statement. In the application in
Section

5
, it will be beneficial to have an FIO representation of this solution (for
t>0
) with a phase that is linear with respect to
t
. This can be achieved with the substitution
\theta\mapsto\theta(t+d_{g}(x,y))^{-1}
upon the FIO from statement 1. (see
[
Zel12
, Eq. (5.14)]
), but as we will want to remove the time dependence from our amplitude as well, we shall use a different method. The fact that the amplitude
a(x,y,\theta)
in statement 2. is independent of
t
will be exploited crucially in
Theorem

5.4
.

Let us first use
[
Hör07
, Thm. 23.2.2]
to guarantee the existence of the solution operator.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
T>0,s\in\mathbb{R}
and
X\Subset\Omega
.

For every
f\in H^{s-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}((-T,T)\times\Omega)
with
\mathrm{supp}f\subset\{t\geq 0\}
, there exists a solution
v\in H^{s}_{\mathrm{loc}}((-T,T)\times\Omega)
to

(\partial_{t}^{2}+P(x,D))v=f\,\ \text{in}\ (-T,T)\times X,\quad\mathrm{supp}v\subset\{t\geq 0\}\,.

(4.2)

Any distribution
\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((-T,T)\times\Omega)
satisfying
Eq.

4.2
agrees with
v
in
D(T,X)
.

From now onward, for appropriately chosen
f
, we let
{S}(f)
denote the solution to
Eq.

4.2
in
H^{s}_{\mathrm{loc}}(D(T,X)\times X)
.

Proof.

Due to our assumptions on
P
, the operator
\partial_{t}^{2}+P
satisfies the conditions necessary to apply
[
Hör07
, Thm. 23.2.4]
, which gives the existence of the solution
v
to
Eq.

4.2
. (We extend the coefficients of
P
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
smoothly so that they remain unchanged in
X
).

In order to prove the uniqueness in
D(T,X)
we shall use the propagation of analytic singularities. Assume there exists some
\tilde{v}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((-T,T)\times\Omega)
solution to
Eq.

4.2
. We find that in
(-T,T)\times X
,

(\partial_{t}^{2}+P(x,D))(v-\tilde{v})=0\,,\quad\text{and in particular}\quad\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}(v-\tilde{v})\subset\mathrm{Char}(\partial_{t}^{2}+P(x,D))\,,

where the second part is a consequence of
[
Hör03
, Thm. 8.6.1]
. We are allowed to apply the propagation of analytic singularities
[
Hör03
, Thm. 7.3]
(or
[
Sjö82
, Thm. 9.1]
) to conclude that because
v-\tilde{v}
vanishes in
t<0
, it must be analytic in
D(T,X)
and thus, in fact, vanish there (by analytic continuation).
∎

We now consider the Hadamard parametrix for the wave equation á la
[
Hör07
, § 17]
, which is given with respect to homogeneous distributions, which we will reformulate in the setting of analytic FIO.

We will need a few properties of homogeneous distributions for which we refer to
[
Hör03
, § 3.2]
and
[
GS64
]
. We also refer to
[
DK10
]
, which has a different notation convention. In particular, using Hörmander’s notation, we will consider the homogeneous distributions
\chi_{+}^{a}\in{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\setminus 0)
defined for all
a\in\mathbb{C}
as
\chi_{+}^{a}(s)=\frac{s^{a}}{\Gamma(a+1)}H(s)
with
H
the Heaviside distribution and
\Gamma
is Euler’s Gamma function. We collect a few facts about these distributions.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
a\in\mathbb{C}
.

1.

\chi^{-1}_{+}=\delta_{0}
and
\chi_{+}^{0}=H
the Heaviside distribution.

2.

Let
f,g\colon\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{R}
be defined as
f(t,x)=t^{2}-\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{2}
and
g(t,x)=t-\left\lvert x\right\rvert
. For
x\neq 0
we have

f^{\ast}\chi_{+}^{a}=(t+\left\lvert x\right\rvert)^{a}g^{\ast}\chi_{+}^{a}\,.

3.

We have

\chi^{a}_{+}(t^{2}-\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{2})=e^{-i\frac{(a+1)\pi}{2}}\int e^{i\theta(t^{2}-\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{2})}(\theta-i0)^{-a-1}\mathrm{d}\theta\,,

where
(\theta-i0)^{-a-1}
is a distribution defined in
[
Hör03
, § 3.2]
, which satisfies
(\theta-i0)^{-a-1}=\theta^{-a-1}
for
\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert>0
.

4.

For
x\neq 0
,

\chi^{a}_{+}(t^{2}-\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{2})=(t+\left\lvert x\right\rvert)^{a}e^{-i\frac{(a+1)\pi}{2}}\int e^{i\theta(t-\left\lvert x\right\rvert)}(\theta-i0)^{-a-1}\mathrm{d}\theta\,.

Proof.

Item 1. is by definition, see
[
Hör03
, § 3.2]
.

We turn to showing item 2. For the Heaviside distribution
H\in{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
a calculation (or see
[
DK10
, Exc. 10.22(ii)]
) gives that for
h\in\{f,g\}
we have

\langle h^{\ast}H,\psi\rangle=\int_{h^{-1}((0,\infty))}\psi(s)\mathrm{d}s\,.

This directly implies that
f^{\ast}H=g^{\ast}H
, which shows item 2. for
a=0
.

To show item 2. for all
a\in\mathbb{C}
, we note that by definition, (see also – with different notation –
[
DK10
, § 13]
),
\chi_{+}^{a}(s)=\frac{s^{a}}{\Gamma(a+1)}H(s)
, where
H
is the Heaviside distribution. Thus, using that we have shown item 2. for
H=\chi_{+}^{0}
already,

\chi_{+}^{a}(t^{2}-\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{2})=(t+\left\lvert x\right\rvert)^{a}\frac{(t-\left\lvert x\right\rvert)^{a}}{\Gamma(a+1)}H(t^{2}-\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{2})=(t+\left\lvert x\right\rvert)^{a}\frac{(t-\left\lvert x\right\rvert)^{a}}{\Gamma(a+1)}H(t-\left\lvert x\right\rvert)=(t+\left\lvert x\right\rvert)^{a}\chi^{a}_{+}(t-\left\lvert x\right\rvert)\,.

According to
[
Hör03
, Exa. 7.1.17]
(or
[
GS64
, p. 172]
), we have

\mathcal{F}(\chi_{+}^{a})(\theta)=e^{-i(a+1)\pi/2}(\theta-i0)^{-a-1}\,,

which completes the proof of item 3. Item 4. is a consequence of items 2. and 3.
∎

We now recall the Hadamard parametrix as it is described in
[
Hör07
, § 17.4]
. This construction will facilitate the proof of
Theorem

4.1
. The notation will be as in
Theorem

4.1
:
y\in\Omega
and
X
is a normal neighborhood of
y
, and
T>0
with
T<\mathrm{inj}_{g}(y)
and we write
X\times X\setminus\mathrm{d}\coloneqq\{(x,y)\in X\times X\colon x\neq y\}
.

According to
[
Hör07
, Prop. 17.4.3]
, there are smooth functions
\tilde{U}_{j}\in C^{\infty}(X\times X)
and distributions
E_{j}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times X\times X),j\in\mathbb{N}
so that for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
and all
(t,x,y)\in(-\infty,T)\times X\times X
,

(\partial_{t}^{2}+P(x,D))\left\lvert g(y)\right\rvert^{-1}\sum_{j=0}^{N}\tilde{U}_{j}(x,y)E_{j}(t,d_{g}(x,y))=\delta_{(0,y)}(t,x)+\left\lvert g(y)\right\rvert^{-1}(P(x,D)\tilde{U}_{N}(x,y))E_{N}(t,d_{g}(x,y))\,,

(4.3)

where
\left\lvert g\right\rvert=(\det g^{jk})^{1/2}
.
Furthermore, the distributions
E_{j}
are supported in
t\geq 0
and satisfy
E_{j}(0,\cdot)=0
.

Following the construction in
[
Hör07
, § 17.4]
where we use the fact that the coefficients of
P
are analytic, it is easy to see that each
\tilde{U}_{j}(x,y),j\in\mathbb{N}
is analytic for all
(x,y)\in X\times X
. In fact, the construction of
\tilde{U}_{j}
is entirely explicit in geodesic normal coordinates, and one finds that there exists
C>0
so that for all
\alpha,\beta,j

\sup_{X\times X}\left\lvert\partial^{\alpha}_{x}\partial^{\beta}_{y}\tilde{U}_{j}(x,y)\right\rvert\leq C^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert+j+1}j!\alpha!\beta!\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\inf_{X\times X}\left\lvert\tilde{U}_{0}\right\rvert>0\,.

(4.4)

Because for
j\geq 0
the distribution
E_{j}
is supported in
t\geq 0
, from
Eq.

4.3
and the uniqueness statement in
Lemma

4.2
we conclude that

\left\lvert g(y)\right\rvert^{-1}\sum_{j=0}^{N}\tilde{U}_{j}(x,y)E_{j}(t,d_{g}(x,y))-{S}\left[\left\lvert g(y)\right\rvert^{-1}(P(x,D)\tilde{U}_{N}(\cdot_{x},y))E_{N}(\cdot_{t},d_{g}(\cdot_{x},y))\right](t,x)={S}(\delta_{(0,y)})(t,x)\,,

(4.5)

for
(t,x,y)\in D(T,X)\times X
.

Furthermore, we may use the explicit definition, given in
[
Hör03
, Lem. 17.4.2]
, for the distributions
E_{j},j\in\mathbb{N}
when
t>0
, and using
Lemma

4.3
, for
t>0

\displaystyle E_{j}(t,d_{g}(x,y))

\displaystyle=2^{-2j-1}\pi^{\frac{1-n}{2}}\chi_{+}^{j+\frac{1-n}{2}}(t^{2}-d_{g}^{2}(x,y))

(4.6)

\displaystyle=2^{-2j-1}\pi^{\frac{1-n}{2}}e^{-i\left({j+\frac{3-n}{2}}\right)\frac{\pi}{2}}\int e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}(\theta-i0)^{-j+\frac{n-3}{2}}\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle=2^{-2j-1}\pi^{\frac{1-n}{2}}(t+d_{g}(x,y))^{j+\frac{1-n}{2}}e^{-i\left({j+\frac{3-n}{2}}\right)\frac{\pi}{2}}\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}(\theta-i0)^{-j+\frac{n-3}{2}}\mathrm{d}\theta\,,

where the last equality is only true for
x\neq y
. We will use the bottom two representations of
E_{j}(t,d_{g}(x,y))
, and since we want to rewrite our solution operator as an FIO, we will extract the ‘amplitude part’ of
E_{j}
. To lighten notation we first introduce for
j\geq 0
,

U_{j}(x,y)\coloneqq 2^{-2j-1}\pi^{\frac{1-n}{2}}e^{-i({j+\frac{3-n}{2}})\pi/2}\tilde{U}_{j}(x,y)\,,

(4.7)

which, due to
Eq.

4.4
satisfy, for some
C>0
and all
j\geq 0
,

\sup_{X\times X}\left\lvert\partial^{\alpha}_{x}\partial^{\beta}_{y}U_{j}(x,y)\right\rvert\leq C^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert+j+1}j!\alpha!\beta!\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\inf_{X\times X}\left\lvert U_{0}\right\rvert>0\,.

(4.8)

We have the following properties for amplitudes defined by
U_{j}
:

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
(\tilde{\varphi}_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be a sequence of Ehrenpreis cut-offs vanishing for
\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert\leq 1
and identically
1
for
\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert\geq 2
, and let
\rho>0
be large enough. Define
\varphi_{j}(\theta)\coloneqq\tilde{\varphi}_{j}(\theta(j\rho)^{-1}),j\geq 1
, and
\varphi_{0}\coloneqq\varphi_{1}
.

Define the expressions

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} a^{\prime}_{j}(t,x,y,\theta)&\coloneqq\left\lvert g(y)\right\rvert^{-1}(t+d_{g}(x,y))^{j+\frac{1-n}{2}}U_{j}(x,y)\varphi_{0}(4\theta)\theta^{-j+\frac{n-3}{2}}\\
a^{\prime\prime}_{j}(t,x,y,\theta)&\coloneqq\left\lvert g(y)\right\rvert^{-1}(t+d_{g}(x,y))^{j+\frac{1-n}{2}}U_{j}(x,y)(1-\varphi_{j}(\theta))(\theta-i0)^{-j+\frac{n-3}{2}}\\
\tilde{a}^{\prime}_{j}(x,y,\theta)&\coloneqq\left\lvert g(y)\right\rvert^{-1}U_{j}(x,y)\varphi_{0}(4\theta)\theta^{-j+\frac{n-3}{2}}\\
\tilde{a}^{\prime\prime}_{j}(x,y,\theta)&\coloneqq\left\lvert g(y)\right\rvert^{-1}U_{j}(x,y)(1-\varphi_{j}(\theta))(\theta-i0)^{-j+\frac{n-3}{2}}\\
r_{j}^{\prime}(x,y,\theta)&\coloneq-\left\lvert g(y)\right\rvert^{-1}(P(x,D)U_{j}(x,y))\varphi_{j}(\theta)\theta^{-j+\frac{n-3}{2}}\\
r_{j}^{\prime\prime}(x,y,\theta)&\coloneq-\left\lvert g(y)\right\rvert^{-1}(P(x,D)U_{j}(x,y))(1-\varphi_{j}(\theta))(\theta-i0)^{-j+\frac{n-3}{2}}\,.\end{aligned}

We have that
a_{0}^{\prime}
and
\tilde{a}_{0}^{\prime}
are non-vanishing for
\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert
away from
0
and
\underline{a^{\prime}}\coloneqq\sum_{j\geq 0}a^{\prime}_{j}\in\mathrm{FS}_{\psi a}^{\frac{n-3}{2}}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times X\times X\setminus\mathrm{d})
and
\underline{\tilde{a}^{\prime}}\coloneqq\sum_{j\geq 0}\tilde{a}^{\prime}_{j}\in\mathrm{FS}_{\psi a}^{\frac{n-3}{2}}(X\times X)
. In addition, each
a_{j}^{\prime}
and each
\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime}
is homogeneous away from the origin of degree
-j+\frac{n-3}{2}
.

Furthermore, for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
(t,x,y)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times X\times X\setminus\mathrm{d}
,

\int e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}(\varphi_{j}\tilde{a}^{\prime}_{j}+\tilde{a}^{\prime\prime}_{j})(x,y,\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta=\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}(\varphi_{j}a^{\prime}_{j}+a^{\prime\prime}_{j})(t,x,y,\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta\,,

(4.9)

and for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
(t,x,y)\in D_{+}(T,X)\times X
,

\displaystyle\int e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\sum_{j=0}^{N}(\varphi_{j}\tilde{a}^{\prime}_{j}+\tilde{a}^{\prime\prime}_{j})(x,y,\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta+S\left[\int e^{i\theta(\cdot_{t}^{2}-d_{g}(\cdot_{x},y)^{2})}(r_{N}^{\prime}(\cdot_{x},y,\theta)+r_{N}^{\prime\prime}(\cdot_{x},y,\theta))\mathrm{d}\theta\right](t,x)

\displaystyle=S(\delta_{(0,y)})(t,x)\,.

(4.10)

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.

From the first part of
Eq.

4.8
we conclude that
\underline{\tilde{a}^{\prime}}\in\mathrm{FS}_{\psi a}^{\frac{n-3}{2}}(X\times X)
due to the common growth rate among the
\tilde{a}^{\prime}_{j}
. That
\tilde{a}_{0}^{\prime}
is non-vanishing for
\theta
away from the origin follows from the second part of
Eq.

4.8
. The same arguments are valid for
a_{0}^{\prime}
and
\underline{a^{\prime}}
.

Note that
\varphi_{j}(\theta)\varphi_{0}(4\theta)=\varphi_{j}(\theta)
for all
j\geq 0
. Using
Eq.

4.6
, for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
and all
(t,x,y)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times X\times X\setminus\mathrm{d}
, we find
Eq.

4.9
. Also due to
Eq.

4.6
, for
(t,x,y)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times X\times X
,

\int e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}(\varphi_{j}\tilde{a}^{\prime}_{j}+\tilde{a}^{\prime\prime}_{j})(x,y,\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta=\left\lvert g(y)\right\rvert^{-1}\tilde{U}_{j}(x,y)E_{j}(t,d_{g}(x,y))\,,

with a similar formula for
r_{N}^{\prime}+r_{N}^{\prime\prime}
. Plugging this into
Eq.

4.5
, we find
Eq.

4.10
.
∎

Note that neither
a_{j}^{\prime\prime}
nor
\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime\prime}
nor
r_{j}^{\prime\prime}
are amplitudes as we have defined them in
Section

2
(as they are distributions with respect to
\theta
), but we will show that their contributions are negligible and focus on
\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime}
and
a_{j}^{\prime}
which are amplitudes.

We are now in position to perform the

Proof of
Theorem

4.1
.

Recall the definitions of
a^{\prime}_{j},a^{\prime\prime}_{j},r^{\prime}_{j},r^{\prime\prime}_{j},\varphi_{j}
from
Lemma

4.4
.
Applying
Lemma

2.6
,
\tilde{a}^{\prime}\coloneqq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\varphi_{j}\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime}\in S^{\frac{n-3}{2}}_{\psi a}(X\times X)
is a well-defined finite realization of
\underline{\tilde{a}^{\prime}}
. Notice that for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

\tilde{a}^{\prime}=\sum_{j=0}^{N}(\varphi_{j}\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime}+\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime\prime})-\sum_{j=0}^{N}\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime\prime}+\left(\tilde{a}^{\prime}-\sum_{j=0}^{N}\varphi_{j}\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime}\right)\,,

and defining

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(D(T,X)\times X)\ni\tilde{q}(t,x,y)\coloneqq

(4.11)

\displaystyle S\left[\int e^{i\theta(\cdot_{t}^{2}-d_{g}(\cdot_{x},y)^{2})}(r_{N}^{\prime}(\cdot_{x},y,\theta)+r_{N}^{\prime\prime}(\cdot_{x},y,\theta))\mathrm{d}\theta\right](t,x)-\int e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\left(-\sum_{j=0}^{N}\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime\prime}+\tilde{a}^{\prime}-\sum_{j=0}^{N}\varphi_{j}\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime}\right)\mathrm{d}\theta\,,

Eq.

4.10
gives us, on
D_{+}(T,X)\times X
,

\int e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\tilde{a}^{\prime}(x,y,\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta+\tilde{q}(t,x,y)=S(\delta_{(0,y)})(t,x)\,.

Notice that we have different formulas for
\tilde{q}
for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
that have to agree. Because the difference of any two finite realizations of the same formal pseudoanalytic amplitude is an exponentially decaying term that gives rise to an analytic error by
[
Tre22
, Prop. 17.1.19]
, the proof of the first statement in
Theorem

4.1
is complete once we show

Lemma 4.5
.

We have

\tilde{q}(t,x,y)\in C^{\omega}(D(T,X)\times X)\,.

We defer the proof of
Lemma

4.5
to
Section

4.1
and move on to prove the second statement in
Theorem

4.1
.

Define
a^{\prime}\coloneqq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\varphi_{j}a^{\prime}_{j}\in S^{\frac{n-3}{2}}_{\psi a}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times X\times X\setminus\mathrm{d})
, which is a finite realization of the formal amplitude
\underline{a}^{\prime}
according to
Lemma

2.6
.
We proceed as before to find that for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

a^{\prime}=\sum_{j=0}^{N}(\varphi_{j}a_{j}^{\prime}+a_{j}^{\prime\prime})-\sum_{j=0}^{N}a_{j}^{\prime\prime}+\left(a^{\prime}-\sum_{j=0}^{N}\varphi_{j}a_{j}^{\prime}\right)\,,

so that

\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}a^{\prime}(t,x,y,\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta-\int e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\tilde{a}^{\prime}(x,y,\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta=q(t,x,y)\,,

(4.12)

where
Eq.

4.9
will give, for
(t,x,y)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times X\times X\setminus\mathrm{d}
,

q\coloneqq\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}\left(-\sum_{j=0}^{N}a_{j}^{\prime\prime}+a^{\prime}-\sum_{j=0}^{N}\varphi_{j}a_{j}^{\prime}\right)-e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\left(-\sum_{j=0}^{N}\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime\prime}+\tilde{a}^{\prime}-\sum_{j=0}^{N}\varphi_{j}\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime}\right)\mathrm{d}\theta\,.

(4.13)

Lemma 4.6
.

We have

{q}(t,x,y)\in C^{\omega}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times X\times X\setminus\mathrm{d})\,.

The proof of this lemma can also be found in
Section

4.1
, so that the penultimate step is to remove the dependence of
a^{\prime}
on
t
, compare these arguments to those in
[
Tre22
, § 21.1.2]
.

Notice first that by Taylor’s theorem, for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
(t,x,y)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times X\times X\setminus\mathrm{d}
,

a^{\prime}(t,x,y,\theta)=\sum_{j=0}^{N}\frac{\partial_{t}^{j}a^{\prime}(d_{g}(x,y),x,y,\theta)}{j!}(t-d_{g}(x,y))^{j}+R_{N+1}(t,x,y,\theta)(t-d_{g}(x,y))^{N+1}\,,

(4.14)

where

R_{N+1}(t,x,y,\theta)=\frac{1}{N!}\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)^{N}(\partial_{t}^{N+1}a^{\prime})((1-s)t+sd_{g}(x,y),x,y,\theta)\mathrm{d}s\,.

(4.15)

By partial integration

\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}\frac{\partial_{t}^{j}a^{\prime}(d_{g}(x,y),x,y,\theta)}{j!}(t-d_{g}(x,y))^{j}\mathrm{d}\theta=\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}\frac{\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{t}^{j}a^{\prime}(d_{g}(x,y),x,y,\theta)}{j!(-1)^{j}}\mathrm{d}\theta\,,

so that we define for
j\geq 0
,

a_{j}^{\circ}(x,y,\theta)\coloneqq\frac{(\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{t}^{j}a^{\prime})(d_{g}(x,y),x,y,\theta)}{j!(-1)^{j}}\,,

(4.16)

and it is an easy exercise to check that
\sum_{j\geq 0}a_{j}^{\circ}\in\mathrm{FS}^{\frac{n-3}{2}}_{\psi a}(X\times X\setminus\mathrm{d})
.
Thus, by
Lemma

2.6
,

a^{\circ}(x,y,\theta)\coloneqq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\varphi_{j}(\theta)a^{\circ}_{j}(x,y,\theta)\in S^{\frac{n-3}{2}}_{\psi a}(X\times X\setminus\mathrm{d})

is a pseudoanalytic amplitude. In
Section

4.1
we prove

Lemma 4.7
.

We have

\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}(a^{\prime}-a^{\circ})\mathrm{d}\theta\in C^{\omega}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times X\times X\setminus\mathrm{d})\,.

We modify the amplitude another time. The reason for this is: the amplitude
a^{\circ}
is the finite realization of a formal pseudoanalytic amplitude
\sum_{j\geq 0}a^{\circ}_{j}
, but each
a^{\circ}_{j}
is not necessarily homogeneous in
\theta
away from the origin (which we require for classicality).

Recalling that
a^{\prime}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\varphi_{k}a_{k}^{\prime}
with absolute convergence, we have for
a^{\circ}
from above,

a^{\circ}(x,y,\theta)=\sum_{j,k\geq 0}\varphi_{k}(\theta)\varphi_{j}(\theta)\frac{\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{t}^{j}a_{k}^{\prime}(d_{g}(x,y),x,y,\theta)}{j!(-1)^{j}}\,.

This motivates us to define

a_{s}(x,y,\theta)\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\geq 0\\
j+k=s\end{subarray}}\frac{(\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{t}^{j}a^{\prime}_{k})(d_{g}(x,y),x,y,\theta)}{j!(-1)^{j}}\in S_{\psi a}^{-s+\frac{n-3}{2}}(X\times X\setminus\mathrm{d})\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\underline{a}\coloneqq\sum_{s\geq 0}a_{s}\in\mathrm{FS}^{\frac{n-3}{2}}_{\psi a}(X\times X\setminus\mathrm{d})\,.

(4.17)

Checking that
\underline{a}
is a formal pseudoanalytic amplitude is routine: use

\left\lvert\frac{1}{j!}\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{t}^{j}a^{\prime}_{k}\right\rvert\leq MC^{2j+k}k!\frac{(2j)!}{j!}\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert^{-k-j+\frac{n-3}{2}}

and the fact that
(j!)^{-1}(2j)!\leq 4^{j}j!
, and
j!k!\leq(j+k)!
to get for a new constant
C>0
, for
j+k=s
,

\left\lvert\frac{1}{j!}\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{t}^{j}a^{\prime}_{k}\right\rvert\leq MC^{s}s!\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert^{-s+\frac{n-3}{2}}\,,

and noting that there are
s
many choices of
(j,k)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
so that
s=j+k
(and estimating
s\leq e^{s}
for
s\geq 1
), we indeed see that
\underline{a}\in\mathrm{FS}_{\psi a}^{\frac{n-3}{2}}
(after performing similar calculations for all derivatives of
a_{s}
with respect to
\theta
).

Define
a(x,y,\theta)\coloneqq\sum_{s\geq 0}\varphi_{s}(2\theta)a_{s}(x,y,\theta)
, which is a finite realization of
\underline{a}
(defined in
Eq.

4.17
), and thus differs from any other by an exponentially decaying function, which by
[
Tre22
, Prop. 17.1.19]
leads to an analytic error. We prove in
Section

4.1
that

Lemma 4.8
.

The pseudoanalytic amplitude
a^{\circ}-a
is exponentially decaying.

We thus conclude that

\displaystyle\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}

\displaystyle a(x,y,\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta-\int e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\tilde{a}^{\prime}(x,y,\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle=\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}a^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\theta-\int e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\tilde{a}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\theta+\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}(a-a^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle=q+\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}((a-a^{\circ})+(a^{\circ}-a^{\prime}))\mathrm{d}\theta\,,

where according to
Lemma

4.6
the first term is analytic, and according to
Lemmas

4.7
and
4.8
(and an application of
[
Tre22
, Prop. 17.1.19]
) the last term is analytic, all of this for
t>0
and away from the diagonal
x=y
. This completes the proof that indeed
\underline{a}
is the formal pseudoanalytic amplitude that we were looking for in this result.
∎

4.1
Proofs of Lemmas

In this section we prove
Lemmas

4.5
,
4.6
,
4.7
and
4.8
, for which we shall first state and prove two other technical lemmas. Let
N^{\ast}\in\mathbb{N}
and open
\mho\subset\mathbb{R}^{N^{\ast}}
be arbitrary.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{R}
and
a_{j},j=0,1,\dots
be a sequence of pseudoanalytic amplitudes
a_{j}\in S^{m-j}_{\psi a}(\mho)
so that there exists a complex open neighborhood
\mho^{\mathbb{C}}\supset\mho
, independent of
j
, so that
a_{j},j\geq 0
can be extended smoothly to a function on
\mho^{\mathbb{C}}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
that is holomorphic with respect to
z
and to every compact
K\subset\mho^{\mathbb{C}}
there exist
C>0,R\geq 0
so that

\displaystyle\sup_{K\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}}\left\lvert a_{j}(z,\eta)\right\rvert

\displaystyle<C^{j+1}\left(1+\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\right)^{m}\,,

(4.18)

\displaystyle\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}\,,\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert>Rj\implies\left\lvert a_{j}(z,\eta)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C\left(Cj\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{-1}\right)^{j}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{m}\,.

(4.19)

Let
\varphi
be an analytic phase and assume that there exists a distribution
A\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mho)
so that

Au=\int e^{i\varphi(z,\eta)}a_{j}(z,\eta)u(z)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z

for all
j
, namely, this distribution is independent of
j
. Under these conditions,
A\in C^{\omega}(\mho)
.

In particular, any formal pseudoanalytic amplitude (as the sequence of
a_{j}
) will satisfy the above conditions.

Proof.

We follow the proof of
Proposition

2.15
and thus keep this proof short. Fix some
z_{0}\in\mho
and
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{\ast}}
with
\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert=1
. Let
\psi_{L}
be a sequence of Ehrenpreis cut-offs supported near
z_{0}
and identically
1
near
z_{0}
. The lemma is proved if we can show that for all large enough
t>0
,

\mathcal{F}(\psi_{L}A)(t\sigma)=t^{n}\iint e^{it(\varphi(z,\eta)-z\cdot\sigma)}a_{L}(z,t\eta)\psi_{L}(z)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z

satisfies
\left\lvert\mathcal{F}(\psi_{L}A)(t\sigma)\right\rvert\leq t^{k}C(CL/t)^{L}
for some constant
C>0
. The reason we were allowed to choose
a_{j}=a_{L}
is because
A
is independent of
j
by assumption.

Following the proof of
Proposition

2.15
, there is
\delta>0
so that if
z\in\mathrm{supp}\psi_{L}
and
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\leq 2\delta
then
\left\lvert\partial_{z}\varphi(z,\eta)-\sigma\right\rvert\geq 1/2
. We let
\gamma_{L}
be a sequence of Ehrenpreis cut-offs identically
1
in
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\leq\delta
and supported in
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\leq 2\delta
.

We split up the integral as
\mathcal{F}(\psi_{L}A)(t\sigma)=t^{n}(I_{1}+I_{2})
with

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=\iint e^{it(\varphi(z,\eta)-z\cdot\sigma)}a_{L}(z,t\eta)\gamma_{L}(\eta)\psi_{L}(z)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle I_{2}
