\displaystyle=\iint e^{it(\varphi(z,\eta)-z\cdot\sigma)}a_{L}(z,t\eta)(1-\gamma_{L}(\eta))\psi_{L}(z)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z\,.

We will want to use partial integration with respect to
z
on the integral in
I_{1}
. Note that
Eq.

4.18
and
Eq.

2.15
imply that for
\kappa=\varphi(z,\eta)-z\cdot\sigma
, (using the Cauchy-inequalities, and recall
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa,2}
from
Eq.

2.12
)

\left\lvert\mathcal{L}_{\kappa,2}^{L}\left(a_{L}(z,t\eta)\gamma_{L}(\eta)\psi_{L}(z)\right)\right\rvert\leq C(C_{\mathcal{L}}CL)^{L}

for some
C>0
. We conclude that

\left\lvert I_{1}\right\rvert\leq t^{-L}\left\lvert\iint e^{it(\varphi(z,\eta)-z\cdot\sigma)}\mathcal{L}_{\kappa,2}^{L}\left(a_{L}(z,t\eta)\gamma_{L}(\eta)\psi_{L}(z)\right)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}z\right\rvert\leq C(CL/t)^{L}

for some
C>0
.

Turning to the integral
I_{2}
, we use the fact that on the support of the integrand we have
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq\delta
, and assuming
L
to be large enough and
t\geq RL\delta^{-1}
, from
Eq.

4.19
we have the bound

\left\lvert a_{L}(z,t\eta)\right\rvert\leq\left(\frac{CL}{t\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\right)^{L}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{m}L^{k}\leq L^{k}\left(\frac{CL}{t\delta}\right)^{L}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{-n-1}\delta^{n+1-m}\,,

from which we conclude that

\left\lvert I_{2}\right\rvert\leq L^{k}C\left(\frac{CL}{t}\right)^{L}\leq Ct^{k}\left(\frac{CL}{t}\right)^{L}

for some
C>0
. This concludes the proof.
∎

We will repeatedly use the above lemma in a specific context, which we state here.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{R}
and
\underline{a}=\sum_{j\geq 0}a_{j}\in\mathrm{FS}^{m}_{\psi a}(\mho)
be a formal pseudoanalytic amplitude. Let
a\in S^{m}_{\psi a}(\mho)
be a finite realization of
\underline{a}
. Let
(\tilde{\varphi}_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be a sequence of Ehrenpreis cut-offs vanishing for
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\leq 1
and identically
1
for
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq 2
, and let
\rho>0
be large enough. Define
\varphi_{j}(\eta)\coloneqq\tilde{\varphi}_{j}(\eta(j\rho)^{-1}),j\geq 1
and
\varphi_{0}\coloneqq\varphi_{1}
.

The sequence of pseudoanalytic amplitudes
c_{k}
, defined via

c_{k}\coloneqq a-\sum_{j=0}^{k}\varphi_{j}a_{j}

satisfies the assumptions of
Lemma

4.4
(for
R=0
in
Eq.

4.19
).

Proof.

By
Lemma

2.6
, we know that
\sum_{j\geq 0}\varphi_{j}a_{j}
is a finite realization of
\underline{a}
that can differ from
a
by a term bounded by
Ce^{-C^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}
for some
C>0
, which naturally satisfies both

Eqs.

4.18
and
4.19
. Therefore, we may assume without loss of generality that
a=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varphi_{j}a_{j}
.

This means that
c_{k}
is given by

c_{k}=a-\sum_{j=0}^{k}\varphi_{j}a_{j}=\sum_{j=k+1}^{\infty}\varphi_{j}a_{j}=\sum_{j=k+1}^{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\rho^{-1}}\varphi_{j}a_{j}

(4.20)

due to the support properties of
\varphi_{j}
.
Since
\sum_{j\geq 0}a_{j}
is a formal pseudoanalytic amplitude, we find for some
C,R>0
,

\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq Rj\implies\left\lvert a_{j}\right\rvert\leq C\left(\frac{Cj}{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\right)^{j}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{m}\,.

Note that if
\rho>R
, then
\varphi_{j}a_{j}
is supported in
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\geq\rho j>Rj
, so that for all
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert>0
,

\left\lvert\varphi_{j}a_{j}\right\rvert\leq C\left(\frac{Cj}{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\right)^{j}\leq C(C/\rho)^{j}\leq Ce^{-j}\,,

(4.21)

if
\rho>Ce
is large enough and we used the support properties of
\varphi_{j}
again. Thus, by
Eq.

4.20
,

\left\lvert c_{k}\right\rvert\leq\sum_{j=k+1}^{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\rho^{-1}}\left\lvert\varphi_{j}a_{j}\right\rvert\leq C\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}e^{-j}<C^{\prime}

for some
C^{\prime}>0
independent of
k
, from which we conclude the first part of
Eq.

4.18
for
c_{k}
.

It is clear from
Eq.

4.21
that
\left\lvert\varphi_{j}a_{j}\right\rvert\leq C(Ck/\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert)^{k}
for all
j\geq k
, so that

\left\lvert c_{k}\right\rvert\leq\sum_{j=k+1}^{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\rho^{-1}}\left\lvert\varphi_{j}a_{j}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\rho^{-1}C\left(\frac{C(k+1)}{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\right)^{k+1}\leq C^{2}k\rho^{-1}\left(\frac{2Ck}{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\right)^{k}\leq C^{2}\rho^{-1}\left(\frac{2eCk}{\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert}\right)^{k}\,,

which gives
Eq.

4.19
for
c_{k}
.
∎

We have all tools in hand to provide the

Proof of Lemma
4.5
.

According to
[
Hör03
, Thm. 8.6.1]
we have

\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}(\tilde{q})\subset\mathrm{Char}\left(\partial_{t}^{2}+P(x,D)\right)\cup\mathrm{WF}_{\mathrm{a}}\left((\partial_{t}^{2}+P(x,D))\tilde{q}\right)\,.

We claim that

\tilde{q}\in C^{\omega}((-T,0)\times X)\,,\quad\text{and}\quad(\partial_{t}^{2}+P(x,D))\tilde{q}\in C^{\omega}(D(T,X)\times X)\,.

(4.22)

Before proving this claim let us show how the lemma follows from it. The second part of
Eq.

4.22
allows us to apply the propagation of analytic singularities, see
[
Hör71b
, Thm. 7.3]
(or
[
Tre22
, Thm. 24.8.6]
), and we deduce from the first part of
Eq.

4.22
that

\tilde{q}\in C^{\omega}(D(T,X)\times X)\,.

The proof of this lemma is thus reduced to proving the claim
Eq.

4.22
.

Let us remind ourselves that for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\tilde{q}(t,x,y)=

\displaystyle S\left[\int e^{i\theta(\cdot_{t}^{2}-d_{g}(\cdot_{x},y)^{2})}(r_{N}^{\prime}(\cdot_{x},y,\theta)+r_{N}^{\prime\prime}(\cdot_{x},y,\theta))\mathrm{d}\theta\right](t,x)-\int e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\left(-\sum_{j=0}^{N}\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime\prime}+\underbrace{\tilde{a}^{\prime}-\sum_{j=0}^{N}\varphi_{j}\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime}}_{\eqqcolon R_{N}^{\prime}}\right)\mathrm{d}\theta\,.

Because
\sum_{j=0}^{N}\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime\prime}
is a distribution that is compactly supported with respect to
\theta
, introducing some
\gamma\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
so that
\int\gamma(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta=1
, we find

\int e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\sum_{j=0}^{N}\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime\prime}\mathrm{d}\theta=\int e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\underbrace{\gamma(\theta)e^{-i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\left\langle\sum_{j=0}^{N}\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime\prime}(x,y,\xi),e^{i\xi(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\right\rangle_{\xi}}_{\eqqcolon\tilde{R}_{N}}\mathrm{d}\theta\,.

Because
S(\cdot)
vanishes for
t<0
, for
t<0
we may then write

\tilde{q}=\int e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\left(\tilde{R}_{N}-R_{N}^{\prime}\right)\mathrm{d}\theta\,.

We now aim to show that the sequence
\tilde{R}_{N}-R_{N}^{\prime}
satisfies the assumptions of
Lemma

4.9
so that we may conclude that
\tilde{q}
is analytic for
t<0
. It is clear that for each fixed
N
this term is a pseudodifferential amplitude, where the analyticity of
\tilde{R}_{N}
in
(t,x,y)
is a consequence of the Paley-Wiener-Schwartz theorem.

By an application of
Lemma

4.9
, the proof of the first part of the claim
Eq.

4.22
is complete once we show

Lemma 4.11
.

1.

For each compact subset of some open complex neighborhood of
X\times X
, there exists
C>0
so that

\left\lvert R_{N}^{\prime}\right\rvert\leq C^{N+1}(1+\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert)^{\frac{n-3}{2}}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert>0\implies\left\lvert R_{N}^{\prime}\right\rvert\leq C\left(\frac{CN}{\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert}\right)^{N}\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert^{\frac{n-3}{2}}\,.

(4.23)

2.

Furthermore, there is
C_{0}>0
so that for each compact subset of some open complex neighborhood of
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times X\times X
, there exists
C_{1}>0
so that

\left\lvert\tilde{R}_{N}\right\rvert\leq C_{1}^{N+1}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert>C_{0}\implies\left\lvert\tilde{R}_{N}\right\rvert\leq C_{1}\left(\frac{C_{1}N}{\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert}\right)^{N}\,.

(4.24)

The proof of
Lemma

4.11
can be found below.

In order to prove that
(\partial_{t}^{2}+P(x,D))\tilde{q}
is analytic, ie. the second part of the claim
Eq.

4.22
, we shall proceed analogously. Let us write

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}^{2}+P(x,D))\left(e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\left(\tilde{a}^{\prime}-\sum_{j=0}^{N}\varphi_{j}\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}b^{\prime}_{N}

(4.25)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}^{2}+P(x,D))\left(e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\sum_{j=0}^{N}\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}b^{\prime\prime}_{N}

for some pseudoanalytic amplitude
b^{\prime}_{N}
(of order
\leq\frac{n-3}{2}+2
) and some
b_{N}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.

Noting that
r_{N}^{\prime\prime}
and
b^{\prime\prime}_{N}
are compactly supported with respect to
\theta
, introducing some
\gamma\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
so that
\int\gamma(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta=1
, we find

\int e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}(r_{N}^{\prime\prime}+b_{N}^{\prime\prime})\mathrm{d}\theta=\int e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\underbrace{\gamma(\theta)e^{-i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\langle(r_{N}^{\prime\prime}+b_{N}^{\prime\prime})(x,y,\xi),e^{i\xi(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\rangle_{\xi}}_{\eqqcolon R_{N}^{\prime\prime}}\mathrm{d}\theta\,,

which allows us to write

(\partial_{t}^{2}+P(x,D))\tilde{q}(t,x,y)=\int e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\big(r_{N}^{\prime}(x,y,\theta)+b^{\prime}_{N}(x,y,\theta)-R_{N}^{\prime\prime}\big)\mathrm{d}\theta\,,

where we interpret
\int\mathrm{d}\theta
as true integration over
\theta
by enforcing

N>\frac{n-3}{2}+3\,,

which guarantees that the pseudoanalytic amplitudes
r_{N}^{\prime},b_{N}^{\prime}
have negative enough order.

Again, we want to show that the sequence
r_{N}^{\prime}+b^{\prime}_{N}-R_{N}^{\prime\prime}
satisfies the assumptions of
Lemma

4.9
to conclude that
(\partial_{t}^{2}+P(x,D))\tilde{q}
is analytic.

We break up these estimates into individual statements as well, and remark that proving the following lemma completes the proof of the claim
Eq.

4.22
, which thus completes the proof of
Lemma

4.5
. The proof of the following lemma is found after the proof of
Lemma

4.11
.

Lemma 4.12
.

For each compact subset of some open complex neighborhood of
X\times X
, there exists
C>0
so that

1.

we have

\left\lvert r_{N}^{\prime}\right\rvert\leq C^{N+1}(1+\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert)^{\frac{n-3}{2}}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert>0\implies\left\lvert r_{N}^{\prime}\right\rvert\leq C\left(\frac{CN}{\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert}\right)^{N}\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert^{\frac{n-3}{2}}\,,

(4.26)

2.

and

\left\lvert b_{N}^{\prime}\right\rvert\leq C^{N+1}(1+\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert)^{\frac{n+1}{2}}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert>0\implies\left\lvert b_{N}^{\prime}\right\rvert\leq C\left(\frac{CN}{\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert}\right)^{N}\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert^{\frac{n+1}{2}}\,.

(4.27)

3.

Furthermore, there is
C_{0}>0
so that for each compact subset of some open complex neighborhood of
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times X\times X
, there exists
C_{1}>0
so that

\left\lvert R_{N}^{\prime\prime}\right\rvert\leq C_{1}^{N+1}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert>C_{0}\implies\left\lvert R_{N}^{\prime\prime}\right\rvert\leq C_{1}\left(\frac{C_{1}N}{\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert}\right)^{N}\,.

(4.28)

∎

Proof of
Lemma

4.11
.

Recalling the definition of
R_{N}^{\prime}=\tilde{a}^{\prime}-\sum_{j=0}^{N}\varphi_{j}\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime}
, the estimate
Eq.

4.23
is a consequence of
Lemma

4.10
.

We move on to show
Eq.

4.24
. Recall that

\tilde{R}_{N}(t,x,y,\theta)=\gamma(\theta)e^{-i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\left\langle\sum_{j=0}^{N}\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime\prime}(x,y,\xi),e^{i\xi(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\right\rangle_{\xi}\,.

Because
\gamma
is compactly supported,
\tilde{R}_{N}
satisfies the second part of
Eq.

4.24
, and it suffices to prove the first part of
Eq.

4.24
for each term
\left\langle\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime\prime}(x,y,\xi),e^{i\xi(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\right\rangle_{\xi}
, because
\gamma(\theta)e^{-i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}
admits some bound independent of
N
.

Let
j\in\{0,\dots,N\}
be arbitrary, and recall the definition of the expressions
\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime\prime}
from
Lemma

4.4
. According to the Paley-Wiener-Schwartz theorem
[
Hör03
, Thm. 7.3.1]
(
\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime\prime}
is supported in
\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert\leq 2j\rho
), we indeed have the desired bound (the first part of
Eq.

4.24
) on each individual
\tilde{R}_{j}
: however, we have no a priori control on the constant
C
in
[
Hör03
, Eq. (7.3.2)]
. Nevertheless, the cases
0\leq j\leq\frac{n-3}{2}
have been taken care of and we may assume that
j>\frac{n-3}{2}
.

We shall denote by
\bar{j}
the largest integer so that
j-\bar{j}>\frac{n-3}{2}
. According to
[
Hör03
, Eq. (3.2.12)]
, we have

(\xi-i0)^{-j+\frac{n-3}{2}}=\partial^{\bar{j}}(\xi-i0)^{-j+\bar{j}+\frac{n-3}{2}}c_{\bar{j}}^{-1}\,,\qquad\text{where}\quad c_{j}=\prod_{k=0}^{\bar{j}-1}\left(\frac{n-3}{2}-j+\bar{j}-k\right)\,,

(4.29)

and because
\frac{n-3}{2}-j+\bar{j}\in\{-1/2,-1\}
, we see that
\left\lvert c_{\bar{j}}\right\rvert\geq(\bar{j}-2)!
. As a consequence of
Lemma

A.1
there is some
C>0
depending only on
j-\bar{j}+2\in\{\frac{n}{2},\frac{n-1}{2}\}
, so that

\frac{j!}{\left\lvert c_{\bar{j}}\right\rvert}\leq\frac{(\bar{j}-2+(j-\bar{j}+2))!}{(\bar{j}-2)!}\leq C^{j}\,.

(4.30)

According to the Leibnitz rule and
Eq.

4.29
, for any
g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, we have

\langle(1-\varphi_{j}(\xi))(\xi-i0)^{-j+\frac{n-3}{2}},g(\xi)\rangle=\left\langle\sum_{k=0}^{\bar{j}}{\bar{j}\choose k}\left(\partial^{\bar{j}-k}(1-\varphi_{j}(\xi))\right)c_{\bar{j}}^{-1}(\xi-i0)^{-j+\bar{j}+\frac{n-3}{2}},\partial^{k}g(\xi)\right\rangle\,,

and because
\varphi_{j}(\xi)=\tilde{\varphi}_{j}(\xi/(j\rho))
,
\tilde{\varphi}_{j}
an Ehrenpreis cut-off sequence, we have, for some
C>0
(independent of
j
),

\sum_{k=0}^{\bar{j}}{\bar{j}\choose k}\partial^{\bar{j}-k}(1-\varphi_{j}(\xi))\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\bar{j}}{\bar{j}\choose k}C^{\bar{j}-k}\leq(2C)^{\bar{j}}\,.

In particular, taking
C_{0}
the maximum of the two constants
C
appearing in
[
Hör03
, Eq. (7.3.2)]
for the distributions
\mathbb{1}_{\{\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert\leq 2j\rho\}}(\xi-i0)^{-k}\,,k\in\{-1/2,-1\}
, we find that for all
z\in\mathbb{C}
,

\left\lvert\langle(1-\varphi_{j}(\xi))(\xi-i0)^{-j+\frac{n-3}{2}},e^{i\xi z}\rangle_{\xi}\right\rvert\leq(2C)^{\bar{j}}\left\lvert c_{\bar{j}}^{-1}\right\rvert C_{0}e^{2j\rho\left\lvert\mathrm{Im}(z)\right\rvert}\,.

Therefore, we find from
Eq.

4.8
, for some other
C,C^{\prime}>0
(independent of
j
) and all
z\in\mathbb{C}
,

\left\lvert\langle\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime\prime}(x,y,\xi),e^{i\xi z}\rangle_{\xi}\right\rvert\leq C^{j}j!c_{\bar{j}}^{-1}e^{2j\rho\left\lvert\mathrm{Im}(z)\right\rvert}\leq\left(C^{\prime}e^{2\rho\left\lvert\mathrm{Im}(z)\right\rvert}\right)^{j+1}\,,

where the second inequality came as a consequence of
Eq.

4.30
. Plugging in
z=t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2}
, where we may choose the complex neighborhood of
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times X\times X
so that
\left\lvert\mathrm{Im}(z)\right\rvert\leq\varepsilon
for some
\varepsilon>0
, and by summing up the terms over
j\in\{0,\dots,N\}
, we thus conclude that
\tilde{R}_{N}
satisfies
Eq.

4.24
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

4.12
.

Looking at the definition of
r_{N}^{\prime}
in
Lemma

4.4
, the second part of
Eq.

4.26
is an immediate consequence of
Eq.

4.8
. For the first part we merely remark that
r_{N}^{\prime}
is supported in
\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert\geq N\rho
, so that the first part of
Eq.

4.26
follows from the second.

Recalling the definition of
b_{N}^{\prime}
in
Eq.

4.25
(and the definition of
R_{N}^{\prime}
), the proof of
Eq.

4.27
is analogous to the proof of
Eq.

4.23
: it will be a consequence of
Lemma

4.10
.

All that remains to prove is statement 3., which, however, is entirely analogous to the proof of
Eq.

4.24
.
∎

We have thus completed the proofs of
Lemmas

4.11
and
4.12
, so that indeed the proof of
Lemma

4.5
is complete.

We turn our attention to
Lemma

4.6
.

Proof of
Lemma

4.6
.

Recall that for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

q=\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}\left(-\sum_{j=0}^{N}a_{j}^{\prime\prime}+a^{\prime}-\sum_{j=0}^{N}\varphi_{j}a_{j}^{\prime}\right)-e^{i\theta(t^{2}-d_{g}(x,y)^{2})}\left(-\sum_{j=0}^{N}\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime\prime}+\tilde{a}^{\prime}-\sum_{j=0}^{N}\varphi_{j}\tilde{a}_{j}^{\prime}\right)\mathrm{d}\theta\,.

The proof of this lemma is analogous to the proof of the analyticity of
\tilde{q}
for
t<0
in
Lemma

4.5
aside from the following:
we have to make sure to exclude the diagonal
x=y
and restrict to
t\geq 0
, as otherwise the amplitudes
a^{\prime},a_{j}^{\prime}
are not guaranteed to be analytic (as well as the phase
\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))
).

Despite the fact that
Lemma

4.9
is stated for distributions defined by an oscillatory integral with only one phase, it is easy to see that one may repeat the proof in the case of the sum of multiple phases as they occur in
q
, and indeed, in
Lemma

4.11
we have already shown bounds for the second group of terms in
q
(the ones with tildes). One will thus only have to show bounds on the amplitudes that we group as follows:

a^{\prime}-\sum_{j=0}^{N}\varphi_{j}a_{j}^{\prime}\,,\qquad\sum_{j=0}^{N}e^{-i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}\gamma(\theta)\langle a_{j}^{\prime\prime},e^{i\xi(t-d_{g}(x,y))}\rangle_{\xi}\,,

where naturally, we took
\gamma\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with
\int\gamma(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta=1
. The first term is dealt with using
Lemma

4.10
. The arguments to estimate
R_{N}^{\prime}
in the proof of
Lemma

4.11
can be repeated for the term coming from
a_{j}^{\prime\prime}
.

These estimates complete the proof of this lemma.
∎

We now proceed to the

Proof of
Lemma

4.7
.

From the calculation in
Eqs.

4.14
and
4.15
, namely the application of Taylor’s theorem, for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, where the argument of each
a^{\prime}
is
(d_{g}(x,y),x,y,\theta)
, and the argument of each
R_{N+1}
is
(t,x,y,\theta)
,

\displaystyle\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}a^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle=\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{N}\frac{\partial_{t}^{j}a^{\prime}}{j!}(t-d_{g}(x,y))^{j}+R_{N+1}(t-d_{g}(x,y))^{N+1}\right)\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle=\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{N}\varphi_{j}\frac{\partial_{t}^{j}a^{\prime}}{j!}(t-d_{g}(x,y))^{j}+\sum_{j=0}^{N}(1-\varphi_{j})\frac{\partial_{t}^{j}a^{\prime}}{j!}(t-d_{g}(x,y))^{j}+R_{N+1}(t-d_{g}(x,y))^{N+1}\right)\mathrm{d}\theta

so that using partial integration on the first term on the RHS, and recalling
a_{j}^{\circ}
from
Eq.

4.16
,

\displaystyle=\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{N}\sum_{k=0}^{j}{j\choose k}(\partial_{\theta}^{j-k}\varphi_{j})\frac{\partial_{\theta}^{k}\partial_{t}^{j}a^{\prime}}{j!(-1)^{j}}+\sum_{j=0}^{N}(1-\varphi_{j})\frac{\partial_{t}^{j}a^{\prime}}{j!}(t-d_{g}(x,y))^{j}+R_{N+1}(t-d_{g}(x,y))^{N+1}\right)\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle=\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{N}\varphi_{j}a_{j}^{\circ}+\sum_{j=0}^{N}\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}{j\choose k}(\partial_{\theta}^{j-k}\varphi_{j})\frac{\partial_{\theta}^{k}\partial_{t}^{j}a^{\prime}}{j!(-1)^{j}}+\sum_{j=0}^{N}(1-\varphi_{j})\frac{\partial_{t}^{j}a^{\prime}}{j!}(t-d_{g}(x,y))^{j}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad+R_{N+1}(t-d_{g}(x,y))^{N+1}\Bigg)\mathrm{d}\theta\,.

Splitting up
R_{N+1}=\varphi_{N+1}R_{N+1}+(1-\varphi_{N+1})R_{N+1}
and applying partial integration as well, we see that

\displaystyle\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}R_{N+1}(t-d_{g}(x,y))^{N+1}\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle=\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}\left((1-\varphi_{N+1})R_{N+1}(t-d_{g}(x,y))^{N+1}+(-1)^{N+1}\partial_{\theta}^{N+1}(\varphi_{N+1}R_{N+1})\right)\mathrm{d}\theta\,.

Now, because
a^{\circ}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varphi_{j}a_{j}^{\circ}
, from the calculations above we see that

\displaystyle\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}(a^{\prime}-a^{\circ})\mathrm{d}\theta

(4.31)

\displaystyle=\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{g}(x,y))}\left(\overbrace{-\sum_{j=N+1}^{\infty}\varphi_{j}a_{j}^{\circ}}^{T_{1,N}}+\overbrace{\sum_{j=0}^{N}\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}{j\choose k}(\partial_{\theta}^{j-k}\varphi_{j})\frac{\partial_{\theta}^{k}\partial_{t}^{j}a^{\prime}}{j!(-1)^{j}}}^{T_{2,N}}+\overbrace{\sum_{j=0}^{N}(1-\varphi_{j})\frac{\partial_{t}^{j}a^{\prime}}{j!}(t-d_{g}(x,y))^{j}}^{T_{3,N}}\right.

\displaystyle+\left.\underbrace{(1-\varphi_{N+1})R_{N+1}(t-d_{g}(x,y))^{N+1}}_{T_{4,N}}-(-1)^{N}\left(\underbrace{\varphi_{N+1}\partial_{\theta}^{N+1}R_{N+1}}_{T_{5,N}}+\underbrace{\sum_{k=0}^{N}{N+1\choose k}(\partial_{\theta}^{N+1-k}\varphi_{N+1})\partial_{\theta}^{k}R_{N+1}}_{T_{6,N}}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}\theta\,.

We will want to apply
Lemma

4.9
to prove this lemma. Thus it will suffice to show that the six terms in the integrand on the RHS of
Eq.

4.31
,
T_{l,N},l\in\{1,\dots,6\}
satisfy the assumptions of
Lemma

4.9
. That for each fixed
N
each term
T_{l,N},l\in\{1,\dots,6\}
is in fact a pseudoanalytic amplitude is immediate from the properties of the cut-offs
\varphi_{j}
and the fact that
a^{\prime}
and each
a^{\circ}_{j}
is a pseudoanalytic amplitude.

It is understood that the following estimates are all valid on some compact subset of some open complex neighborhood of
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times X\times X\setminus\mathrm{d}
.

1.

The term
T_{1,N}
satisfies
Eqs.

4.18
and
4.19
by an application of
Lemma

4.10
.

2.

Notice that in every term in
T_{2,N}
at least one derivative will hit
\varphi_{j}
, which makes
T_{2,N}
supported in
\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert\leq N\rho
, so that we can conclude that
T_{2,N}
satisfies
Eq.

4.19
. In addition, using the pseudoanalytic estimates of
a^{\prime}
, for some
C>0
, we can estimate

\left\lvert\sum_{j=0}^{N}\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}{j\choose k}(\partial_{\theta}^{j-k}\varphi_{j})\frac{\partial_{\theta}^{k}\partial_{t}^{j}a^{\prime}}{j!}\right\rvert\leq\sum_{j=0}^{N}\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}\frac{j!}{(j-k)!}C^{j-k}C^{k+j+1}\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert^{-k}\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert^{\frac{n-3}{2}}\,,

where we used the fact that
(\tilde{\varphi}_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of Ehrenpreis cut-offs and thus (for
C
depending on
\rho
)

\left\lvert\partial_{\theta}^{j-k}\varphi_{j}(\theta)\right\rvert=\left\lvert\partial_{\theta}^{j-k}\tilde{\varphi}_{j}(\theta/(j\rho))\right\rvert\leq C^{j-k}\,.

Now on the support of
\varphi_{j}
, for some
C,C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}>0
,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}\frac{j!}{(j-k)!}C^{j-k}C^{k+j+1}\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert^{-k}

\displaystyle=C^{2j+1}\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}\frac{j!}{k!}\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert^{-j+k}\leq C^{2j+1}\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}\frac{j!}{k!}(j\rho)^{-j+k}

\displaystyle=C^{2j+1}(j\rho)^{-j}\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}\frac{j!}{k!}(j\rho)^{k}\leq C^{2j+1}2^{n}

where we applied
Lemma

A.2
in the last inequality. Thus, for some
C>0
,

\left\lvert T_{2,N}\right\rvert\leq\sum_{j=0}^{N}C^{j+2}\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert^{\frac{n-3}{2}}

and since
T_{2,N}
is supported in
\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert\geq\rho
, we find that
T_{2,N}
also satisfies
Eq.

4.18
.

3.

The term
T_{3,N}
has support in
\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert\leq 2N\rho
and thus satisfies
Eq.

4.19
. In addition, this term can be estimated by

\left\lvert\sum_{j=0}^{N}(1-\varphi_{j}(\theta))a^{\circ}_{j}\right\rvert\leq\sum_{j=0}^{N}C^{j+1}\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert^{\frac{n-3}{2}}\leq(C^{\prime})^{N}\,,

for some
C,C^{\prime}>0
, so that it satisfies
Eq.

4.18
.

4.

The term
T_{4,N}
satisfies
Eq.

4.19
because it is supported in
\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert\leq 2(N+1)\rho\leq 4N\rho
. Furthermore, by explicit observation of the definition of
R_{N+1}
from
Eq.

4.15
,

\left\lvert R_{N+1}\right\rvert\leq\frac{1}{N!}C^{N+1}(N+1)!\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)^{N}\mathrm{d}s\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert^{\frac{n-3}{2}}\leq C^{N+1}\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert^{\frac{n-3}{2}}\,,

and since
R_{N+1}
is supported in
\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert>\rho
, we see that
T_{4,N}
satisfies
Eq.

4.18
too.

5.

For the term
T_{5,N}
, notice that for some
C,C^{\prime}>0
,

\left\lvert\varphi_{N+1}\partial^{N+1}_{\theta}R_{N+1}\right\rvert\leq\frac{1}{N!}C^{N+2}(N+1)!N!\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)^{N}\mathrm{d}s\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert^{\frac{n-3}{2}-N}\leq C^{\prime}\left(\frac{C^{\prime}N}{\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert}\right)^{N}

which thus satisfies
Eq.

4.19
. Because
T_{5,N}
is supported in
\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert\geq(N+1)\rho\geq N\rho
, it also satisfies
Eq.

4.18
from this above calculation.

6.

We turn to the term
T_{6,N}
. Notice that in every term in
T_{6,N}
at least one derivative will hit
\varphi_{N+1}
, which makes
T_{6,N}
supported in
\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert\leq 2(N+1)\rho\leq 4N\rho
, so that we can conclude that
T_{6,N}
satisfies
Eq.

4.19
.

Showing that
T_{6,N}
satisfies
Eq.

4.18
proceeds analogously to the treatment of
T_{2,N}
.

We have thus concluded that all six terms in
Eq.

4.31
satisfy
Eqs.

4.18
and
4.19
which completes the proof of this lemma.
∎

Finally, we shall complete this section with the

Proof of
Lemma

4.8
.

Because

\varphi_{j}\varphi_{k}=\begin{cases}0\quad&\text{if}\quad\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert\leq(j+k)\frac{\rho}{2}\\
1\quad&\text{if}\quad\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert\geq(j+k)\rho\\
\ast&\text{else}\end{cases}\,,

in particular, if
s=j+k
,

\mathrm{supp}\left(\varphi_{j}\varphi_{k}-\varphi_{s}\right)\subset\left\{s\frac{\rho}{2}\leq\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert\leq s\rho\right\}\,,

so that

a^{\circ}-a=\sum_{s\frac{\rho}{2}\leq\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert\leq s\rho}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\geq 0\\
j+k=s\end{subarray}}\left(\varphi_{j}\left(\frac{\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert}{j\rho}\right)\varphi_{k}\left(\frac{\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert}{k\rho}\right)-\varphi_{s}\left(\frac{\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert}{s(\rho/2)}\right)\right)\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j!}\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{t}^{j}a^{\prime}_{k}\,.

(4.32)

Thus, the RHS of
Eq.

4.32
is a locally finite sum of terms bounded above by

M\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert^{n-1}C^{s}s!\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert^{-s}\leq M\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert^{n-1}\left(\frac{Cs}{\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert}\right)^{s}\,,

where if
\rho
large enough so that
2C/\rho<e^{-1}
, this can be bounded by

\leq M\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert^{n-1}e^{-s}\leq M\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert^{n-1}e^{-\left\lvert\theta\right\rvert/\rho}\,.

This implies that
a^{\circ}-a
is an exponentially decaying pseudoanalytic amplitude, completing the proof.
∎

5
Seismic Inversion

We consider a problem arising in exploration seismology, the physical setup of which is as follows: at the surface of the earth one detonates a charge at one point inducing acoustic waves traveling through the earth underground. These waves have singularities propagating at the wavefront and will thus reflect off of differences in material undergound, allowing these reflections to be measured at the surface of the earth again. This is the situation considered in
[
Rak88
]
, and
[
KSV98
]
which is made more rigorous and expanded on in
[
Sto00
]
. See also
[
Han91
,
Bey85
,
SD06
]
, and for the one-dimensional case, also containing a stability statement, we refer to
[
LS91
]
. A more general setup of this problem is when multiple sources of the detonations lie in a manifold, see for example
[
NS97
]
or
[
Fel+22
]
and the references therein, which will not be discussed here. We also point the reader to the survey article
[
Sym09
]
. We will be discussing this problem in an analytic setting.

Let
c\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
be the wave speed and consider the forward problem of finding a solution
u=u(t,x)
to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c(x)^{2}}\partial_{t}^{2}u-\Delta_{x}u

\displaystyle=\delta(t,x)\,,\qquad\mathrm{supp}u\subset\{t\geq 0\}\,.

(5.1)

We are interested in the inverse problem of recovering the wave speed
c
based on surface measurements of the solution
u
, ie. recovering
c
with data
u|_{x_{n}=0}
.
Motivated by this – deferring to
[
Rak88
, § 1]
the well-posedness under assumptions we will also make –
we define the forward map

A\colon c\mapsto u|_{x_{n}=0}\,.

(5.2)

In fact we will simplify the problem by considering the linearization
DA[c]
of the operator
A
, defined in
Eq.

5.6
.

In order to state our results on the injectivity of this operator we fix a few expressions. As we assume that measurements are made in a bounded set over a finite amount of time we will restrict ourselves to this situation.

Let open
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be bounded with
0\in\mathcal{M}
. We shall assume there are open
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
T_{\mathcal{M}}>0
so that
(0,T_{\mathcal{M}})\times\mathcal{M}\subset D_{+}(2T_{\mathcal{M}},\Omega)
(defined prior to
Theorem

4.1
). We define

\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\left\{c\in C^{\omega}(\Omega)\colon\inf_{\Omega}c>0\,,\text{and}\ (\mathcal{M},g)\ \text{is a simple Riemannian manifold where}\ g_{jk}(x)\coloneqq c^{2}(x)\delta_{jk}\right\}\,.

(5.3)

See
[
PSU23
, § 3.8]
for a definition and properties of simple manifolds.

When
c\in\mathcal{C}
, we will in fact abuse notation and write
(\mathcal{M},c)
for the manifold
\mathcal{M}
with metric
g_{jk}(x)=c^{2}(x)\delta_{jk}
, and we will also write
d_{c}
for the Riemannian distance function with this metric. We shall assume also that
T_{\mathcal{M}}>2\mathrm{diam}_{c}(\mathcal{M})
.

Finally, let us fix
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathcal{M}\cap\{x_{n}=0\}
and for any
\varepsilon>0
we define
\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\mathcal{M}\cap\{x_{n}<-\varepsilon\}
. The set
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is the
measurement surface
, it is where in a physical setup one would have seismographs that measure displacement in the ground. The necessity of
\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}
arises as a technicality to guarantee that the linearization of
A
is an FIO.

Throughout this entire section the definitions made above will remain fixed.

Let us introduce ‘physical’ conditions on the geometry
(\mathcal{M},c)
for some compact
\mathcal{K}\Subset\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}\subset\mathcal{M}
.
We introduce

\displaystyle\text{there is no ray starting at $0$ passing through $\mathcal{K}$ that returns to $\mathcal{M}^{\prime}$}\,.

(A1)

\displaystyle\text{A ray from $\mathcal{K}$ hitting $\mathcal{M}^{\prime}$ has non-vanishing vertical momentum when hitting}\ \mathcal{M}^{\prime}\,.

(A2)

Condition
Eq.

A1
is that in
[
Rak88
, Assumption (iii)]
or
[
KSV98
, Thm. 2.4 (2.)]
and is sometimes referred to as no scattering over
\pi
. This condition precludes the existence of rays starting from the origin that return to
\{x_{n}=0\}
, ie. they must have scattered (aka broken) at an angle unequal to
\pi
at some point in their journey. In this formulation, however, we explicitly allow rays traveling along the surface
\{x_{n}=0\}
, or this scattering over
\pi
to happen as long as such rays never intersect the set
\mathcal{K}
.

Condition
Eq.

A2
is
[
Rak88
, Assumption (i)]
or
[
KSV98
, Thm. 2.4 (1.)]
and precludes the existence of rays that graze the surface
\{x_{n}=0\}
(again restricted to the set of interest
\mathcal{K}
).

We will have to assume
Eqs.

A1
and
A2
so that the linearization of
A
is an FIO with salutary phase (see
Definition

2.9
). However, in order to show injectivity of this linearization we will use a layer stripping argument which will require two additional conditions:

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\text{In the metric induced by $c$, for any $x\in\mathcal{K}$, the vertical momentum at $x$}\\
\text{of the ray from $0$ to $x$ is non-vanishing.}\end{matrix}

(B1)

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\text{In the metric induced by $c$, for all $x\in\mathcal{K}$, putting $(\omega^{\prime},\omega_{n})=\omega=-\exp_{x}^{-1}(0)$,}\\
\text{the ray $y(t)=\exp_{x}\left(t\left(\frac{\omega^{\prime}}{\left\lvert\omega\right\rvert}+\sqrt{1-\left\lvert\frac{\omega^{\prime}}{\left\lvert\omega\right\rvert}\right\rvert^{2}}e_{n}\right)\right)$ intersects $\mathcal{M}^{\prime}$ at some $t\in(0,\infty)$}\,.\end{matrix}

(B2)

Condition
Eq.

B1
is that the ray from the origin to some point
x\in\mathcal{K}
must still be ‘pointing downward’ as it reaches
x
. Condition
Eq.

B2
states that the ray from the origin to any
x\in\mathcal{K}
, reflected with respect to its vertical momentum (now pointing upward when before it was downward, given
Eq.

B1
) will hit the measurement surface
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
after some time. These assumptions will guarantee that we can observe the singular directions corresponding to the vectors pointing precisely upward, see the discussion near
[
KSV98
, Eq. (77)]
for which singular directions can be observed. In essence we are guaranteeing the microlocal ellipticity in the upward direction at every point. While in the smooth setting this would not suffice in order to ‘invert’ the measurement, the microlocal Holmgren theorem together with a layer stripping argument show that this suffices in the analytic framework.

We will argue in
Section

D
that if
\mathcal{M}
is a subset of the earth near the surface and
\mathcal{M}
is shallow enough (or equivalently if the initial detonation is powerful enough), then assuming a radial sound speed satisfying the Herglotz condition for the earth (see
[
PSU23
, § 2.1.2]
) will guarantee assumptions
Eqs.

B1
and
A2
within
\mathcal{M}
and will ensure that there are ‘reasonably large’ sets
\mathcal{K}
satisfying
Eqs.

B2
and
A1
.

Writing
DA[c]
for the linearization of
A
from
Eq.

5.2
in the direction
c
(see
Eq.

5.6
), we will prove the injectivity of this linearized operator, in fact, at the end of
Section

5.2
we show

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
c\in\mathcal{C}
, compact
\mathcal{K}\Subset\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon>0
and let
T\in(2\mathrm{diam}_{c}(\mathcal{M}),T_{\mathcal{M}})
and
c_{\delta}\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\mathcal{M})\cap C(\mathcal{K}),\mathrm{supp}c_{\delta}\subset\mathcal{K}
. We assume that
(\mathcal{M},c)
with
\mathcal{K}
satisfy assumptions
Eqs.

A1
,
A2
,
B1
and
B2
.

The following three conditions are equivalent

1.

DA[c](c_{\delta})\in C^{\omega}((0,T)\times\mathcal{M}^{\prime})
,

2.

c_{\delta}\equiv 0
,

3.

DA[c](c_{\delta})=0
.

This result may be interpreted as follows: the observations on the surface
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, collected over the time
(0,T)
determine the analytic singularities of the subsurface inside
\mathcal{K}
.

Let
S
be the solution operator from
Lemma

4.2
for the operator
P(x,D)=-c^{2}(x)\Delta_{x}
, and define
S_{c}
via
S_{c}(f)=S(c^{2}f)
. We see that
S_{c}
is then the solution operator for the equation
Eq.

5.1
and behaves exactly like
S
does as stated in
Lemma

4.2
. Because we assumed simplicity of the metric, reformulating the second statement of
Theorem

4.1
in the present setting we have

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
c\in\mathcal{C}
and
T\in(0,T_{\mathcal{M}})
. Writing
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\setminus d}\coloneqq\mathcal{M}^{2}\setminus\mathrm{diag}(\mathcal{M}^{2})
, there is
\mathrm{FS}_{\psi a}^{\frac{n-3}{2}}(\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\setminus d})\ni\underline{a}=\sum_{j\geq 0}a_{j}
classical and elliptic with the following property. For every finite realization
a\in S_{\psi a}^{\frac{n-3}{2}}(\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\setminus d})
of
\underline{a}
, defining
q_{c}\in{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2})
via

q_{c}(t,x,y)=\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{c}(x,y))}a(x,y,\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta

(5.4)

there is
q_{c}^{\prime}(t,x,y)\in{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2})\cap C^{\omega}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\setminus d})
so that for
(t,x,y)\in(0,T)\times\mathcal{M}^{2}
,

\displaystyle S_{c}(\delta_{(0,y)})(t,x)=(q_{c}+q_{c}^{\prime})(t,x,y)\,.

(5.5)

5.1
Derivatives of the Forward Operator

We shall follow
[
Rak88
,
KSV98
,
Sym09
]
in considering the formal linearization of
A
from
Eq.

5.2
about some
c\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M}),c>0
:

DA[c](c_{\delta})(t,y^{\prime})\coloneqq 2\partial_{t}^{2}S_{c}\left(c^{-3}c_{\delta}S_{c}(\delta_{0})\right)\Big|_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\,,

(5.6)

where
c_{\delta}\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon})
for some
\varepsilon>0
.
[
Rak88
]
shows that
DA[c]
is in fact a well-defined FIO, which we will establish again in
Theorem

5.4
.

We refer the reader to
[
Sto00a
,
KR14
]
which discuss the Gateaux or Fréchet differentiability of
A
when restricted to some spaces of initial data, but we will be content with treating
DA[c]
as a formal linearization.

When the coefficient
c
is analytic, we may use
Proposition

5.2
to give a more explicit representation of
DA[c]
.

Corollary 5.3
.

Let
c\in\mathcal{C}
,
\varepsilon>0
and
T\in(0,T_{\mathcal{M}})
.

The map

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon})\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,T)\times\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\,,\quad c_{\delta}\mapsto 2\partial_{t}^{2}\circ|_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\circ\left(S_{c}(c^{-3}c_{\delta}S_{c}(\delta_{0}))(t,y)\right)

(5.7)

is well-defined. In particular, for all
c_{\delta}\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon})
,

\displaystyle DA[c](c_{\delta})(t,y^{\prime})

\displaystyle=2\iint\partial_{t}^{2}q_{c}(t-s,y^{\prime},x)c^{-3}(x)c_{\delta}(x)q_{c}(s,x,0)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x\mod C^{\omega}((0,T)\times\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\,.

(5.8)

Proof.

Because
S_{c}(\delta_{0})
solves

\left(c^{-2}\partial_{t}^{2}-\Delta_{x}\right)S_{c}(\delta_{0})=\delta_{0}\,,

we know by
[
Hör03
, Thm. 8.3.1]
, that

WF(S_{c}(\delta_{0}))\subset\left\{(t,x,\tau,\xi)\colon c^{-2}(x)\tau^{2}=\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^{2}\right\}\cup T^{\ast}_{(0,0)}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})\setminus\{0\}\,,

so that because
c_{\delta}
is supported away from
x=0
, by
[
Hör03
, Thm. 8.2.10]
the product
c_{\delta}(x)S_{c}(\delta_{0})(t,x)
is well-defined, and has support in
t>0
(by finite speed of propagation, see for example
[
Hör07
, p. 35]
).

We show
Eq.

5.7
: we first argue the well-definedness of
Eq.

5.7
, which is realized as the composition of two distributions
K_{1},K_{2}
and a final application of
2\partial_{t}^{2}
, where

K_{2}\colon\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon})\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,T)\times\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon})\,,\quad c_{\delta}\mapsto c^{-3}(x)c_{\delta}(x)S_{c}(\delta_{0})(t,x)\,,

which is well-defined by the arguments above, and

K_{1}\colon\mathcal{E}^{\prime}((0,T)\times\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon})\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,T)\times\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\,,\quad u\mapsto S_{c}(u)(t,y^{\prime})=|_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\circ S_{c}(u)(t,y)\,.

Let us argue why
K_{1}
is well-defined: by
[
Hör03
, Thm. 8.3.1]
, for
u\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}((0,T)\times\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon})

WF(S_{c}(u))\subset\left\{(t,x,\tau,\xi)\colon c^{-2}(x)\tau^{2}=\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^{2}\right\}\cup WF(u)

where the second term on the RHS restricted to
x_{n}=0
is empty because
u
is identically
0
there, and the first term cannot contribute wavefront set that is perpendicular to
x_{n}=0
at
x_{n}=0
. This concludes arguing that
K_{1}
is well-defined.

Now, note that by the finite speed of propagation, denoting by
K_{2}(s,x,z)
the kernel of
K_{2}
, the projection
\mathrm{supp}K_{2}\ni(s,x,z)\mapsto(s,x)
is proper.

Since we let
K_{1}
act only on expressions supported in
\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}
, and
y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
lies in
\{y_{n}=0\}
, from the finite speed of propagation we gather that
K_{1}(t,y^{\prime},s,x)
, the kernel of
K_{1}
, is supported in
t-s>0
so that it admits the representation

K_{1}(t,y^{\prime},s,x)=S_{c}(\delta_{(0,x)})(t-s,y^{\prime})=(q_{c}+q_{c}^{\prime})(t-s,y^{\prime},x)\,.

Thus we may use the explicit representation
Eq.

5.4
to calculate the wavefront set of
K_{1}
, and find that (using the notation of
[
Hör03
, § 8.2]
),

WF^{\prime}(K_{1})_{((0,T)\times\mathcal{M}^{\prime})}=\emptyset.

Therefore, by the remarks before
[
Hör03
, Thm. 8.2.14]
(or see
[
Dui11
, Thm. 1.3.7]
) we may conclude that

\left[c_{\delta}\mapsto|_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\circ S_{c}(c^{-3}c_{\delta}S_{c}(\delta_{0}))\right]=K_{1}\circ K_{2}(c_{\delta})

is well-defined, from which we conclude
Eq.

5.7
.

We start proving
Eq.

5.8
. Because
K_{1}(t,y^{\prime},s,x)
is supported in
t-s>0
, and
\mathrm{sing}\ \mathrm{supp}_{a}q_{c}(t-s,y^{\prime},x)\subset\{t-s>0\}
by the explicit
Eq.

5.4
and
Proposition

2.15
, we have

\mathrm{sing}\ \mathrm{supp}_{a}\left(K_{1}(t,y^{\prime},s,x)-q_{c}(t-s,y^{\prime},x)\right)\subset\{t-s>0\}\,.

However, by
Eq.

5.5
, when
t-s>0
, we have
K_{1}(t,y^{\prime},s,x)-q_{c}(t-s,y^{\prime},x)=q_{c}^{\prime}(t-s,y^{\prime},x)
which is analytic because
x
is away from
y^{\prime}
. Therefore,

K_{1}(t,y^{\prime},s,x)-q_{c}(t-s,y^{\prime},x)\in C^{\omega}((0,T)\times\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\times(0,T)\times\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon})\,.

(5.9)

By
Eq.

5.9
, the operator with kernel defined by
K_{1}-q_{c}
is analytically regularizing (
[
Tre22
, Def. 17.1.17]
), so that by
[
Tre22
, Prop. 17.1.18]
modulo a term in
C^{\omega}((0,T)\times\mathcal{M}^{\prime})
, we have

K_{1}\circ K_{2}(c_{\delta})(t,y^{\prime})=\iint q_{c}(t-s,y^{\prime},x)c^{-3}c_{\delta}(x)S_{c}(\delta_{0})(s,x)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x\,.

We find that modulo a term in
C^{\omega}((0,T)\times\mathcal{M}^{\prime})
,

K_{1}\circ K_{2}(c_{\delta})(t,y^{\prime})=\iint q_{c}(t-s,y^{\prime},x)c^{-3}(x)q_{c}(s,x,0)c_{\delta}(x)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x+\iint q_{c}(t-s,y^{\prime},x)h(s,x)c_{\delta}(x)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x\,,

(5.10)

where (arguing as for
Eq.

5.9
)

h(s,x)\coloneqq c^{-3}(x)S_{c}(\delta_{0})(s,x)-c^{-3}(x)q_{c}(s,x,0)\in C^{\omega}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon})\,.

(5.11)

Using the explicit expression of
q_{c}
from
Eq.

5.4
, performing the substitution
s\mapsto t-d_{c}(x,y^{\prime})-s
, we have

\int q_{c}(t-s,y^{\prime},x)h(s,x)\mathrm{d}s=-\iint e^{i\theta s}a(x,y,\theta)h(t-d_{c}(x,y^{\prime})-s,x)\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}s\,,

where one sees from
Corollary

2.16
that the RHS is an analytic function of
(t,y^{\prime},x)
in
C^{\omega}((0,T)\times\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon})
, because
h\in C^{\omega}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon})
by
Eq.

5.11
. Therefore, the kernel of the operator giving rise to the second term on the RHS of
Eq.

5.10
is analytically regularizing and we find

K_{1}\circ K_{2}(c_{\delta})(t,y^{\prime})=\iint q_{c}(t-s,y^{\prime},x)c^{-3}(x)c_{\delta}(x)q_{c}(s,x,0)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x\mod C^{\omega}((0,T)\times\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\,,

so that, in fact, we may conclude
Eq.

5.8
from
Eq.

5.6
.
∎

The next section is dedicated to studying the linearization
DA[c]
when
c
is analytic.

5.2
The Linearization as an Analytic FIO

We will now prove the analytic version of
[
Rak88
, Thm. B]
, see also
[
KSV98
, Thm. 2.4]
.

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
c\in\mathcal{C}
and
c_{\delta}\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\mathcal{K})
for some
\mathcal{K}\Subset\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon},\varepsilon>0
so that
(\mathcal{M},g)
satisfies
Eqs.

A1
and
A2
for
\mathcal{K}
, and let
T\in(0,T_{\mathcal{M}})
.

There is
\mathrm{FS}^{n-1}_{\psi a}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{K})\ni\underline{b}=\sum_{j\geq 0}b_{j}
classical and elliptic so that for any finite realization
b
of
\underline{b}
, for
0<t<T,y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
,

DA[c](c_{\delta})(t,y^{\prime})=\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{c}(y^{\prime},x)-d_{c}(x,0))}b(y^{\prime},x,\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta c_{\delta}(x)\mathrm{d}x\mod C^{\omega}((0,T)\times\mathcal{M}^{\prime})

(5.12)

which (as the kernel of a distribution in
((0,T)\times\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\times\mathcal{K}
) is an FIO with non-degenerate analytic salutary phase according to
Definition

2.9
, with canonical relation satisfying
Eq.

3.2
.

Proof.

Let
t>0
and let
y=(y^{\prime},0)\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, where we interpret
y^{\prime}=(y^{\prime},0)\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
by abuse of notation. Throughout this proof, when writing
\equiv
, we shall mean equality modulo
C^{\omega}((0,T)\times\mathcal{M}^{\prime})
.

According to
Eq.

5.8
, and the explicit formula for
q_{c}
from
Eq.

5.4
, we have

DA[c](c_{\delta})(t,y^{\prime})\equiv 2\iiiint e^{i\theta((t-s)-d_{c}(y^{\prime},x))+i\sigma(s-d_{c}(x,0))}a(y^{\prime},x,\theta)i^{2}\theta^{2}c^{-3}(x)a(x,0,\sigma)c_{\delta}(x)\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x\,,

and since only the phase depends on
s
, we identify that the integral over
s
is in fact
(2\pi)\delta_{0}(\theta-\sigma)
, which gives

DA[c](c_{\delta})(t,y^{\prime})\equiv-4\pi\iint e^{i\theta(t-d_{c}(y^{\prime},x)-d_{c}(x,0))}a(y^{\prime},x,\theta)\theta^{2}c^{-3}(x)a(x,0,\theta)c_{\delta}(x)\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}x\,.

(5.13)

Notice that each
a
in the integrand of
Eq.

5.13
is the finite realization of
\underline{a}=\sum_{k\geq 0}a_{k}\in\mathrm{FS}_{\psi a}^{\frac{n-3}{2}}(\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\setminus d})
defined in
Proposition

5.2
. We introduce

b_{s}(y^{\prime},x,\theta)\coloneqq-4\pi\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\geq 0\\
j+k=s\end{subarray}}c^{-3}(x)\theta^{2}a_{j}(y^{\prime},x,\theta)a_{k}(x,0,\theta)\in S^{n-1-s}_{\psi a}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{K})\,,\quad\underline{b}\coloneqq\sum_{s\geq 0}b_{s}\in\mathrm{FS}^{n-1}_{\psi a}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{K})

and will want to show that we can replace the amplitude in
Eq.

5.13
by a finite realization of
\underline{b}
. In fact, one may repeat the proof of
Lemma

4.8
(found in
Section

4.1
) together with an application of
[
Tre22
, Prop. 17.1.19]
to find that indeed, for
b
a finite realization of
\underline{b}
, we have

DA[c_{0}](c_{\delta})(t,y^{\prime})\equiv\iint e^{i\theta(t-d_{c}(y^{\prime},x)-d_{c}(x,0))}b(y^{\prime},x,\theta)c_{\delta}(x)\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}x\,,

(5.14)

with
b_{0}
non-vanishing as long as
\theta
away from
0
and
x
away from
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, which is guaranteed by
x\in\mathrm{supp}c_{\delta}
.

The phase
\theta(t-d_{c}(y^{\prime},x)-d_{c}(x,0))
is non-degenerate since there is only one frequency variable and for example
\partial_{t}\partial_{\theta}\theta(t-d_{c}(y^{\prime},x)-d_{c}(x,0))=1\neq 0
. It is analytic because we assumed that
c_{\delta}
vanishes for
x_{n}
near
0
, so that
d_{c}(y^{\prime},x)
and
d_{c}(x,0)
are analytic.

We now check that the phase of the FIO in
Eq.

5.14
is salutary, where we will use that
\nabla_{x}d_{c}(x,y^{\prime})=-\frac{\exp_{x}^{-1}(y^{\prime})}{d_{c}(x,y^{\prime})}
and similar when differentiating with respect to
y^{\prime}
.

Notice first that the stationary set of the phase and canonical relation of the FIO (in
Eq.

5.14
) are given by, respectively,

\displaystyle\Sigma

\displaystyle=\left\{(t,y^{\prime},x,\theta)\in(0,T)\times\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{K}\times\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}\colon t=d_{c}(y^{\prime},x)+d_{c}(x,0)\right\}\,,

(5.15)

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle=\left\{\left((t,y^{\prime}),\left(\theta,\theta\frac{(\exp_{y^{\prime}}^{-1}(x))^{\prime}}{d_{c}(y^{\prime},x)}\right);x,-\theta\left(\frac{\exp_{x}^{-1}(y^{\prime})}{d_{c}(y^{\prime},x)}+\frac{\exp_{x}^{-1}(0)}{d_{c}(x,0)}\right)\right)\colon(t,y^{\prime},x,\theta)\in\Sigma\right\}\,,

where by
(\exp_{y^{\prime}}^{-1}(x))^{\prime}
we mean the first
n-1
components of the vector
\exp_{y^{\prime}}^{-1}(x)
. See
Fig.

2
for a sketch of the roles that each component of
\Lambda
plays.

x_{n}=0
y^{\prime}
x_{n}<0
x
0
\sigma_{o}
\sigma_{i}
\omega_{o}
\omega_{i}
s
t-s

Figure 2:
The points
0,x
and
y^{\prime}
marked in space. The arrows indicate the (outgoing and incoming) momenta
\sigma_{o}=\exp_{0}^{-1}(x)
and
\sigma_{i}=-\exp_{x}^{-1}(0)
for the first part of the broken ray. The arrows marked
\omega_{0}
and
\omega_{i}
are the analogous momenta for the second part of the ray. The symbol
s
indicates the time
s=d_{c}(x,0)
to travel from
0
to
x
. Note that if
(x,\xi)\in\pi_{R}\Lambda
, then (after unit normalization of
\sigma_{i},\omega_{o}
) we have
\xi=\sigma_{i}-\omega_{o}
, the ‘reflection’ angle, in some sense. Furthermore, if
(t,y^{\prime},\tau,\zeta^{\prime})\in\pi_{L}\Lambda
, then
\zeta^{\prime}=-\omega_{i}^{\prime}
(or a scalar multiple thereof).

First we note that since
t>0
, on the support of
c_{\delta}
(
x
away from the surface
\{y_{n}=0\}
), the set
\Lambda
is a well-defined analytic canonical relation corresponding to the phase
\theta(t-d_{c}(y^{\prime},x)-d_{c}(x,0))
.

We now proceed to check that
\Lambda
and the phase
\theta(t-d_{c}(y^{\prime},x)-d_{c}(x,0))
are salutary according to
Definitions

2.8
and
2.9
and that
Eq.

3.2
holds for
\Lambda
. Let
(t,y^{\prime},x,\theta)\in\Sigma
.

1.

To check
Eq.

3.2
we must verify that
\frac{\exp_{x}^{-1}(y^{\prime})}{d_{c}(y^{\prime},x)}+\frac{\exp_{x}^{-1}(0)}{d_{c}(x,0)}\neq 0
. Note that for two vectors
u,v
with
\left\lvert v\right\rvert=\left\lvert u\right\rvert=1
we have

u+v=0\iff\langle u+v,u+v\rangle=0\iff 2\langle u,v\rangle=-2\iff\langle u,v\rangle=-1\iff\arccos(\langle u,v\rangle)=\pi\,.

Therefore, the failure of
Eq.

3.2
is equivalent to the statement that the reflection angle

\arccos\left(\left\langle\frac{\exp_{x}^{-1}(y^{\prime})}{d_{c}(y^{\prime},x)},\frac{\exp_{x}^{-1}(0)}{d_{c}(x,0)}\right\rangle\right)=\pi\,,

which is precluded by
Eq.

A1
(because from
(t,y^{\prime},x,\theta)\in\Sigma
we know that
x_{n}<0
).

2.

We see immediately that

ph

4.
is satisfied since the time-derivative of the phase is
\theta
, the frequency.

3.

The truth of

ph

3.
is implied by the fact that
\left(\theta,\theta\frac{(\exp_{y^{\prime}}^{-1}(x))^{\prime}}{d_{c}(y^{\prime},x)}\right)\neq 0
for
\theta\neq 0
.

4.

Checking

ph

2.
reduces to showing that
\frac{\exp_{x}^{-1}(y^{\prime})}{d_{c}(y^{\prime},x)}+\frac{\exp_{x}^{-1}(0)}{d_{c}(x,0)}\neq 0
, which, exactly as in checking
Eq.

3.2
, is precluded by
Eq.

A1
.

Thus, the proof of
Theorem

5.4
is complete once we prove

Lemma 5.5
.

The canonical relation
\Lambda
defined in
Eq.

5.15
satisfies the Bolker condition,

ph

1.
.

∎

Proof of
Lemma

5.5
.

We begin with the injectivity of
\pi_{L}
.

Let

\left(d_{c}(y^{\prime},x_{0})+d_{c}(x_{0},0),y^{\prime},\theta,\theta\frac{(\exp_{y^{\prime}}^{-1}(x_{0}))^{\prime}}{d_{c}(y^{\prime},x_{0})};x_{0},-\theta\left(\frac{\exp_{x_{0}}^{-1}(y^{\prime})}{d_{c}(y^{\prime},x_{0})}+\frac{\exp_{x_{0}}^{-1}(0)}{d_{c}(x_{0},0)}\right)\right)\in\Lambda

and

\left(d_{c}(y^{\prime},x_{1})+d_{c}(x_{1},0),y^{\prime},\theta,\theta\frac{(\exp_{y^{\prime}}^{-1}(x_{1}))^{\prime}}{d_{c}(y^{\prime},x_{1})};x_{1},-\theta\left(\frac{\exp_{x_{1}}^{-1}(y^{\prime})}{d_{c}(y^{\prime},x_{1})}+\frac{\exp_{x_{1}}^{-1}(0)}{d_{c}(x_{1},0)}\right)\right)\in\Lambda

satisfy

(t,\zeta^{\prime})\coloneqq\left(d_{c}(y^{\prime},x_{0})+d_{c}(x_{0},0),\frac{(\exp_{y^{\prime}}^{-1}(x_{0}))^{\prime}}{d_{c}(y^{\prime},x_{0})}\right)=\left(d_{c}(y^{\prime},x_{1})+d_{c}(x_{1},0),\frac{(\exp_{y^{\prime}}^{-1}(x_{1}))^{\prime}}{d_{c}(y^{\prime},x_{1})}\right)\,.

We want to show that
x_{0}=x_{1}
.

Note that for
j\in\{0,1\}
,

1=\left\lvert\frac{\exp_{y^{\prime}}^{-1}(x_{j})}{d_{c}(y^{\prime},x_{j})}\right\rvert^{2}\,,\quad\text{so that}\quad(\exp_{y^{\prime}}^{-1}(x_{j}))_{n}=\sqrt{1-\left\lvert\frac{(\exp_{y^{\prime}}^{-1}(x_{j}))^{\prime}}{d_{c}(y^{\prime},x_{j})}\right\rvert^{2}}=\sqrt{1-\left\lvert\zeta^{\prime}\right\rvert^{2}}

where the RHS of the second equation is analytic in
\zeta^{\prime}
due to
Eq.

A2
. We took the positive square root which corresponds to the ray coming from below the surface.

We thus define
\zeta\coloneqq\left(\zeta^{\prime},\sqrt{1-\left\lvert\zeta^{\prime}\right\rvert^{2}}\right)
, which has norm
1
and have

x_{j}=\exp_{y^{\prime}}\left(d_{c}(x_{j},y^{\prime})\zeta\right)\,,\quad j\in\{0,1\}\,,

so that showing that
d_{c}(x_{0},y^{\prime})=d_{c}(x_{1},y^{\prime})
will imply that
x_{0}=x_{1}
, which will prove the injectivity of
\pi_{L}
.

We now define two functions

x(s)=\exp_{y^{\prime}}(s\zeta)\,,\quad\text{and}\quad f(s)=t-s-d_{c}(x(s),0)

and note that
x_{j}=x(d_{c}(x_{j},y^{\prime}))
and
f(d_{c}(x_{j},y^{\prime}))=0
for
j\in\{0,1\}
.

Notice first that
x(s)
is a smooth function for
s>0
and that it satisfies

\nabla x(s)=\partial_{h}\exp_{y^{\prime}}((s+h)\zeta)|_{h=0}=D\exp_{y^{\prime}}(s\zeta)\zeta\,,

and by the Gauss lemma
[
Ilm20
, Thm. 6.2]
and the fact that
\left\lvert\zeta\right\rvert=1
we have

\left\lvert\nabla x(s)\right\rvert^{2}=\langle\nabla x(s),\nabla x(s)\rangle=\frac{1}{s}\langle D\exp_{y^{\prime}}(s\zeta)s\zeta,D\exp_{y^{\prime}}(s\zeta)\zeta\rangle=\frac{1}{s}\langle s\zeta,\zeta\rangle=1\,.

Note further that because
\left\lvert\zeta\right\rvert=1
we have
d_{c}(x(s),y^{\prime})=s
and thus

0=\partial_{s}(s-d_{c}(x(s),y^{\prime}))=1+\frac{\exp_{x(s)}^{-1}(y^{\prime})}{d_{c}(x(s),y^{\prime})}\nabla x(s)\,,

and because
\nabla x(s)
and
\frac{\exp_{x(s)}^{-1}(y^{\prime})}{d_{c}(x(s),y^{\prime})}
both have norm
1
, we must have that

\nabla x(s)=-\frac{\exp_{x(s)}^{-1}(y^{\prime})}{d_{c}(x(s),y^{\prime})}\,.

(5.16)

We remark that
Eq.

5.16
is also seen intuitively if one were to draw a picture of the ray
x(s)
.

Notice also that
f
is a smooth function for
s>0
and its derivative is

f^{\prime}(s)=-1+\frac{\exp_{x(s)}^{-1}(0)}{d_{c}(x(s),0)}\cdot\nabla x(s)\,,

where both vectors
\frac{\exp_{x(s)}^{-1}(0)}{d_{c}(x(s),0)}
and
\nabla x(s)
have norm
1
, so that

f^{\prime}(s)=0\iff\left\langle\frac{\exp_{x(s)}^{-1}(0)}{d_{c}(x(s),0)},\nabla x(s)\right\rangle=1\,.

Therefore, using
Eq.

5.16
,

f^{\prime}(s)=0\iff\left\langle\frac{\exp_{x(s)}^{-1}(0)}{d_{c}(x(s),0)},\frac{\exp_{x(s)}^{-1}(y^{\prime})}{d_{c}(x(s),y^{\prime})}\right\rangle=-1\,,

which again is precluded by
Eq.

A1
.

We therefore see that
f
is a smooth function with non-vanishing derivative for
s>0
. Therefore, it attains at most one zero in
s>0
, and since both
d_{c}(x_{0},y^{\prime})
and
d_{c}(x_{1},y^{\prime})
are zeroes of
f
and neither of these distances is
0
by
Eq.

A1
, we must have
d_{c}(x_{0},y^{\prime})=d_{c}(x_{1},y^{\prime})
.

We have thus concluded that
x_{0}=x_{1}
, wherefore
\pi_{L}
is injective.

Thus, let us turn to showing that
\pi_{L}\colon\Lambda\to T^{\ast}((0,T)\times\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\setminus\{0\}
is an immersion. This will follow from the fact that
\pi_{R}\colon\Lambda\to T^{\ast}\mathcal{M}\setminus\{0\}
is a submersion and
[
SH02
, Lem. 4.3]
.

We will show that for any
(x_{0},\xi_{0})\in\pi_{R}(\Lambda)
, for some analytic function
\alpha
,

\left\{(\alpha(x,\xi);x,\xi)\colon(x,\xi)\ \text{near}\ (x_{0},\xi_{0})\right\}=\Lambda\cap\{\text{an open neighborhood of}\ \pi_{R}^{-1}((x_{0},\xi_{0}))\}\,,

from which one can readily see that
\pi_{R}
must be a submersion.

Throughout the rest of this proof, for any vector
u
, the notation
u^{\prime}
refers to the first
n-1
components of
u
. Let
(x,\xi)
near
(x_{0},\xi_{0})\in\pi_{R}(\Lambda)
.

By
Eq.

5.15
,

(t,y^{\prime},\theta,\zeta^{\prime};x,\xi)\in\Lambda\iff F(t,y^{\prime},\theta,\zeta^{\prime};x,\xi)\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}t-d_{c}(x,0)-d_{c}(x,y^{\prime})\\
\begin{pmatrix}y^{\prime}\\
0\end{pmatrix}-\exp_{x}\left((t-d_{c}(x,0))\left(-\frac{\xi}{\theta}-\frac{\exp_{x}^{-1}(0)}{d_{c}(x,0)}\right)\right)\\
\zeta^{\prime}-\theta\frac{(\exp_{y^{\prime}}^{-1}(x))^{\prime}}{d_{c}(x,y^{\prime})}\end{pmatrix}=0\,.

We will want to appeal to the implicit function theorem to show that
F=0\iff(t,y^{\prime},\theta,\zeta^{\prime})=\alpha(x,\xi)
for some analytic function
\alpha
.

Because
(x_{0},\xi_{0})\in\pi_{R}(\Lambda)
, there is some
(t_{0},y^{\prime}_{0},\theta_{0},\zeta_{0}^{\prime})
so that
F(t_{0},y_{0}^{\prime},\theta_{0},\zeta_{0}^{\prime};x_{0},\xi_{0})=0
. Throughout the rest of this proof, to lighten notation we write
(t,y^{\prime},\theta,\zeta^{\prime};x,\xi)
for
(t_{0},y_{0}^{\prime},\theta_{0},\zeta_{0}^{\prime};x_{0},\xi_{0})
, the argument of
F
will always be this point.

Let us introduce the shorthands

v\coloneqq\left(-\frac{\xi}{\theta}-\frac{\exp_{x}^{-1}(0)}{d_{c}(x,0)}\right)\,,\quad G\coloneqq(D\exp_{x})\left((t-d_{c}(x,0))\left(-\frac{\xi}{\theta}-\frac{\exp_{x}^{-1}(0)}{d_{c}(x,0)}\right)\right)=(D\exp_{x})\left((t-d_{c}(x,0))v\right)

and note that
\left\lvert v\right\rvert=1
because
F=0
, because then
v=\frac{\exp_{x}^{-1}(y^{\prime})}{d_{c}(x,y^{\prime})}
by
Eq.

5.15
.

We calculate (where
\ast
refers to some expression we do not explicitly calculate):

\partial_{(t,y^{\prime},\theta,\zeta^{\prime})}F=\begin{pmatrix}1&\frac{(\exp_{y^{\prime}}^{-1}(x))^{\prime}}{d_{c}(x,y^{\prime})}&0&0\\
-(Gv)^{\prime}&\mathrm{id}^{\prime}&(\theta^{-1}(t-d_{c}(x,0))G(-\frac{\xi}{\theta}))^{\prime}&0\\
-(Gv)_{n}&0&(\theta^{-1}(t-d_{c}(x,0))G(-\frac{\xi}{\theta}))_{n}&0\\
0&\ast&\ast&\mathrm{id}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}

which is invertible if and only if the matrix

K\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}1&\frac{(\exp_{y^{\prime}}^{-1}(x))^{\prime}}{d_{c}(x,y^{\prime})}&0\\
-(Gv)^{\prime}&\mathrm{id}^{\prime}&(\theta^{-1}(t-d_{c}(x,0))G(-\frac{\xi}{\theta}))^{\prime}\\
-(Gv)_{n}&0&(\theta^{-1}(t-d_{c}(x,0))G(-\frac{\xi}{\theta}))_{n}\end{pmatrix}

is invertible.

Putting
y(s)\coloneqq\exp_{x}(sv)
, we proceed as in the proof of the injectivity of
\pi_{L}
to calculate that because
d_{c}(y(s),x)=s
, we have by differentiation with respect to
s
that

-1=\frac{\exp_{y(s)}^{-1}(x)}{d_{c}(y(s),x)}\cdot\dot{y}(s)\,,

where by the Gauss lemma and the fact that
\left\lvert v\right\rvert=1
we find that
\left\lvert\dot{y}(s)\right\rvert=1
, so that we may conclude that

\dot{y}(s)=-\frac{\exp_{y(s)}^{-1}(x)}{d_{c}(y(s),x)}\,,

and since
y^{\prime}=y(d_{c}(x,y^{\prime}))=y(t-d_{c}(x,0))
(here
y
is the function
y(s)
), we find

\frac{\exp_{y^{\prime}}^{-1}(x)}{d_{c}(x,y^{\prime})}=-\partial_{s}\exp_{x}((t-d_{c}(x,0)+s)v)|_{s=0}=-Gv\,,

which we can insert into the matrix
K
.

Using elementary matrix operations to remove the entries to the left and right of the central
\mathrm{id}^{\prime}
, we see that
K
is invertible if and only if

\begin{pmatrix}1-\langle(Gv)^{\prime},(Gv)^{\prime}\rangle&-(Gv)^{\prime}&\theta^{-1}(t-d_{c}(x,0))\langle(G(-\frac{\xi}{\theta}))^{\prime},(Gv)^{\prime}\rangle\\
0&\mathrm{id}^{\prime}&0\\
-(Gv)_{n}&0&(\theta^{-1}(t-d_{c}(x,0))G(-\frac{\xi}{\theta}))_{n}\end{pmatrix}

is invertible.

This is true if and only if the
2
-vectors

\begin{pmatrix}1-\langle(Gv)^{\prime},(Gv)^{\prime}\rangle\\
-(Gv)_{n}\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{and}\quad\begin{pmatrix}\theta^{-1}(t-d_{c}(x,0))\langle(G(-\frac{\xi}{\theta}))^{\prime},(Gv)^{\prime}\rangle\\
(\theta^{-1}(t-d_{c}(x,0))G(-\frac{\xi}{\theta}))_{n}\end{pmatrix}

are linearly independent and non-zero.

Now define
w\coloneqq\frac{\exp_{x}^{-1}(0)}{d_{c}(x,0)}
, which has norm
1
, and note that

\frac{-\xi}{\theta}=v+w\,.

(5.17)

Use Gauss’ lemma
[
Ilm20
, Thm. 6.2]
again to write that

\langle(Gv)^{\prime},(Gv)^{\prime}\rangle=\langle Gv,Gv\rangle-(Gv)_{n}^{2}=\langle v,v\rangle-(Gv)_{n}^{2}=1-(Gv)_{n}^{2}\,,

and using
Eq.

5.17
,

\left\langle\left(G\left(-\frac{\xi}{\theta}\right)\right)^{\prime},(Gv)^{\prime}\right\rangle=\langle Gv,Gv\rangle-(Gv)_{n}^{2}+\langle Gw,Gv\rangle-(Gw)_{n}(Gv)_{n}=1-(Gv)_{n}^{2}+\langle w,v\rangle-(Gw)_{n}(Gv)_{n}\,.

So that we must check that

\begin{pmatrix}(Gv)_{n}^{2}\\
-(Gv)_{n}\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{and}\quad\begin{pmatrix}1+\langle w,v\rangle-(Gv)_{n}^{2}-(Gw)_{n}(Gv)_{n}\\
(Gw)_{n}+(Gv)_{n}\end{pmatrix}

are non-zero and linearly independent.

The first of these vectors is non-zero by assumption of no grazing rays
Eq.

A2
:
(Gv)_{n}=-\left(\frac{\exp_{y^{\prime}}^{-1}(x)}{d_{c}(x,y^{\prime})}\right)_{n}\neq 0
(again since we must have
x_{n}<0
), whereas the second vector vanishes if and only if
1+\langle w,v\rangle=0
, which is (due to the fact that
\left\lvert v\right\rvert=\left\lvert w\right\rvert=1
) equivalent to
w=-v
, which is precluded exactly by the assumption of no scattering over
\pi
,
Eq.

A1
.

Finally, checking that the vectors are linearly independent reduces to showing that
1+\langle w,v\rangle\neq 0
, which is still true by
Eq.

A1
.

We have thus shown that
\partial_{(t,y^{\prime},\theta,\zeta^{\prime})}F
is invertible at the point
(t,y^{\prime},\theta,\zeta^{\prime};x,\xi)=(t_{0},y_{0}^{\prime},\theta_{0},\zeta_{0}^{\prime};x_{0},\xi_{0})
, so that an application of the implicit function theorem completes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.6
.

We remark how the above construction can be repeated for non-smooth but regular enough
c\in C^{k},c>0,k\gg 1
. In the special case of the linear wave equation with variable coefficients, one may repeat the proof of
Lemma

4.2
with references to
[
HR23
, Cor. 6.2]
instead to give existence of the operator
S_{c}
for rough coefficients. Then, while not explicitly stated, the functions
U_{j}
in the proof of
Proposition

5.2
(that is
Theorem

4.1
) satisfy
U_{j}\in C^{k-2j}
if
c\in C^{k}
, and
E_{j}\in C^{s}
if
j>s+\frac{n-1}{2}
(
[
Hör07
, p. 36]
). This allows one to repeat the proof of
Proposition

5.2
with the modification that
Eq.

4.10
only holds for a finite amount of
N\in\mathbb{N}
. The arguments that remove dependence of the amplitude on
t
only depend on the analyticity of the amplitude with respect to
t,\theta
, which will still be guaranteed.

One will then find for
c\in C^{k},c>0
with
k
sufficiently large that for some rough amplitude
a
(see for example definitions in
[
Mar88
]
or
[
Roz23
]
),

S_{c}(\delta_{(0,y)})=\int e^{i\theta(t-d_{c}(x,y))}a(x,y,\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta\mod C^{s}\,,

where
s
can be chosen as large as one wishes by taking
k
large enough. One may then repeat the proof of
Theorem

5.4
for this rough FIO without modification, which will show that
DA[c]
is given by
Eq.

5.12
, where the amplitude and phase are as regular as one wishes by taking
k
large enough.

In fact, due to the explicit nature of the formula
Eq.

5.12
one will be able to compose
DA[c]
with its adjoint by hand to find that
DA[c]^{\ast}DA[c]
is a rough elliptic
\Psi
DO that is microlocally invertible on the set
\pi_{R}\Lambda
. After a microlocal change of variables to replace the phase by
\xi\cdot(x-y)
near
\pi_{R}\Lambda
(with
x,y,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
) the amplitude of
DA[c]^{\ast}DA[c]
will have only finite smoothness in all of its variables (including the frequency variable). Performing the usual reduction step of an amplitude to a symbol up to finitely many steps
one may employ the calculus of
[
Mar88
]
or
[
Mar96
]
to argue that the normal operator
DA[c]^{\ast}DA[c]
maps between Sobolev spaces
H^{s}\to H^{s-n+1}
(for a bounded set of
s\in\mathbb{R}
determined by the size of
k
).

In this way one will be able to recover the results
[
Rak88
, Thm. B,Cor. C,Cor. D]
(with a restricted set of Sobolev space orders) with wave coefficient
c\in C^{k}
for
k
sufficiently large.

We now understand
DA[c]
sufficiently well to prove its injectivity.

Proof of
Theorem

5.1
.

Note first that 2.
\implies
3. follows from the fact that
DA[c](\cdot)
is a linear operator, and 3.
\implies
1. is immediate. Thus we must only show that 1.
\implies
2. to complete this proof which we do now.

This is a consequence of
Theorem

5.4
and
Theorem

3.4
. We follow a layer stripping argument, compare to the proof of
[
MST23
, Thm. 1.4]
.
Recall that
c_{\delta}\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\mathcal{M})\cap C(\mathcal{K})
with
\mathrm{supp}c_{\delta}\subset\mathcal{K}\cap\{x_{n}<-\varepsilon\}
for some
\varepsilon>0
, so that we may interpret
c_{\delta}\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon})
.

Note that by assumption
\mathcal{K}
is compact, so we may let
S=-\min_{x\in\mathcal{K}}x_{n}>0
, and we then consider the set

I\coloneqq\left\{s\in(0,S]\colon c_{\delta}=0\ \text{in}\ \{x_{n}\geq S-s\}\right\}\,,

where we note that by assumption on
c_{\delta}
,
(S-\varepsilon,S]\subset I
, so that
I
is non-empty and closed by the continuity of
c_{\delta}
.

We now make the claim that if
s\in I
, then an open neighborhood of
s
is in
I
. This claim implies that
I
is open and closed and thus
I=(0,S]
, so that
c_{\delta}\equiv 0
on
\mathcal{K}\setminus\{x_{n}=S\}
, and by continuity we can conclude that
c_{\delta}=0
on
\mathcal{K}
.

We thus conclude this proof by proving the claim that
s\in I
implies there is an open neighborhood of
s
in
I
: Let us first show that for all
x
with
x_{n}<0
there exist
t,y^{\prime},\theta,\zeta^{\prime}
so that

((t,y^{\prime}),(\theta,\zeta^{\prime});x,e_{n})\in\Lambda\,.

Using the explicit formula from
Eq.

5.15
, in order for
((t,y^{\prime}),(\theta,\zeta^{\prime});x,\xi)\in\Lambda\,,
we must have that

\xi=-\theta\left(\frac{\exp_{x}^{-1}(0)}{d_{c}(x,0)}+\frac{\exp_{x}^{-1}(y^{\prime})}{d_{c}(x,y^{\prime})}\right)\,,

(5.18)

where we want to choose
\xi
to be
e_{n}
as this is the normal to the hypersurface
\{x_{n}=s\}
. Writing
\omega=-\exp_{x}^{-1}(0)
we note that
Eq.

B1
gives
\omega_{n}<0
, and noting that
\left\lvert\frac{\exp_{x}^{-1}(y^{\prime})}{d_{c}(x,y^{\prime})}\right\rvert=1
, we find the condition

1=\left\lvert-\theta^{-1}e_{n}+\frac{\omega}{\left\lvert\omega\right\rvert}\right\rvert^{2}\,,\quad\text{which is equivalent to}\quad\sqrt{1-\left\lvert\frac{\omega^{\prime}}{\left\lvert\omega\right\rvert}\right\rvert^{2}}-\frac{\omega_{n}}{\left\lvert\omega\right\rvert}=-\theta^{-1}\,,

which can be solved by
0<\theta<\infty
due to the fact that
-\omega_{n}>0
. Choosing
\xi=\theta e_{n}
for this
\theta
, by
Eq.

5.18
we know that

\frac{\exp_{x}^{-1}(y^{\prime})}{d_{c}(x,y^{\prime})}=\frac{\omega^{\prime}}{\left\lvert\omega\right\rvert}+\sqrt{1-\frac{\left\lvert\omega^{\prime}\right\rvert^{2}}{\left\lvert\omega\right\rvert^{2}}}e_{n}\,,

which is to say that if the ray from the origin to
x
were to ‘reflect at an angle’
\theta e_{n}
, then the reflected ray would be parametrized by

y(r)=\exp_{x}\left(r\left(\frac{\omega^{\prime}}{\left\lvert\omega\right\rvert}+\sqrt{1-\frac{\left\lvert\omega^{\prime}\right\rvert^{2}}{\left\lvert\omega\right\rvert^{2}}}e_{n}\right)\right)\,,

which by
Eq.

B2
will hit the measurement surface
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
at some time
r=r^{\ast}>0
.

Noting that by calculations similar to those in the proof of
Theorem

5.4
,

\partial_{r}y(r)|_{r=r^{\ast}}=-\frac{\exp_{y^{\prime}}^{-1}(x)}{d_{c}(y^{\prime},x)}\,,

so that we have established that

((t,y^{\prime}),(\theta,\zeta^{\prime});x,\theta e_{n})\coloneqq\left((d_{c}(x,0)+r^{\ast},(y(r^{\ast}))^{\prime}),(\theta,-\theta(\partial_{r}y(r^{\ast}))^{\prime});x,\theta e_{n}\right)\in\Lambda\,.

(5.19)

Here we used the fact that
T>2\mathrm{diam}_{c}(\mathcal{M})\geq d_{c}(x,0)+r^{\ast}
.

Now let
s\in I
, and we start layer stripping by taking
H_{s}=\{x_{n}=-s\}
. Notice that the conormal vector to
H_{s}
at each point is just
\xi_{0}=\pm e_{n}
(standard unit vector). By
Eq.

5.19
, applying
Theorem

3.4
and the assumption that
DA[c](c_{\delta})\in C^{\omega}((0,T)\times\mathcal{M}^{\prime})
, we know that
c_{\delta}
must vanish in a neighborhood of
x
for all
x\in H_{s}\cap\mathcal{K}
. The compactness of
\mathcal{K}
lets us conclude there is some
\delta>0
so that
(s-\delta,s]\subset I
, this completes the proof of the claim that
I
is open, which completes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.7
.

In essence, in
Eq.

5.19
we show that
\pi_{R}\Lambda\supset\mathcal{K}\times\{e_{n}\}
. By the ellipticity of the amplitude in
DA[c]
, this in fact implies that
DA[c]
is microlocally elliptic at any
x\in\mathcal{K}
in the direction
e_{n}
. Thus, the proof of
Theorem

5.1
boils down to making the right assumptions to guarantee this microlocal ellipticity and exploit Holmgren’s theorem which allows us to upgrade this microlocal ellipticity to injectivity (via a layer stripping argument).

6
Appendix

A
Some Numerics

For combinatorical results useful for dealing with estimates of analytic functions, see also
[
Rod93
, § 1.2]
.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed.

1.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
C=C(n)
, independent of
N
, so that

(N+n)!\leq C^{N}N!\,.

2.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We have for
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
denoting multi-indices,

\sum_{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert=N}1\leq n^{N}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert\leq N}1\leq Nn^{N}\leq(en)^{N}\,.

(A.1)

Proof.

Notice that for
N\geq 1
, using
x\leq e^{x-1}
for
x\geq 1
,

(N+1)^{N+1}\leq(2N)^{N+1}\leq 2^{N+1}NN^{N}\leq 2^{N+1}e^{N-1}N^{N}\leq c^{N+1}N^{N}\,.

Thus by induction, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

(N+n)^{N+n}\leq c^{N+n+1}c^{N+n}\cdots c^{N+1}N^{N}=(c^{n})^{N}c^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}N^{N}\leq C^{N}N^{N}\,,

for some
C=C(n)
.

Using Stirling’s approximation twice,

(N+n)!\leq 3\sqrt{N+n}\left(\frac{N+n}{e}\right)^{N+n}\leq 3\sqrt{N+n}(CN)^{N}e^{-N}\leq\frac{3}{2}\frac{\sqrt{N+n}}{\sqrt{N}}C^{N}N!\leq\tilde{C}^{N}N!\,,

which concludes the proof of item 1.

By the multinomial theorem, for
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
,
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{N}=\sum_{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert=N}\frac{N!}{\alpha!}x^{\alpha}

and taking
x=(1,\dots,1)
we see that

n^{N}=\sum_{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert=N}\frac{N!}{\alpha!}\geq\sum_{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert=N}1\,.

The second part of
Eq.

A.1
follows immediately from the first.
∎

Lemma A.2
.

Let
C,c>0
be constants with
C\geq 4c
,
\gamma\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
a multi-index and
L\in\mathbb{N},L\geq 1
with
\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert\leq L
. We have

\sum_{\beta\leq\gamma}\frac{\gamma!}{\beta!}c^{\left\lvert\gamma-\beta\right\rvert}(CL)^{\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}\leq 2^{n}C^{\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert}L^{\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert}\,.

Proof.

Notice first that

\sum_{\beta\leq\gamma}\frac{\gamma!}{\beta!}c^{\left\lvert\gamma-\beta\right\rvert}(CL)^{\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}=\sum_{\beta_{1}\leq\gamma_{1}}\frac{\gamma_{1}!}{\beta_{1}!}c^{\gamma_{1}-\beta_{1}}(CL)^{\beta_{1}}\dots\sum_{\beta_{n}\leq\gamma_{n}}\frac{\gamma_{n}!}{\beta_{n}!}c^{\gamma_{n}-\beta_{n}}(CL)^{\beta_{n}}\,,

and pulling out the powers of
c
, the proof is reduced to showing for
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
l\leq L
that

\sum_{k=0}^{l}\frac{l!}{k!}(CL)^{k}\leq 2C^{l}L^{l}\,.

(A.2)

for any
C\geq 4
.

Writing
\Gamma(\cdot,\cdot)
for the incomplete Gamma function (see
[
Tem96
, § 11.2]
for a definition), the left-hand side of
Eq.

A.2
is equal to

e^{CL}\Gamma\left(l+1,CL\right)\,.

Now, if we define
\tau\coloneqq\frac{CL}{l+1}
, then since
C\geq 4
and
l\leq L
,

\tau=\frac{CL}{l+1}\geq 2\,,\quad\text{and}\quad l=\tau^{-1}CL-1\,.

According to
[
Nem16
, Thm. 1.1]
, for any
M>0
and any
\lambda>1
we have

\Gamma(M,\lambda M)=(\lambda M)^{M}e^{-\lambda M}\left(\frac{1}{(\lambda-1)M}+R_{1}(M,\lambda)\right)

with

R_{1}(M,\lambda)=-M^{-2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{t}e^{-t(\lambda-\log\lambda)}}{\Gamma(t)(1+t/M)}\mathrm{d}t\,,

which is non-positive for positive
M
and
\lambda>1
.

Thus, for
\lambda\geq 2
,

\Gamma(M,\lambda M)\leq(\lambda M)^{M-1}e^{-\lambda M}\frac{\lambda}{\lambda-1}\leq 2(\lambda M)^{M-1}e^{-\lambda M}\,.

Thus, putting
M=\lambda^{-1}CL
and
\lambda=\tau\geq 2
, we see that

e^{CL}\Gamma\left(l+1,CL\right)=e^{\lambda M}\Gamma(M,\lambda M)\leq 2(\lambda M)^{M-1}=2(CL)^{l}\,.

This concludes the proof.
∎

The result above lets us determine bounds on an iterated differential operator.

Lemma A.3
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open set, and let
L\in\mathbb{N}
. Let

\mathcal{L}(x,\partial)=\sum_{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert\leq 1}c_{\alpha,1}(x)\partial^{\alpha}

be a differential operator of order
1
on
\Omega
with coefficients
c_{\alpha,1}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{C}
satisfying the following: there are
C,M\colon\Omega\to(0,\infty)
so that

x\in\Omega,\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert\leq L\implies\left\lvert\partial^{\beta}c_{\alpha,1}(x)\right\rvert\leq M(x)C(x)^{\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}\beta!\,,

(A.3)

then for every
k\leq L
, the operator
\mathcal{L}^{k}
is a differential operator of order
\leq k
,

\mathcal{L}^{k}(x,\partial)=\sum_{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert\leq k}c_{\alpha,k}(x)\partial^{\alpha}

with
c_{\alpha,k}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{C}
so that for all
\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert\leq k
, for
x\in\Omega
and
\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert\leq L-k+\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert

\left\lvert\partial^{\beta}c_{\alpha,k}(x)\right\rvert\leq(2^{n}(2n+1)M(x))^{k-1}M(x)(4C(x)(\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert+k-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert))^{\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert+k-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert},

(A.4)

Proof.

We proceed by induction noting that for
k=1
our assumption
Eq.

A.3
guarantees the statement as
\beta!\leq\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert!\leq\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert^{\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}
(and we say
0^{0}=1
here). Let us thus assume
k\geq 2
and
Eq.

A.4
is valid for positive integers
<k
.

A calculation gives

\mathcal{L}^{k+1}=\sum_{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert\leq k+1}c_{\alpha,k+1}(x)\partial^{\alpha}\,,

where for all
\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert\leq k+1

\displaystyle c_{\alpha,k+1}=c_{0,1}c_{\alpha,k}+\sum_{\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert=1}c_{\gamma,1}\partial^{\gamma}c_{\alpha,k}+\sum_{\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert=1}c_{\gamma,1}c_{\alpha-\gamma,k}

and we adopted the convention that
c_{\alpha,j}=0
if
\alpha
has a negative index or if
\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert>j
.

We will show explicitly that
c_{\gamma,1}\partial^{\gamma}c_{\alpha,k}
satisfies the desired bound, where the proof can be copied for all other terms.

Put
K\coloneqq 2^{n}(2n+1)
. We consider for
\left\lvert\gamma\right\rvert=1
, using the induction hypothesis
Eq.

A.4
and
Eq.

A.3
, for
\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert\leq L-k+\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert-1
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\partial^{\beta}(c_{\gamma,1}\partial^{\gamma}c_{\alpha,k})\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\sum_{\delta\leq\beta}{\beta\choose\delta}\partial^{\beta-\delta}c_{\gamma,1}\partial^{\delta+\gamma}c_{\alpha,k}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq M(x)(M(x)K)^{k-1}C(x)^{\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}(4C(x))^{k+1-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}\sum_{\delta\leq\beta}\frac{\beta!}{\delta!}4^{\left\lvert\delta\right\rvert}(\left\lvert\delta\right\rvert+k+1-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert)^{\left\lvert\delta\right\rvert+k+1-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}

\displaystyle\leq M(x)(M(x)K)^{k-1}C(x)^{\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}(4C(x)(\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert+k+1-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert))^{k+1-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}\sum_{\delta\leq\beta}\frac{\beta!}{\delta!}4^{\left\lvert\delta\right\rvert}(\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert+k+1-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert)^{\left\lvert\delta\right\rvert}\,,

where we note that
\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert+k-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert
, so that we may apply
Lemma

A.2
to bound

\sum_{\delta\leq\beta}\frac{\beta!}{\delta!}4^{\left\lvert\delta\right\rvert}(\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert+k+1-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert)^{\left\lvert\delta\right\rvert}\leq 2^{n}4^{\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}(\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert+k+1-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert)^{\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}\,,

which gives

\left\lvert\partial^{\beta}(c_{\gamma,1}\partial^{\gamma}c_{\alpha,k})\right\rvert\leq M(x)^{2}2^{n}(M(x)K)^{k-1}(4C(x)(\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert+k+1-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert))^{\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert+k+1-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}\,,

as desired.

Similar estimates for all other terms will conclude the proof if we sum up the
\leq(2n+1)
terms of
c_{\alpha,k+1}
.
∎

As a consequence of the above we can state

Corollary A.4
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open set, and

\mathcal{L}(x,\partial)=\sum_{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert\leq 1}c_{\alpha}(x)\partial^{\alpha}

be a differential operator of order
1
on
\Omega
with coefficients
c_{\alpha}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{C}
satisfying for all multi-indices
\beta

\left\lvert\partial^{\beta}c_{\alpha}(x)\right\rvert\leq M(x)C(x)^{\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}\beta!

for some
C,M\colon\Omega\to(0,\infty)
.

Let
L\in\mathbb{N}
and
a\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{C}
be a function so that there exist
R_{a},M_{a},C_{a}\colon\Omega\to(0,\infty)
so that

\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert\leq R_{a}(x)\implies\left\lvert\partial^{\alpha}a(x)\right\rvert\leq M_{a}(x)(C_{a}(x)L)^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}\,.

For
L\leq R_{a}(x)
,

\left\lvert\mathcal{L}^{L}a(x)\right\rvert\leq M_{a}(x)\left(en2^{n}(2n+1)M(x)\max\{C_{a}(x),4C(x)\}L\right)^{L}\,.

Proof.

Using
Lemma

A.3
we calculate that

L\leq R_{a}(x)\implies\left\lvert\mathcal{L}^{L}a(x)\right\rvert\leq M(x)^{L}K^{L-1}M_{a}(x)\sum_{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert\leq L}(L-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert)^{L-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}(4C(x))^{L-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}(C_{a}(x)L)^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}\,,

where estimating
(L-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert)^{L-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}L^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}\leq L^{L}
and using
Eq.

A.1
concludes the proof of the estimate.
∎

B
A Result from Section
2.3

The expressions in this section are those from
Section

2.3
.

We introduce notation for an application of Faà di Bruno’s formula. For every multi-index
\alpha
, let

\Pi_{\alpha}\coloneqq\{\text{partitions of }\{1,\dots,\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert\}\}\,,

(B.1)

and for every
\pi\in\Pi_{\alpha}
, and any
P\in\pi
, let
\alpha_{P}
be the multi-index with

(\alpha_{P})_{j}=\#\left(P\cap\left\{1+\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\alpha_{i},\dots,\sum_{i=1}^{j}\alpha_{i}\right\}\right)\,,

(B.2)

where if
1+\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\alpha_{i}>\sum_{i=1}^{j}\alpha_{i}
we set
(\alpha_{P})_{j}=0
.

Lemma B.1
.

Assume
\kappa
satisfies assumption

κ

1.
. For
\rho_{\kappa}
as above, for any multi-indices
\alpha,\beta
we may bound
(\rho_{\kappa})^{-1}
as follows:

\displaystyle(z,\eta)\in\Omega_{1}\times\Gamma

\displaystyle\implies\left\lvert\partial^{\alpha}_{z}\partial^{\beta}_{\eta}\left(\rho_{\kappa}^{-1}(z,\eta)\right)\right\rvert\leq C^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert+1}\alpha!\beta!\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{-2-\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}

for some
C>0
.

Proof.

Throughout this proof let
(z,\eta)\in\Omega_{1}\times\Gamma
.

By an application of Faà di Bruno’s formula (using notation from
Eqs.

B.1
and
B.2
) we have

\partial^{\alpha}_{z}\partial^{\beta}_{\eta}\rho_{\kappa}^{-1}=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{(\alpha,\beta)}}(-1)^{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert!\rho_{\kappa}^{-(1+\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert)}\prod_{P\in\pi}\partial^{(\alpha,\beta)_{P}}\rho_{\kappa}\,.

(B.3)

We will thus try to find bounds for
\partial^{(\alpha,\beta)_{P}}\rho_{\kappa}
.

Note here that using notation
Eq.

B.2
for
\beta_{P}
, for each
\pi\in\Pi_{(\alpha,\beta)}

\sum_{P\in\pi}\left\lvert P\right\rvert=\left\lvert(\alpha,\beta)\right\rvert\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{P\in\pi}\left\lvert\beta_{P}\right\rvert=\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert\,.

(B.4)

Furthermore, by
[
CNR15
, Eq. (3.8)]
,

\prod_{P\in\pi}\left\lvert P\right\rvert!\leq\frac{\left\lvert(\alpha,\beta)\right\rvert!}{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert!}

(B.5)

and by elementary properties of multi-indices

\left\lvert(\alpha,\beta)\right\rvert!\leq(N+n)^{\left\lvert\alpha+\beta\right\rvert}\alpha!\beta!\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\left\lvert(\alpha,\beta)_{P}\right\rvert=\left\lvert P\right\rvert\,,\quad\text{and}\quad(\alpha,\beta)_{P}!\leq\left\lvert P\right\rvert!\,.

(B.6)

According to the second part of
Eq.

2.10
we see that

\left\lvert\partial^{\alpha}_{z}\partial^{\beta}_{\eta}\rho_{\kappa}\right\rvert\leq C^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert+1}\alpha!\beta!\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2-\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}\,,

which implies

\prod_{P\in\pi}\left\lvert\partial^{(\alpha,\beta)_{P}}\rho_{\kappa}\right\rvert\leq\prod_{P\in\pi}C^{\left\lvert(\alpha,\beta)_{P}\right\rvert+1}(\alpha,\beta)_{P}!\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2-\left\lvert\beta_{P}\right\rvert}\,,

so that using
Eqs.

B.4
,
B.5
and
B.6
, for some
C>0
we may bound

\prod_{P\in\pi}\left\lvert\partial^{(\alpha,\beta)_{P}}\rho_{\kappa}\right\rvert\leq\prod_{P\in\pi}C^{\left\lvert P\right\rvert+1}\left\lvert P\right\rvert!\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{-\left\lvert\beta_{P}\right\rvert}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2}\leq C^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert+\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}(N+n)^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}\alpha!\beta!(\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert!)^{-1}\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert-\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}\,,

which, using
Eq.

B.3
, leads to the bound

\displaystyle\left\lvert\partial^{\alpha}_{z}\partial^{\beta}_{\eta}\rho_{\kappa}^{-1}\right\rvert
