\displaystyle\leq(C(N+n))^{\left\lvert\alpha+\beta\right\rvert}\alpha!\beta!\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{-\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}\rho_{\kappa}^{-1}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{(\alpha,\beta)}}\left(\frac{C\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2}}{\rho_{\kappa}}\right)^{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}

where

\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{(\alpha,\beta)}}\left(\frac{C\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2}}{\rho_{\kappa}}\right)^{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}\leq\left(\frac{C\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2}}{\rho_{\kappa}}\right)^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{(\alpha,\beta)}}1\leq(3\tilde{C})^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}\,,

where we used
\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{(\alpha,\beta)}}1\leq 3^{\left\lvert\alpha+\beta\right\rvert}
from
[
Pri09
]
, and
(C\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{2}/\rho_{\kappa})\leq\tilde{C}
from the first part of
Eq.

2.10
, which completes the proof.
∎

We are now in position to give

Proof of
Lemma

2.12
.

The inequality
Eq.

2.13
is a simple consequence of Leibnitz’s rule together with
Lemma

B.1
and
Eq.

2.10
, where we used that
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert^{-1}\leq C
for
\eta\in\Gamma
.
∎

C
Classical Analytic Stationary Phase

We recall the definition of formal classical analytic amplitudes from
[
Sjö82
, § 1]
:

Definition C.1
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}^{d}
be open for some
d\in\mathbb{N}
and let
(q_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
be a sequence of holomorphic functions so that for every compact
K\Subset\Omega
there are
M,C>0
independent of
k
, so that

\sup_{K}\left\lvert q_{k}\right\rvert\leq MC^{k}k!\,.

We call
\underline{q}=(q_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
a
formal classical analytic amplitude
. We say
\underline{q}
is
elliptic
if
q_{0}\neq 0
on
\Omega
.

For any
R>eC
sufficiently large and any
\lambda\geq 1
, we call

q(a;\lambda)\coloneqq\sum_{0\leq k\leq\lambda R^{-1}}\lambda^{-k}q_{k}(a)\in\mathcal{O}(K)

a
finite realization
of
\underline{q}
on
K
.

We recall that as a consequence of
[
Sjö82
, Ex. 1.1]
, two finite realizations of the same formal classical analytic amplitude differ by a term
\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})
for some
\varepsilon>0
.

We will first provide a supplementary lemma and a version of the method of stationary phase that is taken from
[
Tre22
, § 19.3]
. We briefly remark that the following result can also be seen as a consequence of
[
Hör03
, Thm. 7.7.1]
.

Lemma C.2
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
and let
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded open set. There is a constant
C>0
so that the following holds. Let
b,\kappa\in C^{\omega}(X;\mathbb{C})
be complex valued real-analytic functions with
\kappa^{i\mathbb{R}}(x)\geq-\delta
for some
\delta\geq 0
and
\left\lvert\partial^{\alpha}b\right\rvert\leq M_{b}C_{b}^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}\alpha!
.
Let
(\chi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of Ehrenpreis cutoffs compactly supported in
X
, and
\inf_{j\in\mathbb{N},x\in\mathrm{supp}\chi_{j}}\left\lvert\nabla\kappa(x)\right\rvert>0
.

For
j>0
large enough, we have

\int_{X}e^{i\lambda\kappa(x)}\chi_{j}(x)b(x)\mathrm{d}x\leq CM_{b}e^{(\delta-j^{-1}/2)\lambda}\,.

Proof.

Due to the assumptions we may use partial integration with the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}
on
X
which satisfies assumption

κ

2.
on
{X}
, to see that after
j
applications of
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}
,

\int_{{X}}e^{i\lambda\kappa({x})}\chi_{j}({x})b({x})\mathrm{d}{x}=(-i\lambda)^{-j}\int_{{X}}e^{i\lambda\kappa({x})}\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}^{j}(\chi_{j}b)({x})\mathrm{d}{x}

where choosing
\vartheta\leq\vartheta_{0}\coloneqq(eC_{\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}}C_{b})^{-1}
and
j
according to
\vartheta\lambda/2\leq j\leq\vartheta\lambda
, estimate
Eq.

2.16
gives

\left\lvert\int_{{X}}e^{i\lambda\kappa({x})}\mathcal{L}^{j}_{\kappa}(\chi_{j}b)({x})\mathrm{d}{x}\right\rvert\leq M_{b}\left\lvert{X}\right\rvert e^{\delta\lambda}\left(\frac{C_{\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}}C_{b}j}{\lambda}\right)^{j}\leq M_{b}Ce^{\delta\lambda}e^{-j}\leq CM_{b}e^{(\delta-\vartheta/2)\lambda}\,.

∎

Lemma C.3

(
[
Tre22
, § 19.3]
)
.

Let
d_{1},d_{2},k\in\mathbb{N}
,
r>0
and
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}^{d_{2}}
. Let
\underline{p}=(p_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{O}(\{{a}\in\mathbb{C}^{d_{1}}\colon\left\lvert{a}\right\rvert<2r\}\times\Omega)
be a formal classical analytic amplitude satisfying for some
M_{p},C_{p}>0
independent of
k
,

\sup_{\left\lvert{a}\right\rvert\leq r,{b}\in\Omega}\left\lvert p_{k}({a},{b})\right\rvert\leq M_{p}C_{p}^{k}k!\,.

Let
\lambda\geq 1
and
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{d_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}
. There exist
C_{1},C_{2}>0
so that for every
K\in\mathbb{N},K\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\int_{\left\lvert{a}\right\rvert\leq r}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{d_{1}}(1-i\sigma_{j}){a}_{j}^{2}}p_{k}({a},{b})\mathrm{d}{a}=\left(\frac{2\pi}{\lambda}\right)^{\frac{d_{1}}{2}}\sum_{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert<K}\frac{(2\lambda)^{-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}}{\alpha!}\sum_{j=1}^{d_{1}}(1-i\sigma_{j})^{-\alpha_{j}-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{{a}}^{2\alpha}p_{k}(0,{b})+R_{k}^{K}({b},\lambda)

(C.1)

and for every
R>0
large enough and
\lambda\geq R
, if
K=\lfloor\lambda R^{-1}\rfloor+1
, and
k<K
,

\left\lvert\lambda^{-k}R_{k}^{K-k}({b},\lambda)\right\rvert\leq Ce^{-\varepsilon\lambda}

(C.2)

for some
C,\varepsilon>0
.

Proof.

According to
[
Tre22
, § 19.3.2]
we know that
Eq.

C.1
is true with remainder
R^{K}_{k}=R_{k,1}^{K}+R_{k,2}^{K}
satisfying, according to
[
Tre22
, Lem. 19.3.5,Lem. 19.3.6]
respectively, the estimates

\left\lvert R_{k,1}^{K}({b},\lambda)\right\rvert\leq r^{-2K}\left\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d_{1}-1}\right\rvert\left(K+\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(K+\frac{d_{1}}{2}\right)\left(\frac{2}{\lambda}\right)^{K+\frac{d_{1}}{2}}M_{p}C_{p}^{k}k!

and

\left\lvert R_{k,2}^{K}({b},\lambda)\right\rvert\leq e^{-\frac{1}{4}\lambda r^{2}}r^{d_{1}}\left\lvert\mathbb{S}^{d_{1}-1}\right\rvert\sum_{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert<K}\Gamma\left(\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\frac{d_{1}}{2}\right)\left(\frac{2}{\lambda r^{2}}\right)^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\frac{d_{1}}{2}}M_{p}C_{p}^{k}k!\,.

By
Lemma

A.1
we can bound
\Gamma(K+d_{1}/2)\leq(K+d_{1})!\leq C_{0}^{K}K!
for some
C_{0}>0
depending only on the dimension
d_{1}
, and putting
C\geq\max\{C_{0},C_{p}\}
and absorbing constants into
C>0
,

\left\lvert R_{k,1}^{K}({b},\lambda)\right\rvert\leq C^{K}\lambda^{-K}(K+1/2)K!M_{p}C_{p}^{k}k!\leq C^{K+k}(K+1)!k!\lambda^{-K}\,.

Using
K=\lfloor\lambda R^{-1}\rfloor+1
,
k<K
and
\lambda\geq R
, we have
K\leq R^{-1}\lambda+1\leq 2R^{-1}\lambda
,
so that with Stirling’s estimate and
K\geq\lambda R^{-1}
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\lambda^{-k}R_{k,1}^{K-k}({b},\lambda)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{C}{\lambda}\right)^{K}K!(K+1)\leq(K+1)\sqrt{2\pi K}\left(\frac{CK}{e\lambda}\right)^{K}\leq(K+1)\sqrt{2\pi K}\left(\frac{2CR^{-1}}{e}\right)^{K}

\displaystyle\leq(K+1)\sqrt{2\pi K}e^{-\lambda R^{-1}}\leq C^{\prime}e^{-\lambda R^{-1}}

as long as
R\geq 2C
large enough, for some
C^{\prime}>0
.

We turn to estimating
R_{k,2}^{K}
. By
Lemma

A.1
we can bound
\Gamma(\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+d_{1}/2)\leq C^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert!
so that for all
l\leq\lfloor\lambda R^{-1}\rfloor+1
, for
R>0
large enough,

\displaystyle r^{d_{1}}\lambda^{d_{1}/2}\sum_{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert<l}\Gamma\left(\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\frac{d_{1}}{2}\right)\left(\frac{2}{\lambda r^{2}}\right)^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\frac{d_{1}}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert<l}\left(\frac{C}{r^{2}\lambda}\right)^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert!=\sum_{j=0}^{l-1}\left(\frac{C}{r^{2}\lambda}\right)^{j}j!\sum_{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert=j}1

(C.3)

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{j=0}^{l-1}\sqrt{j}\left(\frac{Cd_{1}j}{er^{2}\lambda}\right)^{j}\leq\sqrt{\lambda R^{-1}}\sum_{j\geq 0}\left(\frac{d_{1}CR^{-1}}{er^{2}}\right)^{j}\leq C\lambda^{1/2}\,.

where we used Stirling’s estimate,
Eq.

A.1
, and assumed
R\geq Cd_{1}r^{-2}
large enough.

Additionally, using Stirling’s estimate and
k<K\leq 2R^{-1}\lambda
and taking
R>2C_{p}
, we have

C_{p}^{k}k!\lambda^{-k}\leq\sqrt{2\pi k}\left(\frac{C_{p}k}{e\lambda}\right)^{k}\leq\sqrt{2\pi k}e^{-k}\leq C^{\prime}\sqrt{\lambda}\,.

Thus, we can conclude that, using
Eq.

C.3
with
l=K-k
,

\left\lvert\lambda^{-k}R_{k,2}^{K-k}({b},\lambda)\right\rvert\leq e^{-\frac{1}{4}\lambda r^{2}}C\lambda^{1/2-d_{1}/2}M_{p}C_{p}^{k}k!\lambda^{-k}\leq e^{-\frac{1}{4}\lambda r^{2}}\lambda^{1-\frac{d_{1}}{2}}CC^{\prime}\leq C^{\prime\prime}e^{-\varepsilon\lambda}\,,

for some
C^{\prime\prime}>0
.
∎

The following theorem is proved in the same way as
[
Sjö80a
, Prop. 1.4]
, but due to some differences in the statement, we give the full proof.

Theorem C.4

(Classical Analytic Stationary Phase)
.

Let
{A}_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N},{B}_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N^{\prime}}
denote open sets where
N,N^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
and let
(\hat{a},\hat{b})\in A_{0}\times B_{0}
be fixed. Let
A_{0}{C}\supset A_{0}
and
B_{0}{C}\supset B_{0}
be open complex neighborhoods of
A_{0}
and
B_{0}
respectively and let
\underline{q}=(q_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{O}(A_{0}{C}\times B_{0}{C})
be a formal classical analytic amplitude.

Let
\Phi\in\mathcal{O}({A}_{0}{C}\times{B}_{0}{C})
be holomorphic satisfying

\nabla_{{a}}\Phi(\hat{{a}},\hat{{b}})=0\,,\quad\mathrm{det}\partial_{{a}}^{2}\Phi(\hat{{a}},\hat{{b}})\neq 0\,,\quad\mathrm{Im}\Phi|_{A_{0}\times B_{0}}\geq 0\,.

(C.4)

For any small enough open
A\subset A_{0}
with
\hat{a}\in A
there exist open
B\subset B_{0}
with
\hat{b}\in B
and
\vartheta_{0}>0
so that for all
0<\vartheta<\vartheta_{0}
and any function
\tau\colon\mathbb{R}_{>0}\to\mathbb{N}
satisfying

\vartheta\lambda/2\leq\tau(\lambda)\leq\vartheta\lambda

the following is true.

Let
(\chi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(A_{0})
be a sequence of Ehrenpreis cut-offs compactly supported inside
A
and identically
1
in a (real) neighborhood of
\hat{a}
we call
A^{\prime}\subset A
.

For every
\lambda\geq 1
large enough, there is a formal classical analytic amplitude

\underline{q^{\sharp}}({b})
so that if
q^{\sharp}({b};\lambda)
is any finite realization of
\underline{q^{\sharp}}({b})
and
q(a,b;\lambda)
is any finite realization of
\underline{q}(a,b)

then for some
\varepsilon>0
and all
b\in B
,

\lambda^{-\frac{{N}}{2}}e^{i\lambda\Phi(\mathbf{{a}}({b}),{b})}q^{\sharp}({b};\lambda)=\int e^{i\lambda\Phi({a},{b})}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}({a})q({a},{b};\lambda)\mathrm{d}{a}+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})\,.

(C.5)

Furthermore, if

q_{0}(\hat{a},\hat{b})\neq 0\,,

(C.6)

then
\underline{q^{\sharp}}
is elliptic in
B
, provided
B
is sufficiently contracted about
\hat{b}
.

Proof.

This proof is segmented into nine parts. The contour deformations made in this proof are in the spirit of
[
Sjö82
, Thm. 2.8]
,
[
BJ20
, Prop. 1.2]
and those alluded at in
[
Tre22
, § 19.3.1]
.

1. Preparations

First we record a consequence of the holomorphic implicit function thereom. Namely, there exist complex open neighborhoods
A{C}
of
\hat{a}
and
B{C}
of
\hat{b}
and a holomorphic function
\mathbf{a}\colon B{C}\to A{C}
so that

(a,b)\in A{C}\times B{C}\colon\nabla_{a}\Phi(a,b)=0\iff a=\mathbf{a}(b)\,,

(C.7)

and
\mathbf{a}(\hat{b})=\hat{a}
.

Let us thus first take
A=\pi{R}A{C}
and
B=\pi{R}B{C}
. Throughout this proof we adopt the following convention: when we write that we contract
{A}
about
\hat{{a}}
or write that we choose
{A}
small enough, we mean that we choose
\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}
according to a small enough
{A}
(and thus also a modified (smaller)
{A}^{\prime}
). This will entail the constant determining the growth of this cut-off to possibly increase, which we will absorb into
C_{q}
, the growth of the amplitude
q_{k}
, by assuming the amplitude grows more quickly. This will done at most finitely many times.

Furthermore, we choose to extend
\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}
as a
C^{\infty}
function into
\mathbb{C}
as follows:
\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}=\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}{C}=\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}\circ\pi{R}
, where
\pi{R}
is the projection onto the real part of any complex value.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
be arbitrary. We shall consider the expression

\int_{{A}}e^{i\lambda\Phi({a};{b})}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}({a})q_{k}({a},{b})\mathrm{d}{a}\,.

(C.8)

The rest of the proof is organized as follows: we will consider
k
to be fixed for parts
2.
through
7.
, performing calculations on the RHS of
Eq.

C.8
, with part
7.
containing an application of stationary phase
[
Tre22
, Thm. 19.3.3]
with estimates from
Lemma

C.3
. In parts
8.
and
9.
we allow
k
to be variable and embed the previous calculations into the formal amplitude framework from
Definition

2.2
and
[
Sjö82
, § 1]
.

2. First Contour Deformation

In order to apply stationary phase to the integral in
Eq.

C.8
, we require that there exists exactly one non-degenerate critical point of the exponent in the domain of integration. However, we are integrating over
{A}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
, so that the critical point of
\Phi
guaranteed by
Eq.

C.7
may not lie in this domain. In order to remedy this, we use a contour deformation.

We define the contour
\Gamma_{s}
on
{A}\times{B}
,

\Gamma_{s}({a},{b})={a}-s\hat{{a}}+s\mathbf{a}({b})\,,

and, after fixing
{b}\in{B}
arbitrary, we let
M={A}\times\{{b}\}\times[0,1]
and define

H\colon M\to\mathbb{C}^{N}\,,({a},{b},s)\mapsto\Gamma_{s}({a},{b})\,,

and we choose
{A},{B}
small enough so that the image of
H
is in the domain of the holomorphic extensions of
q_{k}
and
\Phi
we have chosen above.

By holomorphicity,

\mathrm{d}\left(H^{\ast}(e^{i\lambda\Phi}q_{k}\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{N})\right)=H^{\ast}\left(\bar{\partial}_{{a}}\left(e^{i\lambda\Phi}q_{k}\right)\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{N}\right)=0\,,

(C.9)

where
H^{\ast}
is the pull-back for
H
and we used the fact that

(\partial_{{a}}e^{i\lambda\Phi}q_{k})\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{1}\dots\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{N}=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\partial_{{a}^{j}}e^{i\lambda\Phi}q_{k}\mathrm{d}{a}^{j}\right)\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{1}\dots\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{N}=0\,.

According to
Eq.

C.9
we then have

\mathrm{d}\left(H^{\ast}(e^{i\lambda\Phi}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}q_{k}\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{N})\right)=H^{\ast}(e^{i\lambda\Phi}q_{k}(\bar{\partial}_{{a}}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)})\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{N})\,,

where
(\bar{\partial}_{{a}}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)})
is supported away from
\hat{{a}}
.

Now applying Stokes’ theorem to the
N
-form
H^{\ast}(e^{i\lambda\Phi}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}q_{k}\mathrm{d}{a}^{1}\dots\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{N})
over
M
, we find for the integral in the RHS of
Eq.

C.8
,

\displaystyle\int_{{A}}e^{i\lambda\Phi({a};{b})}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}({a})q_{k}({a},{b})\mathrm{d}{a}

\displaystyle=\int_{{A}}e^{i\lambda\Phi({a}-\hat{{a}}+\mathbf{a}({b});{b})}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}({a}-\hat{{a}}+\mathbf{a}({b}))q_{k}({a}-\hat{{a}}+\mathbf{a}({b}),{b})\mathrm{d}{a}

(C.10)

\displaystyle-\int_{M}H^{\ast}(e^{i\lambda\Phi}(\bar{\partial}_{{a}}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)})q_{k}\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{N})\,,

where the integral over the set
\partial{A}\times[0,1]
vanished because
\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}({a}-s\hat{{a}}+s\mathbf{a}({b}))
is identically
0
in
\partial{A}\times[0,1]
when
{b}=\hat{{b}}
, and thus this must also be true for all
{b}\in{B}
when
{B}
is chosen small enough (depending on
{A}
).

We will show later (in part
5.
) that the integral over
M
, that is to say the second term in the RHS of
Eq.

C.10
, is exponentially decaying. However, before we do so we will carry out two more contour deformations.

3. Second Contour Deformation

Giving the new phase a name,

\tilde{\Phi}({a};{b})\coloneqq\Phi(\Gamma_{1}({a},{b});{b})=\Phi({a}-\hat{{a}}+\mathbf{a}({b});{b})\,,

(C.11)

we see that that
\tilde{\Phi}
has a unique non-degenerate critical point in
{A}{C}
at
{a}=\hat{{a}}
. That this is the unique stationary point comes from
Eq.

C.7
. That it is non-degenerate comes from the calculation

\partial_{{a}}^{2}\tilde{\Phi}(\hat{{a}};{b})=\partial_{{a}}^{2}\Phi({a}-\hat{{a}}+\mathbf{a}({b});{b})|_{{a}=\hat{{a}}}=(\partial_{a}^{2}\Phi)(\mathbf{a}(b);b)\,,

which by
Eq.

C.4
is invertible at
b=\hat{b}
and thus also in a small neighborhood around
\hat{b}
.

Thus, choosing
{B}
small enough, we see that for all
{a}\in{A}{C}

\nabla_{{a}}\tilde{\Phi}({a};{b})=0\iff{a}=\hat{{a}}\,,\,\mathrm{det}\partial_{{a}}^{2}\tilde{\Phi}({a};{b})\neq 0\,.

(C.12)

However, notice that we are in the position that we have no guarantee that
\mathrm{Im}\partial_{a}^{2}\Phi(\hat{a};\hat{b})
is
non-degenerate. We will remedy this with another contour deformation.

Thus, for some fixed small
\delta>0
that we will choose later, we define the contour

\Gamma^{\delta}_{s}({a},{b})={a}+is\delta\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};{b})

(C.13)

where in the definition of
\Gamma^{\delta}_{s}({a},{b})
,
\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}
is meant as the holomorphic extension of the real-analytic complex valued map
\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}
, so that
\Gamma^{\delta}_{s}
is holomorphic with respect to
{a},{b}
.

Fixing
{b}\in{B}
arbitrary, we let
M^{\delta}={A}\times\{{b}\}\times[0,1]
and define

H^{\delta}\colon M^{\delta}\to\mathbb{C}^{N}\,,\quad({a},{b},s)\mapsto\Gamma^{\delta}_{s}({a},{b})\,.

Following the argument in part
2.
of this proof, putting

\tilde{q}_{k}({a},{b})=q_{k}({a}-\hat{{a}}+\mathbf{a}({b}),{b})\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda)}({a},{b})=\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}({a}-\hat{{a}}+\mathbf{a}({b}),{b})\,,

(C.14)

we see

\mathrm{d}(H^{\delta})^{\ast}(e^{i\lambda\tilde{\Phi}}\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda)}\tilde{q}_{k}\mathrm{d}{a}^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{N})=(H^{\delta})^{\ast}(e^{i\lambda\tilde{\Phi}}\tilde{q}_{k}(\bar{\partial}_{{a}}\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda)})\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{N})\,,

where we assumed that
\delta>0
was chosen small enough for the image of
H^{\delta}
to be in the domain of the holomorphic functions
\tilde{\Phi}
and
\tilde{q}_{k}
.

Applying Stokes’ theorem on
M^{\delta}
to the first term in the RHS of
Eq.

C.10
,

\displaystyle\int_{{A}}e^{i\lambda\tilde{\Phi}}\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda)}\tilde{q}_{k}\mathrm{d}{a}

\displaystyle=\int_{{A}}e^{i\lambda\tilde{\Phi}({a}+i\delta\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};{b});{b})}(\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda)}\tilde{q}_{k})({a}+i\delta\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};{b}),{b})\det J_{{a}}\Gamma^{\delta}_{1}({a},{b})\mathrm{d}{a}

(C.15)

\displaystyle-\int_{M^{\delta}}(H^{\delta})^{\ast}\left(e^{i\lambda\tilde{\Phi}}\tilde{q}_{k}(\bar{\partial}_{{a}}\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda)})\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{N}\right)\,,

where the boundary integral over
\partial{A}\times[0,1]
vanished because
\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda)}({a}+is\delta\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};{b}),{b})
is identically zero in
\partial{A}\times[0,1]
when
\delta=0
and
{b}=\hat{{b}}
so that this is also true for a small enough
\delta>0
and small enough
{B}
. Here and throughout this proof
J_{{a}}
denotes the Jacobian with respect to
{a}
.

We will deal with the term in
Eq.

C.15
integrating over
M^{\delta}
in part
6.
and show that it is exponentially decaying.

We claim now that, putting

\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}({a};{b})\coloneqq\tilde{\Phi}({a}+i\delta\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};{b});{b})\,,

(C.16)

we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{{a}}\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}({a};{b})=0\iff{a}=\hat{{a}}\,,\,\mathrm{det}\partial_{{a}}^{2}\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}({a};{b})\neq 0\,,\,\mathrm{det}\,\mathrm{Im}\partial_{{a}}^{2}\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}({a};{b})\neq 0\,.

(C.17)

We begin proving this claim.

We use throughout this proof the fact that
\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}({a};{b})=\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};{b})
for
({a},{b})\in{A}\times{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N+N^{\prime}}
.

First, using the Taylor expansion and
\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}(\hat{{a}};{b})=0
,

\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};{b})=\partial_{{a}}^{2}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}(\hat{{a}};{b})({a}-\hat{{a}})+\mathcal{O}(\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert^{2})\,,

where from
Eq.

C.12
the non-degeneracy of
\partial_{{a}}^{2}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}(\hat{{a}},{b})
near
\hat{{b}}
tells us that, for
{A},{B}
small enough, for some
C_{1},C_{2}>0

C_{1}\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};{b})\right\rvert\leq C_{2}\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert\,.

(C.18)

Using
Eq.

C.18
, for
\delta>0
small enough,

\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}+i\delta\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};{b})\right\rvert\geq(1-\delta C_{2})\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert\,,\quad\text{and so}\quad\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}+i\delta\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};{b})\right\rvert=0\iff{a}=\hat{{a}}\,,

so that together with
Eq.

C.12
, for
\delta>0
small enough, and
\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}
defined in
Eq.

C.16
,

\nabla_{{a}}\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}({a};{b})=0\iff\nabla_{{a}}\tilde{\Phi}({a}+i\delta\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};{b});{b})=0\iff{a}+i\delta\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};{b})=\hat{{a}}\iff{a}=\hat{{a}}\,.

Now let us check the first non-degeneracy claim in
Eq.

C.17
, where due to continuity it suffices to check that

\mathrm{det}\partial_{{a}}^{2}\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}(\hat{{a}};\hat{{b}})\neq 0\,,\,\mathrm{det}\,\mathrm{Im}\partial_{{a}}^{2}\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}(\hat{{a}};\hat{{b}})\neq 0

and choosing
{B}
small enough. Using the chain rule we see

\partial_{{a}}^{2}\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}(\hat{{a}};\hat{{b}})=(\partial_{{a}}^{2}\tilde{\Phi})(\hat{{a}};\hat{{b}})(\mathrm{id}+i\delta\partial_{{a}}^{2}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}(\hat{{a}};\hat{{b}}))\,,

where according to
Eq.

C.12
we know that
(\partial_{{a}}^{2}\tilde{\Phi})(\hat{{a}};\hat{{b}})
is non-degenerate, and thus choosing
\delta>0
small enough, the perturbation is small enough so that
\partial_{{a}}^{2}\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}(\hat{{a}};\hat{{b}})
is non-degenerate and we have thus already shown the first non-degeneracy claim of
Eq.

C.17
.

To show the second non-degeneracy claim in
Eq.

C.17
, we use the Taylor expansion to see that, recalling
\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}=\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}
on
{A}\times{B}
,

\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}({a};\hat{{b}})=\tilde{\Phi}({a};\hat{{b}})+i\delta\left\lvert\nabla_{{a}}\tilde{\Phi}({a};\hat{{b}})\right\rvert^{2}+\mathcal{O}\left(\delta^{2}\left\lvert\nabla_{{a}}\tilde{\Phi}({a};\hat{{b}})\right\rvert^{2}\right)

so that, noting
\tilde{\Phi}({a};\hat{{b}})=\Phi({a};\hat{{b}})
has non-negative imaginary part (by
Eq.

C.4
),

\mathrm{Im}\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}({a};\hat{{b}})\geq\delta\left\lvert\nabla_{{a}}\tilde{\Phi}({a};\hat{{b}})\right\rvert^{2}+\mathcal{O}\left(\delta^{2}\left\lvert\nabla_{{a}}\tilde{\Phi}({a};\hat{{b}})\right\rvert^{2}\right)\,.

and using
Eq.

C.18
(which is also true for
\tilde{\Phi}
in
{A}\times{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N+N^{\prime}}
)

\mathrm{Im}\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}({a};\hat{{b}})\geq C_{\delta}\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert^{2}

(C.19)

for some
C_{\delta}>0
when
{A}
and
\delta>0
are chosen small enough.

According to Taylor’s expansion we now have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}({a};\hat{{b}})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Im}\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}(\hat{{a}};\hat{{b}})+\mathrm{Im}\nabla_{{a}}\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}(\hat{{a}};\hat{{b}})\cdot({a}-\hat{{a}})+\mathrm{Im}\partial_{{a}}^{2}\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}(\hat{{a}};\hat{{b}})({a}-\hat{{a}})\cdot({a}-\hat{{a}})+\mathcal{O}(\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert^{3})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Im}\partial_{{a}}^{2}\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}(\hat{{a}};\hat{{b}})({a}-\hat{{a}})\cdot({a}-\hat{{a}})+\mathcal{O}(\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert^{3})\,,

which, combined with
Eq.

C.19
and choosing
{A}
small, gives for some
C_{\delta}^{\prime}>0
,

\left\lvert\mathrm{Im}\partial_{{a}}^{2}\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}(\hat{{a}};\hat{{b}})({a}-\hat{{a}})\cdot({a}-\hat{{a}})\right\rvert\geq C_{\delta}^{\prime}\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert^{2}

for all
{a}
in a (real) neighborhood of
\hat{{a}}
. Therefore, we may conclude that
\mathrm{Im}\partial_{{a}}^{2}\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}(\hat{{a}};\hat{{b}})
is non-degenerate, concluding the proof of the claim.

4. Morse Lemma and Third Contour Deformation

We apply holomorphic Morse’s lemma to
\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}
with respect to
{a}
(see
[
Tre22
, Lem. 19.3.1]
), which we are allowed to do due to
Eq.

C.17
. That is to say, contracting
{A}{C},{B}{C}
about
\hat{{a}},\hat{{b}}
respectively if necessary, for each
{b}\in{B}{C}
there exists a biholomorphism
{a}\mapsto{a}+\kappa({a};{b})
from
{A}{C}
to an open subset of
{A}{C}
so that
\sup_{{b}\in{B}{C}}\left\lvert\kappa({a},{b})\right\rvert\lesssim\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert^{2}
and

\Phi^{\circ}({a};{b})\coloneqq\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}({a}+\kappa({a};{b});{b})=\Phi^{\circ}(\hat{{a}};{b})+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{{a}}^{2}\Phi^{\circ}(\hat{{a}};\hat{{b}})({a}-\hat{{a}})\cdot({a}-\hat{{a}})\,.

(C.20)

We will want to use another contour deformation in order to have the phase
\Phi^{\circ}({a};{b})
inside the first integral on the RHS of
Eq.

C.10
. Thus we define the contour

\tilde{\Gamma}_{s}({a},{b})={a}+s\kappa({a},{b})

(C.21)

and fixing
{b}\in{B}
arbitrary, we let
\tilde{M}={A}\times\{{b}\}\times[0,1]
and define

\tilde{H}\colon\tilde{M}\to\mathbb{C}^{N},({a},{b},s)\mapsto\tilde{\Gamma}_{s}({a},{b})\,.

Following the argument in parts
2.
or
3.
of this proof, putting

\tilde{q}_{k,\delta}({a},{b})=\tilde{q}_{k}({a}+i\delta\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a},{b});{b})\det J_{{a}}\Gamma_{1}^{\delta}({a},{b})\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda),\delta}({a},{b})=\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda)}({a}+i\delta\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};{b}),{b})\,,

(C.22)

we see

\mathrm{d}\tilde{H}^{\ast}(e^{i\lambda\tilde{\Phi}}\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda),\delta}\tilde{q}_{k,\delta}\mathrm{d}{a}^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{N})=\tilde{H}^{\ast}(e^{i\lambda\tilde{\Phi}}\tilde{q}_{k,\delta}(\bar{\partial}_{{a}}\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda),\delta})\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{N})\,,

and applying Stokes’ theorem on
\tilde{M}
to the first term in the RHS of
Eq.

C.15
,

\displaystyle\int_{{A}}e^{i\lambda\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}}(\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda),\delta}\tilde{q}_{k,\delta})({a},{b})\mathrm{d}{a}

\displaystyle=\int_{{A}}e^{i\lambda\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}({a}+\kappa({a},{b});{b})}(\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda),\delta}\tilde{q}_{k,\delta})({a}+\kappa({a},{b}),{b})\det J_{{a}}\tilde{\Gamma}_{1}({a},{b})\mathrm{d}{a}

(C.23)

\displaystyle-\int_{\tilde{M}}\tilde{H}^{\ast}(e^{i\lambda\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}}\tilde{q}_{k,\delta}(\bar{\partial}_{{a}}\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda),\delta})\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{N})\,,

where the integral over the set
\partial{A}\times[0,1]
vanished due to the following argument:

Let
\inf\{\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert\colon{a}\in\partial{A}\}\eqqcolon\varepsilon>0
, and unraveling the definition of
\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda),\delta}
, we know that for all
s\in[0,1]
, at
{b}=\hat{{b}}
we have

\mathrm{supp}\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda),\delta}({a}+s\kappa({a},\hat{{b}}),\hat{{b}})\subset\left\{\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}+s\kappa({a},\hat{{b}})+i\delta\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};\hat{{b}})\right\rvert<\varepsilon^{\prime}\right\}\,.

for some
0<\varepsilon^{\prime}<\varepsilon
depending only on the choice of
\mathrm{supp}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}\subset{A}
, which we can contract about
\hat{{a}}
as we like.
Using the fact that
\sup_{{b}\in{B}}\left\lvert\kappa({a},{b})\right\rvert\lesssim\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert^{2}
and choosing
\delta>0
small enough (depending on these
\varepsilon^{\prime},\varepsilon
), we then see that

s\in[0,1],{a}\in\partial{A}\implies\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}+s\kappa({a},\hat{{b}})+i\delta\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};\hat{{b}})\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert-\mathcal{O}(\delta)-\mathcal{O}(\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert^{2})>\varepsilon/2>2\varepsilon^{\prime}\,,

for
\mathrm{supp}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}\subset{A}
chosen small enough. By a perturbation argument, choosing
{B}
small enough we see that the integrand present in the boundary integral over
[0,1]\times\partial{A}
arising from Stokes’ theorem vanishes on
[0,1]\times\partial{A}
.

We will now argue why the second terms in the right-hand-sides of
Eq.

C.10
,
Eq.

C.15
and
Eq.

C.23
are exponentially decaying.

5. Stokes’ Theorem Remainders I

In this and the next part of the proof we will consider the the second terms in the right-hand-sides of
Eqs.

C.10
,
C.15
and
C.23
, and show that they are exponentially decaying.

First note that
\bar{\partial}_{{a}}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\bar{\partial}_{{a}^{j}}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}\mathrm{d}\bar{{a}}^{j}\,,
so that by
[
Lee13
, Lem. 14.16]
,

H^{\ast}\left(e^{i\lambda\Phi}q_{k}(\bar{\partial}_{{a}}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)})\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{N}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}(e^{i\lambda\Phi}q_{k}\bar{\partial}_{{a}^{j}}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)})\circ H\mathrm{d}\bar{H}^{j}\wedge\mathrm{d}H^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\mathrm{d}H^{N}

Now abusing notation by writing
(s,{a}_{1},\dots,{a}_{N})
for coordinates on
M=[0,1]\times{A}
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\bar{H}^{j}\wedge\mathrm{d}H^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\mathrm{d}H^{N}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial s},\frac{\partial}{\partial{a}^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial{a}^{N}}\right)=\det\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{s}\bar{H}^{j}&(\nabla_{{a}}\bar{H}^{j})^{T}\\
\partial_{s}H&J_{{a}}H\end{pmatrix}\eqqcolon\tilde{J}_{j}H\,.

where
J_{{a}}H
is the Jacobian of
H
with respect to
{a}
.

We thus see that, using Fubini’s theorem, for the second term in the RHS of
Eq.

C.10
,

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle\int_{M}H^{\ast}\left(e^{i\lambda\Phi}q_{k}(\bar{\partial}_{{a}}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)})\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{N}\right)

(C.24)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{{A}}e^{i\lambda\Phi({a}-s\hat{{a}}+s\mathbf{a}({b});{b})}\bar{\partial}_{{a}^{j}}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}({a}-s\hat{{a}}+s\mathbf{a}({b}),{b})w_{k,j}(s,{a},{b})\mathrm{d}{a}\mathrm{d}s\,.

where

w_{k,j}(s,{a},{b})=q_{k}({a}-s\hat{{a}}+s\mathbf{a}({b}),{b})(\tilde{J}_{j}H)(s,{a},{b})\,,

satisfies for every
j\in\{1,\dots,N\},{b}\in{B}
and all multi-indices
\alpha
the bound

\sup_{s\in[0,1],{a}\in{A}}\left\lvert\partial^{\alpha}_{{a}}w_{k,j}\right\rvert\leq M_{w}C_{q}^{k}k!C_{q}^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}\alpha!\,,

(C.25)

since
\tilde{J}_{j}H
is real-analytic (and we assumed that
C_{q}
is larger than the analytic bound on
\tilde{J}_{j}H
). Here
M_{w}>0
is some constant.

We now wish to apply
Lemma

C.2
using
Eq.

C.25
for which we have to set up two things: argue why the imaginary part of the phase
\Phi^{i\mathbb{R}}({a}-s\hat{{a}}+s\mathbf{a}({b});{b})
is bounded below by a small negative number, and that the phase has no critical point in the support of the cut-off
\bar{\partial}_{{a}^{j}}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}({a}-s\hat{{a}}+s\mathbf{a}({b}),{b})
. We do this now:

For any
\varepsilon_{1}>0
we may choose
{B}
small enough so that for all
s\in[0,1]
we have
\Phi^{i\mathbb{R}}({a}-s\hat{{a}}+s\mathbf{a}({b});{b})\geq-\varepsilon_{1}
, because
\Phi^{i\mathbb{R}}({a}-s\hat{{a}}+s\mathbf{a}(\hat{{b}});\hat{{b}})=0
. Furthermore, since
\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}
is identically
1
near
\hat{{a}}
, we know that
\bar{\partial}_{{a}^{j}}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}({a}-s\hat{{a}}+s\mathbf{a}({b}),{b})
is supported away from
\hat{{a}}
for
{B}
small enough, so that
\Phi({a}-s\hat{{a}}+s\mathbf{a}({b});{b})
has no critical point with respect to
{a}
on the support of
\bar{\partial}_{{a}^{j}}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}({a}-s\hat{{a}}+s\mathbf{a}({b}),{b})
.

We now apply
Lemma

C.2
to see that, for some
\vartheta_{0}>0
and all
0<\vartheta\leq\vartheta_{0}
, if

\vartheta\lambda/2\leq\tau(\lambda)+1\leq\vartheta\lambda\,,

we have, choosing
{B}
small enough so that
\varepsilon_{1}=\vartheta/4
, and using
Eq.

C.24
,

\left\lvert\int_{M}H^{\ast}\left(e^{i\lambda\Phi}q_{k}(\bar{\partial}_{{a}}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)})\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{N}\right)\right\rvert\leq M_{w}C_{q}^{k}k!e^{-\frac{1}{4}\vartheta\lambda}\,.

(C.26)

This concludes the estimation of the second term on the RHS of
Eq.

C.10
. We will use the same arguments in the following part.

6. Stokes’ Theorem Remainders II

Moving on to the terms coming from
Eq.

C.15
and
Eq.

C.23
, arguments similar to those at the beginning of part
5.
yield

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle\int_{M^{\delta}}(H^{\delta})^{\ast}\left(e^{i\lambda\tilde{\Phi}}\tilde{q}_{k}(\bar{\partial}_{{a}}\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda)})\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{N}\right)

(C.27)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{{A}}e^{i\lambda\tilde{\Phi}({a}+is\delta\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};{b});{b})}\bar{\partial}_{{a}^{j}}\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda)}({a}+is\delta\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};{b}),{b})w_{k,j,\delta}(s,{a},{b})\mathrm{d}{a}\mathrm{d}s

and

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{M}}\tilde{H}^{\ast}\left(e^{i\lambda\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}}\tilde{q}_{k,\delta}(\bar{\partial}_{{a}}\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda),\delta})\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{N}\right)

(C.28)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{{A}}e^{i\lambda\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}({a}+s\kappa({a},{b});{b})}\bar{\partial}_{{a}^{j}}\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda),\delta}({a}+s\kappa({a},{b}),{b})\tilde{w}_{k,j,\delta}(s,{a},{b})\mathrm{d}{a}\mathrm{d}s

where

\sup_{s\in[0,1],{a}\in{A}}\left\lvert\partial^{\alpha}_{{a}}w_{k,j,\delta}\right\rvert\leq M_{w}^{\prime}k!C_{q}^{k+\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}\alpha!\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\sup_{s\in[0,1],{a}\in{A}}\left\lvert\partial^{\alpha}_{{a}}\tilde{w}_{k,j,\delta}\right\rvert\leq\tilde{M}_{b}k!C_{q}^{k+\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}\alpha!\,,

(C.29)

and we made the assumption that
C_{q}
was large enough and
M_{w}^{\prime},\tilde{M}_{b}>0
are some constants.

Using
Eq.

C.29
, we will want to apply
Lemma

C.2
again to show exponential decay, so we will verify that the phases in both of the integrals on the right-hand-sides of
Eqs.

C.27
and
C.28
have imaginary part bounded below by a small negative number and have no critical point in the support of the integrand.

We turn first to
Eq.

C.27
.
Notice that, writing
\delta^{\prime}=s\delta>0
for
s>0
we know by

Eq.

C.19
that

\tilde{\Phi}^{i\mathbb{R}}({a}+is\delta\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};{b});{b})=\mathrm{Im}\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta^{\prime}}({a};\hat{{b}})\geq C_{\delta^{\prime}}\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert^{2}\,,

which is non-negative. At
s=0
and
{b}=\hat{{b}}
, we know by definition of
\tilde{\Phi}
(see
Eq.

C.11
) that
\tilde{\Phi}^{i\mathbb{R}}({a}+is\delta\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};{b});{b})=0
. Thus, by a perturbation argument, for every
\varepsilon_{1}>0
, choosing
{B}
small enough, we can maintain that

s\in[0,1],{b}\in{B}\implies\tilde{\Phi}^{i\mathbb{R}}({a}+is\delta\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};{b});{b})\geq-\varepsilon_{1}\,.

Furthermore, for
s=0
and
{b}=\hat{{b}}
, we know

\tilde{\Phi}^{i\mathbb{R}}({a}+is\delta\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};{b});{b})=\tilde{\Phi}^{i\mathbb{R}}({a};\hat{{b}})=\Phi^{i\mathbb{R}}({a};\hat{{b}})\,,

which has a unique critical point in
{A}
at
\hat{{a}}
.

For
0<s\leq 1
, writing
\delta^{\prime}=\delta s>0
, we know from

Eq.

C.17
that
\tilde{\Phi}({a}+is\delta\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};{b});{b})=\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta^{\prime}}({a};{b})
has a unique critical point in
{A}
at
\hat{{a}}
. Thus, in total, for all
0\leq s\leq 1
and all
\delta>0
small and
{B}
small,
\tilde{\Phi}^{i\mathbb{R}}({a}+is\delta\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};{b});{b})
has the unique critical point
\hat{{a}}
in
{A}
.

Because for
\delta=0
and
{b}=\hat{{b}}
we know that
\bar{\partial}_{{a}^{j}}\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda)}({a}+is\delta\nabla_{{a}}\bar{\tilde{\Phi}}^{\mathrm{hol}}({a};{b}),{b})
is identically
0
near
\hat{{a}}
this is also true for small
\delta>0
and
{B}
small, so that the critical point of the phase does not lie in the support of this cut-off.

We turn to the phase in
Eq.

C.28
. Due to the fact that
\sup_{{b}\in{B}}\left\lvert\kappa({a},{b})\right\rvert\lesssim\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert^{2}
, we know by
Eq.

C.19
that

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}^{i\mathbb{R}}({a}+s\kappa({a},\hat{{b}});\hat{{b}})

\displaystyle\geq C_{\delta}\left\lvert{a}+s\kappa({a},\hat{{b}})-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert^{2}\geq C_{\delta}\left(\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert^{2}-s^{2}\left\lvert\kappa({a},\hat{{b}})\right\rvert^{2}-2s\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert\left\lvert\kappa({a},\hat{{b}})\right\rvert\right)

\displaystyle\geq C_{\delta}^{\prime}\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert^{2}\,,

for some
C_{\delta}^{\prime}>0
uniform for
s\in[0,1]
, so that choosing
{B}
small enough, we can maintain

s\in[0,1],{b}\in{B}\implies\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}^{i\mathbb{R}}({a}+s\kappa({a},{b});{b})\geq-\varepsilon_{1}\,,

for any
\varepsilon_{1}>0
.
Note that

\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}+s\kappa({a},{b})\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert-sC\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert^{2}\,,

where we used
\sup_{{b}\in{B}}\left\lvert\kappa({a},{b})\right\rvert\lesssim\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert^{2}
. Thus, for
{A}
(and
{B}
) small enough, because
\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}
has a unique critical point at
\hat{{a}}
, we see that the phase
\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}({a}+s\kappa({a},{b});{b})
has a unique critical point in
{A}
at
\hat{{a}}
.

We check that
\bar{\partial}_{{a}^{j}}\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda),\delta}({a}+s\kappa({a},{b});{b})
is not supported near
{a}=\hat{{a}}
: we know that
\bar{\partial}_{{a}^{j}}\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda),\delta}({a},{b})
is not supported near
\hat{{a}}
and because
\left\lvert\kappa({a},{b})\right\rvert\lesssim\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert^{2}
this conclusion also holds for
\bar{\partial}_{{a}^{j}}\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda),\delta}({a}+s\kappa({a},{b});{b})
when
{A}
and
{B}
are sufficiently small.

We have thus verified that both of the phases appearing in
Eqs.

C.27
and
C.28
have no critical point in the support of the integrand and have imaginary parts bounded below by
-\varepsilon_{1}
, which can be chosen as small as we like by choosing
\delta>0
and
{B}
small. In particular, we do
not
have to shrink
{A}
(and thus increase
C_{q}
) to make
\varepsilon_{1}>0
as small as we wish.

Applying
Lemma

C.2
, for some
\varepsilon_{2}>0
, we deduce, for
L
large enough,

\left\lvert\int_{M^{\delta}}(H^{\delta})^{\ast}\left(e^{i\lambda\tilde{\Phi}}\tilde{q}_{k}(\bar{\partial}_{{a}}\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda)})\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{N}\right)\right\rvert\leq M_{w}^{\prime}C_{q}^{k}k!e^{-\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{2}\lambda}\,,

(C.30)

and

\left\lvert\int_{\tilde{M}}\tilde{H}^{\ast}\left(e^{i\lambda\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}}\tilde{q}_{k,\delta}(\bar{\partial}_{{a}}\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda),\delta})\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\mathrm{d}{a}^{N}\right)\right\rvert\leq\tilde{M}_{w}C_{q}^{k}k!e^{-\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{2}\lambda}\,.

(C.31)

7. Stationary Phase

Recapping the past six parts of this proof, we have shown, using Stokes’ theorem in
Eq.

C.10
,
Eq.

C.15
, and
Eq.

C.23
, recalling the definition of
\Phi^{\circ}
from
Eq.

C.20
, that

\displaystyle\int_{A}e^{i\lambda\Phi(a;b)}

\displaystyle\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(a)q_{k}(a,b)\mathrm{d}a

(C.32)

\displaystyle=\int_{{A}}e^{i\lambda\Phi^{\circ}({a};{b})}(\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda),\delta}\tilde{q}_{k,\delta})({a}+\kappa({a},{b}),{b})\det J_{{a}}\tilde{\Gamma}_{1}({a},{b})\mathrm{d}{a}+R_{k,2}({b};\lambda)

with
R_{k,2}({b};\lambda)\leq Ce^{-\varepsilon\lambda}C_{q}^{k}k!
, where we used
Eqs.

C.26
,
C.30
and
C.31
for the estimates on the remainder integrals.

We will want to apply
[
Tre22
, Lem. 19.3.2]
to
\Phi^{\circ}
, for which, using
Eq.

C.20
, we make the calculation with the chain rule and using
\nabla_{{a}}\kappa(\hat{{a}},\hat{{b}})=0
to get

\mathrm{Im}\partial_{{a}}^{2}\Phi^{\circ}(\hat{{a}};\hat{{b}})=\mathrm{Im}\partial_{{a}}^{2}\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}(\hat{{a}};\hat{{b}})(\mathrm{id}+\nabla_{{a}}\kappa(\hat{{a}},\hat{{b}}))=\mathrm{Im}\partial_{{a}}^{2}\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}(\hat{{a}};\hat{{b}})\,.

which we know to be non-degenerate by
Eq.

C.17
.

We may thus apply
[
Tre22
, Lem. 19.3.2]
to
\Phi^{\circ}
to see that there is an invertible real
N\times N
-matrix
P
and real numbers
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{N}
so that

\Phi^{\circ}(\hat{{a}}+P{a};{b})=\Phi^{\circ}(\hat{{a}};{b})+\frac{1}{2}i\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}(1-i\sigma_{j}){a}_{j}^{2}\right)\,,

and we define

\displaystyle q_{k}^{\flat}({a},{b})

\displaystyle=\tilde{q}_{k,\delta}(\hat{{a}}+P{a}+\kappa\left(\hat{{a}}+P{a},{b}\right),{b})\det J_{{a}}\tilde{\Gamma}_{1}(\hat{{a}}+P{a},{b})

(C.33)

\displaystyle\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}^{\flat}({a},{b})

\displaystyle=\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda),\delta}(\hat{{a}}+P{a}+\kappa\left(\hat{{a}}+P{a},{b}\right),{b})\,,

in anticipation of using substitution.

Let us, however, first argue that
\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}^{\flat}({a};{b})
is identically
1
in a neighborhood around
{a}=0
(compare to the argument at the end of part
6.
). Without loss we make these considerations at
{b}=\hat{{b}}
. Unraveling the definition of
\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda),\delta}({a},\hat{{b}})
, we see that
\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda),\delta}
is identically
1
in a neighborhood of
{a}=\hat{{a}}
for
\delta>0
small. Then, due to the fact that
\left\lvert\kappa({a},\hat{{b}})\right\rvert\lesssim\left\lvert{a}-\hat{{a}}\right\rvert^{2}
, for
{A}
small enough, we see readily that
\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}^{\flat}({a};{b})
is identically
1
in a neighborhood of
{a}=0
.

Now, after using substitution with
{a}\mapsto\hat{{a}}+P{a}
,

\displaystyle\int_{{A}}e^{i\lambda\Phi^{\circ}({a};{b})}(\tilde{\chi}_{\tau(\lambda),\delta}\tilde{q}_{k})({a}+\kappa({a},{b}),{b})\det J_{{a}}\tilde{\Gamma}_{1}({a},{b})\mathrm{d}{a}

(C.34)

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\det P\right\rvert^{-1}e^{i\lambda\Phi(\mathbf{a}(b);b)}\int_{P^{-1}({A}-\hat{{a}})}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{N}(1-i\sigma_{j}){a}_{j}^{2}}\chi^{\flat}_{\tau(\lambda)}({a},{b})q^{\flat}_{k}({a},{b})\mathrm{d}{a}\,,

where we used the fact that
\Phi^{\circ}(\hat{{a}},{b})=\tilde{\Phi}_{\delta}(\hat{{a}};{b})=\tilde{\Phi}(\hat{{a}};{b})=\Phi(\mathbf{a}(b);b)
.

Now
P^{-1}({A}-\hat{{a}})\cap\{\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}^{\flat}\equiv 1\}
is an open neighborhood of the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
and will thus contain some open ball
\{\left\lvert{a}\right\rvert<r_{P}\}\eqqcolon\tilde{{A}}
.
We have
\inf_{{a}\in(P^{-1}({A}-\hat{{a}}))\setminus{\tilde{{A}}}}{\left\lvert{a}\right\rvert}\geq r_{P}>0
and so

\left\lvert\int_{(P^{-1}({A}-\hat{{a}}))\setminus\tilde{{A}}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{N}(1-i\sigma_{j}){a}_{j}^{2}}(\chi^{\flat}_{\tau(\lambda)}q^{\flat}_{k})({a},{b})\mathrm{d}{a}\right\rvert\leq Ce^{-\frac{1}{2}\lambda r_{P}^{2}}\sup_{(P^{-1}({A}-\hat{{a}}))\setminus\tilde{{A}}}\left\lvert q^{\flat}_{k}(\cdot,{b})\right\rvert\leq Ce^{-\frac{1}{2}\lambda r_{P}^{2}}M_{q}C_{q}^{k}k!\,.

(C.35)

Thus, combining
Eq.

C.32
with
Eq.

C.34
and
Eq.

C.35
we get

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle\int_{A}e^{i\lambda\Phi(a;b)}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(a)q_{k}(a,b)\mathrm{d}a

(C.36)

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\det P\right\rvert^{-1}e^{i\lambda\Phi(\mathbf{a}(b);b)}\left(\int_{\left\lvert{a}\right\rvert<r_{P}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{N}(1-i\sigma_{j}){a}_{j}^{2}}q^{\flat}_{k}({a},{b})\mathrm{d}{a}+R_{k,3}({b};\lambda)\right)+R_{k,2}({b};\lambda)

for some remainder
R_{k,3}({b};\lambda)
coming from
Eq.

C.35
satisfying for some
\varepsilon>0
,

\left\lvert R_{k,3}({b};\lambda)\right\rvert\leq Ce^{-\varepsilon\lambda}C_{q}^{k}k!\,.

Applying stationary phase, that is
Lemma

C.3
, to the integral term in the RHS of
Eq.

C.36
, for any
K>k
, we choose to expand
K-k
terms in the method of stationary phase to get

\displaystyle

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle\int_{\left\lvert{a}\right\rvert<r_{P}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{N}(1-i\sigma_{j}){a}_{j}^{2}}q^{\flat}_{k}({a},{b})\mathrm{d}{a}-R_{k}^{K-k}({b},\lambda)

(C.37)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{2\pi}{\lambda}\right)^{\frac{N}{2}}\sum_{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert<K-k}\frac{(2\lambda)^{-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}}{\alpha!}\sum_{j=1}^{N}(1-i\sigma_{j})^{-\alpha_{j}-\frac{1}{2}}(\partial_{{a}}^{2\alpha}q^{\flat}_{k})(0,{b})

with
R_{k}^{K-k}
satisfying
Eq.

C.2
, where each
(1-i\sigma_{j})^{1/2}
stands for the main branch of the square root.

8. Analytic Symbol

We now let
k
be variable and consider the expression defined by

q^{\sharp}_{k}(b)\coloneqq\left\lvert\det P\right\rvert^{-1}(2\pi)^{\frac{N}{2}}\sum_{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert\leq k}\frac{2^{-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}}{\alpha!}\sum_{j=1}^{N}(1-i\sigma_{j})^{-\alpha_{j}-\frac{1}{2}}(\partial_{{a}}^{2\alpha}q^{\flat}_{k-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert})(0,{b})\,.

(C.38)

Notice that by
Eq.

2.2
, we have for some
C>0
, (where we may assume the real-analytic bound on the Jacobian in
q^{\flat}_{k}
is smaller than
C_{q}
)

\left\lvert\left(\partial_{{a}}^{2\alpha}q^{\flat}_{k-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}\right)({a},{b})\right\rvert\leq CM_{q}C_{q}^{k+\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}(2\alpha)!(k-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert)!

so that

\left\lvert\frac{2^{-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}}{\alpha!}\sum_{j=1}^{N}(1-i\sigma_{j})^{-\alpha_{j}-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\partial_{{a}}^{2\alpha}q^{\flat}_{k-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}\right)(0,{b})\right\rvert\leq(N)2^{-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}\frac{(2\alpha)!}{\alpha!}M_{q}C_{q}^{k+\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}(k-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert)!

and taking into account that
(2\alpha)!/\alpha!\leq 4^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}\alpha!\leq 4^{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert!
we get

\left\lvert q^{\sharp}_{k}\right\rvert\leq M_{q}N^{k+2}k!(2C_{q}^{2})^{k}\leq C^{k+1}k!

where we used the fact that
\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert\leq k
in bounding
C_{q}^{k+\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}
and
Eq.

A.1
from
Lemma

A.1
.

This implies that
\underline{q^{\sharp}}
defined by

\underline{q^{\sharp}}(b)=(q^{\sharp}_{k}(b))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

(C.39)

is a formal classical analytic amplitude.

Notice at this point that for the contour deformations from
Eqs.

C.21
and
C.13
, we calculate that
J_{{a}}\Gamma^{\delta}_{1}(\hat{{a}},{b})=\mathrm{id}+i\delta\nabla_{{a}}^{2}\bar{\Phi}^{\mathrm{hol}}(\mathbf{a}({b});{b})
and
J_{{a}}\tilde{\Gamma}_{1}(\hat{{a}},{b})=\mathrm{id}
, where
\bar{\Phi}^{\mathrm{hol}}
is the holomorphic extension of the real-analytic function
\bar{\Phi}
.

Unraveling the definition of
q^{\sharp}_{0}
from
Eq.

C.38
, then the definitions of
q_{k}^{\flat},\tilde{q}_{k,\delta}
, and
\tilde{q}_{k}
from
Eq.

C.33
,
Eq.

C.22
, and
Eq.

C.14
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\det P\right\rvert(2\pi)^{-\frac{N}{2}}q^{\sharp}_{0}(b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}(1-i\sigma_{j})^{-\frac{1}{2}}q^{\flat}_{0}(0,{b})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}(1-i\sigma_{j})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\det\left(\mathrm{id}+i\delta\nabla_{{a}}^{2}\bar{\Phi}^{\mathrm{hol}}(\mathbf{a}(b);b)\right)q_{0}(\mathbf{a}(b),b)\,.

By
Eq.

C.6
,
q_{0}
is non-vanishing at
(\hat{a},\hat{b})
, so that for
A,B
and
\delta>0
small enough, we conclude that
\underline{q^{\sharp}_{v}}
, defined in
Eq.

C.39
is elliptic.

9. Equality of Finite Realizations

Let
q^{\sharp}
be any finite realization of
\underline{q^{\sharp}}
and
q
any finite realization of
\underline{q}
, which is to say that there exists large enough
R>0
and
\varepsilon>0
so that

q^{\sharp}(b;\lambda)=\sum_{k\leq\lambda R^{-1}}\lambda^{-k}q^{\sharp}_{k}(b)+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})\,,\quad\text{and}\quad q(a,b;\lambda)=\sum_{k\leq\lambda R^{-1}}\lambda^{-k}q_{k}(a,b)+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})\,.

(C.40)

We remark here that taking
K\coloneqq\lfloor\lambda R^{-1}\rfloor+1
(presuming
R>0
to be large enough), by
Eq.

C.37
and
Eq.

C.38
, using Fubini’s theorem,

\displaystyle\sum_{k\leq\lambda R^{-1}}\lambda^{-N/2-k}q^{\sharp}_{k}(b;\lambda)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k<K}\sum_{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert\leq k}\lambda^{-N/2-k+\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}\left\lvert\det P\right\rvert^{-1}(2\pi)^{\frac{N}{2}}\frac{(2\lambda)^{-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}}{\alpha!}\sum_{j=1}^{N}(1-i\sigma_{j})^{-\alpha_{j}-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\partial_{{a}}^{2\alpha}q^{\flat}_{k-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}\right)(0,{b})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k<K}\lambda^{-k}\sum_{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert<K-k}\left\lvert\det P\right\rvert^{-1}(2\pi/\lambda)^{\frac{N}{2}}\frac{(2\lambda)^{-\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert}}{\alpha!}\sum_{j=1}^{N}(1-i\sigma_{j})^{-\alpha_{j}-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\partial_{{a}}^{2\alpha}q^{\flat}_{k}\right)(0,{b})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k<K}\lambda^{-k}\left\lvert\det P\right\rvert^{-1}\int_{\left\lvert{a}\right\rvert<r_{P}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{N}(1-i\sigma_{j}){a}_{j}^{2}}q^{\flat}_{k}({a},{b})\mathrm{d}{a}-\sum_{k<K}\lambda^{-k}R_{k}^{K-k}({b},\lambda)\,.

According to
Eq.

C.2
we may bound, using the fact that
K=\lfloor R^{-1}\lambda\rfloor+1
,

\sum_{k<K}\lambda^{-k}R_{k}^{K-k}({b};\lambda)\leq K\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})=\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})\,.

Therefore, we may conclude

\sum_{k\leq\lambda R^{-1}}\lambda^{-N/2-k}q^{\sharp}_{k}(b;\lambda)=\sum_{k<K}\lambda^{-k}\left\lvert\det P\right\rvert^{-1}\int_{\left\lvert{a}\right\rvert<r_{P}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{N}(1-i\sigma_{j}){a}_{j}^{2}}q^{\flat}_{k}({a},{b})\mathrm{d}{a}+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})\,.

(C.41)

From
Eq.

C.36
we find that

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle\left\lvert\det P\right\rvert^{-1}\int_{\left\lvert{a}\right\rvert<r_{P}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{N}(1-i\sigma_{j}){a}_{j}^{2}}q^{\flat}_{k}({a},{b})\mathrm{d}{a}

(C.42)

\displaystyle=e^{-i\lambda\Phi(\mathbf{a}(b);b)}\left(\int_{A}e^{i\lambda\Phi(a;b)}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(a)q_{k}(a,b)\mathrm{d}a-R_{k,2}({b};\lambda)\right)-\left\lvert\det P\right\rvert^{-1}R_{k,3}({b};\lambda)\,,

where routine calculations (see for example the proof of
Lemma

C.3
) give
\sum_{k<K}\lambda^{-k}R_{k,3}({b};\lambda)=\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})
and
\sum_{k<K}\lambda^{-k}R_{k,2}({b};\lambda)=\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})
.

Thus, combining
Eq.

C.41
with
Eq.

C.42
, we get (recall
k<K\iff k\leq R^{-1}\lambda
)

\sum_{k\leq\lambda R^{-1}}\lambda^{-N/2-k}q^{\sharp}_{k}(b;\lambda)=e^{-i\lambda\Phi(\mathbf{a}(b);b)}\left(\int_{{A}}e^{i\lambda\Phi({a},{b})}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(a)\sum_{k\leq R^{-1}\lambda}\lambda^{-k}q_{k}({a},{b})\mathrm{d}{a}+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})\right)+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})\,,

which together with
Eq.

C.40
gives

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle e^{i\lambda\Phi(\mathbf{a}(b);b)}\left(\lambda^{-N/2}q^{\sharp}(b;\lambda)+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})\right)

(C.43)

\displaystyle=\int_{A}e^{i\lambda\Phi(a;b)}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(a)\left(q(a,b;\lambda)+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})\right)\mathrm{d}a+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})

\displaystyle=\int_{A}e^{i\lambda\Phi(a;b)}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(\eta)q(a,b;\lambda)\mathrm{d}a+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})\,,

because
\Phi^{i\mathbb{R}}(a;b)\geq 0
on
A\times B
according to
Eq.

C.4
and
\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}
is compactly supported.
Therefore, from
Eq.

C.43
we find (for a possibly different
\varepsilon>0
)

\lambda^{-\frac{N}{2}}e^{i\lambda\Phi(\mathbf{a}(b);b)}q^{\sharp}(b;\lambda)=\int_{A}e^{i\lambda\Phi(a;b)}\chi_{\tau(\lambda)}(a)q(a,b;\lambda)\mathrm{d}a+(1-e^{i\lambda\Phi(\mathbf{a}(b);b)})\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})\,.

Because
\Phi(\mathbf{a}(\hat{b});\hat{b})
has non-negative imaginary part, shrinking
B
(which leaves
\varepsilon>0
unchanged), we can guarantee that
e^{i\lambda\Phi(\mathbf{a}(b);b)}\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda})=\mathcal{O}(e^{-\varepsilon/2\lambda})
,
which concludes the proof.
∎

D
Plausibility of Assumptions

In this section we will argue that the assumptions
Eqs.

A1
,
A2
and
B1
are ‘reasonable’ and that sets satisfying assumption
Eq.

B2
are ‘not too small’ given ‘reasonable assumptions’.

As this problem is motivated by exploration seismology of the earth, we will consider the earth to be a unit ball having radial sound speed satisfying the
Herglotz condition
, see
[
PSU23
, § 2]
, introduced by
[
Her05
]
and used by
[
WZ07
]
. Since exploration seismology is interested in the upper layer of the earth it is not too far from the truth to presume that if
\mathcal{M}
is the set near the boundary, its (straight) boundary
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{M}\cap\{x_{n}=0\}
is in fact the (curved) boundary of the earth. The origin
0\in\mathcal{M}
will then be replaced by
e_{n}
, the north pole of the unit sphere.

We remark that in the preliminary reference model for the earth
[
DA81
]
, the wave speed will satisfy the Herglotz condition in the crust, in particular, near the boundary of the earth where we will be mainly interested in taking our measurements. For similar considerations see
[
IHK23
]
.

We will use results and notation from
[
PSU23
, § 2]
freely in this section and merely make the remark that our wavespeed
c
must be replaced by
c^{-1}
to match the notation in that reference. In particular, we consider the earth to be a ball of radius
1
with center being the origin. For any point
x
we let
r(x)=\left\lvert x\right\rvert_{e}
the euclidean distance of
x
to the origin.

The result of this section is the following:

Lemma D.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\subset\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\colon\left\lvert x\right\rvert\leq 1\}
be open and bounded, with
e_{n}\in\mathcal{M}
.

1.

For any
\mathcal{K}\Subset\mathcal{M}\cap\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\colon\left\lvert x\right\rvert<1\}
,
Eq.

A2
is satisfied.

2.

If
\mathcal{M}\subset\{x\colon r(x)>1-\delta\}
for some
\delta>0
small enough, then every set
\mathcal{K}\Subset\mathcal{M}\cap\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\colon\left\lvert x\right\rvert<1\}
will satisfy
Eq.

B1
.

3.

There are constants
k,K>0
so that if
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\supset\{x^{\prime}\colon\left\lvert x^{\prime}\right\rvert=1,d_{c}(x^{\prime},e_{n})<K\}
and
\mathcal{K}
satisfies
d_{c}(\mathcal{K},e_{n})<k
, the set
\mathcal{K}
satisfies
Eq.

B2
, and
Eq.

A1
is satisfied.

These results can be interpreted as

1.

There are no grazing rays.

2.

If the set
\mathcal{M}
is chosen shallow enough, rays will reach their deepeest point outside of
\mathcal{K}
, which is the unique point they have vanishing vertical momentum.

3.

If
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
contains a disc around the origin and
\mathcal{K}
lies near the origin, then a ray reflected off
\mathcal{K}
along the ‘vertical’ angle will return to the measurement surface
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and when not reflected this ray will return to the surface away from
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Note first that the existence of grazing rays would imply there are rays with
0
vertical momentum that penetrate into the earth. This is impossible for radial sound speeds, proving 1.

Let us now characterize geodesics (rays) starting at
e_{n}
. For some
p\coloneqq(p_{1},\dots,p_{n-1})
with
\left\lvert p\right\rvert^{2}\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}p_{j}^{2}<1
, consider the momentum defined by

\xi_{p}=\frac{1}{c(1)}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}p_{j}e_{j}-\sqrt{1-\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}p_{j}^{2}}e_{n}\right)\,,

and we will now investigate the geodesic defined by the initial conditions
x_{p}(0)=e_{n}
and
\xi_{p}(0)=\xi_{p}
. This is the
n
-dimensional formulation of
[
PSU23
, Eq. (2.4)]
when taking the initial point
x_{p}(0)
as
e_{n}
.

From this point onward we let
\gamma_{p}(t)=(x_{p}(t),\xi_{p}(t))
denote the geodesic defined by the initial condition
(x_{p}(0),\xi_{p}(0))
and we define
r_{p}(t)
to be
\left\lvert x_{p}(t)\right\rvert_{e}
, the (euclidean) distance of the ray
\gamma_{p}
at time
t
from the center of the earth, so that in particular
r_{p}(0)=1
.

According to
[
PSU23
, Thm. 2.1.6]
, the geodesic
\gamma_{p}
will penetrate into the earth, reach its nadir at some time
t_{p}>0
and return to the surface, and the vertical momentum of
\gamma_{p}
will vanish uniquely at this time
t_{p}
. We will first find a lower bound on the magnitude of
t_{p}
.

By assumption on the Herglotz condition, we know that

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\left(\frac{r}{c(r)}\right)\bigg|_{r=r(t)}>0\,,

so let
r_{l}\in(0,1)
be the depth of
\mathcal{M}
:

r_{l}\coloneqq\inf\left\{r\colon\exists x\in\mathcal{M}\colon\left\lvert x\right\rvert_{e}=r\right\}\,,

and let

\varepsilon_{l}\coloneqq\min_{r\in[r_{l},1]}c(r)\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\left(\frac{r}{c(r)}\right)\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\varepsilon_{u}\coloneqq\max_{r\in[r_{l},1]}c(r)\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\left(\frac{r}{c(r)}\right)\,.

The time
t_{p}
is defined as the unique solution to

x_{p}(t)\cdot\xi_{p}(t)=0\,.

We consider only rays that reach their deepest point inside of
\mathcal{M}
. By
[
PSU23
, Eq. (2.9)]
we have
\partial_{t}(x_{p}(t)\cdot\xi_{p}(t))=c(r_{p}(t))\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\left(\frac{r}{c(r)}\right)|_{r=r_{p}(t)}\leq\varepsilon_{u}
, so we know that

t_{p}\geq\frac{-x_{p}(0)\cdot\xi_{p}(0)}{\varepsilon_{u}}=\frac{c(1)^{-1}\sqrt{1-\left\lvert p\right\rvert^{2}}}{\varepsilon_{u}}\,,

where
-\sqrt{1-\left\lvert p\right\rvert^{2}}
is the vertical component of the (unit normalized) initial momentum at
x_{p}(0)=e_{n}
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be any compact subset of
\mathcal{M}
. We will successively shrink
\mathcal{K}
in such a way that it will satisfy
Eqs.

B1
and
B2
.

Since
\mathcal{K}
is a compact set and
\mathcal{M}
is simple, for each
x\in\mathcal{K}
there is a unique unit speed geodesic starting at the origin
e_{n}
that hits
x
. Each of these geodesics must have had vertical momentum
-\sqrt{1-\left\lvert p_{x}\right\rvert^{2}}
for some
p_{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
with
\left\lvert p_{x}\right\rvert^{2}<1
, so that by compactness there is some
b_{\mathcal{K}}>0
so that a geodesic starting at the origin traveling into
\mathcal{K}
must have vertical momentum
\left\lvert-\sqrt{1-p^{2}}\right\rvert\geq b_{\mathcal{K}}
. Therefore, we may conclude that all rays starting at the origin traveling into
\mathcal{K}
will reach their deepest point at some time
t_{p}
with

t_{p}\geq\frac{c(1)^{-1}b_{\mathcal{K}}}{\varepsilon_{u}}>0\,.

We now determine a lower bound on how deep rays traveling through
\mathcal{K}
must go. Let the geodesic with initial conditions
(x_{p},\xi_{p})
pass into
\mathcal{K}
.

We calculate

r_{p}(t_{p}/2)-1=r_{p}(t_{p}/2)-r_{p}(0)=\dot{r}_{p}(t)t_{p}

for some
t\in[0,t_{p}/2]
, and since
\dot{r}_{p}(t)=-c(r_{p}(t))\sqrt{1-\left(\frac{pc(r_{p}(t))}{r_{p}(t)c(1)}\right)^{2}}
for
t\leq t_{p}
(see
[
PSU23
, Eq. (2.10)]
) we find that

r_{p}(t_{p})\leq r_{p}(t_{p}/2)\leq 1-t_{p}/2\min_{0\leq t\leq t_{p}/2}c(r_{p}(t))\sqrt{1-\left(p\frac{c(r_{p}(t))}{r_{p}(t)c(1)}\right)^{2}}\eqqcolon r_{p}<1\,,

where the minimum above is non-zero because it is uniquely minimized at
t=t_{p}
. By compactness there is thus some
0<d_{\mathcal{K}}<1
so that all rays traveling from the origin into
\mathcal{K}
reach their deepest point at a depth
<d_{\mathcal{K}}
.

Thus by possibly shrinking
\mathcal{K}
so that
\mathcal{K}\subset\{x\in\mathcal{M}\colon\left\lvert x\right\rvert_{e}>d_{\mathcal{K}}\}
, we have insured that all rays from the origin traveling through
\mathcal{K}
reach their deepest point outside of
\mathcal{K}
.
Thus, we have guaranteed
Eq.

B1
, since this turning point is the unique one where the vertical momentum of the ray
\gamma_{p}
vanishes.

We will now pay special attention to the ray pointing vertically downward to prove item 3.

We prove that the ray from the origin pointing only vertically downward will continue to point downward vertically forever (until reflected off a point in
\mathcal{K}
, at which point it will point vertically upward).

The geodesic we consider is again given by the initial point
x(0)=e_{n}
but now the initial momentum
\xi(0)=-e_{n}
. For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
let
\{x,x^{\perp}_{1},\dots,x^{\perp}_{n-1}\}
be a basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and write
\hat{x}=\frac{x}{\left\lvert x\right\rvert}
and
\hat{\xi}=\frac{\xi}{\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert}
, and calculate

\hat{\xi}(t)=(\hat{\xi}(t)\cdot\hat{x}(t))\hat{x}(t)+\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(\hat{\xi}(t)\cdot\hat{x}^{\perp}_{j}(t))\hat{x}_{j}^{\perp}(t)=(\hat{\xi}(t)\cdot\hat{x}(t))\hat{x}(t)\,,

which followed because
\hat{\xi}(t)\cdot\hat{x}^{\perp}_{j}(t)=\hat{\xi}(0)\cdot\hat{x}^{\perp}_{j}(0)=0
by
[
PSU23
, Eq. (2.8)]
. Now because
\left\lvert\hat{\xi}(t)\right\rvert=\left\lvert\hat{x}(t)\right\rvert=1
we find from the above calculation that
\left\lvert\hat{\xi}(t)\cdot\hat{x}(t)\right\rvert=1
and thus conclude that
\hat{\xi}(t)=\pm\hat{x}(t)
and since at
t=0
we have
\hat{\xi}(0)=-\hat{x}(0)
we get
\hat{\xi}(t)=-\hat{x}(t)
, which is to say that
\frac{\xi(t)}{c(r(t))}=-\frac{x(t)}{r(t)}
by
[
PSU23
, Eq. (2.7)]
and the fact that
\left\lvert x\right\rvert=r
.

Because
\dot{r}(t)=-c(r(t))
by
[
PSU23
, Eq. (2.10)]
, we find

\dot{x}(t)=\xi(t)=-\frac{c(r(t))}{r(t)}x(t)=\frac{\dot{r}(t)}{r(t)}x(t)

so we calculate

\frac{x}{r}=\frac{\dot{x}}{\dot{r}}=\frac{r}{\dot{r}}\partial_{t}\left(\frac{x}{r}\right)+\frac{x}{r}\,,

from which we find
0=\frac{r}{\dot{r}}\partial_{t}\left(\frac{x}{r}\right)
which is to say that as long as
r(t)\neq 0
we have
\partial_{t}(\frac{x}{r})=0
, so that

\frac{x}{r}(t)=\frac{x(0)}{r(0)}=x(0)=e_{n}\,,

and thus

\xi(t)=\dot{x}(t)=-c(r(t))e_{n}\,,

which is to say that this geodesic will only ever point vertically downward, and since its reflection (via the formula present in
Eq.

B2
) is the ray itself traversed in the opposite direction, the reflected ray will point upward until hitting the initial point
e_{n}
.

Using a continuity argument, there is an open neighborhood of the points directly beneath the origin
e_{n}
that will reflect back to an open neighborhood of the origin at the surface which gives the truth of
Eq.

B2
in statement 3.

To argue that
Eq.

A1
holds as well we repeat the above argument as follows: rays starting at the origin with initial momentum in a neighborhood of the purely vertical momentum will reach their nadir near the origin (center of the ball) and return to the surface near the antipodal point of the inital point
e_{n}
(see the reflection statement in
[
PSU23
, Thm. 2.1.6]
), so that if
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
stays away from the antipodal point of
e_{n}
we have proven 3.
∎
